# Judges

# ANNOTATIONS ON THE BOOK OF JUDGES

## THE ARGUMENT

This Scripture is entitled The Book of Judges, because it contains an history of many memorable things done under the government of the judges, and shows the state of the people of Israel both in church and commonwealth during their times, for three hundred years saving one, from Joshua to Eli. Which sum is made up of these parcels: Othniel governed forty years, chap. 3:11. Ehud, eighty, chap. 3:30. Deborah, forty, chap. 5:31. Gideon, forty, chap. 8:28. Abimelech, three, chap. 9:22. Tola, twenty-three, chap. 10:2. Jair, twenty-two, chap. 10:3. Jephthah, six, chap. 12:7. Ibzan, seven, chap. 12:9. Elon, ten, chap. 12:11. Abdon, eight, chap. 12:14. Samson, twenty, chap. 16:31. In all, two hundred ninety and nine years. Within which space we are to comprehend the years attributed to the several oppressions mentioned in this book. See on chap. 3:11. Yet some, that they may make the apostle’s account Acts 13:19, 20 agree with the chronology of this story, will have the years of the oppressors distinct from the years of the judges: Now Cushan-rishathaim is said to have oppressed Israel eight years, chap. 3:8. Eglon, eighteen, chap. 3:14. Jabin, twenty, chap. 4:3. The Midianites, seven, chap. 6:1. The Ammonites, eighteen, chap. 10:8. The Philistims, forty, chap. 13:1. All which amount to an hundred and eleven. Which years, if several indeed from those of the judges, would make this book a story of four hundred and ten years, which having the forty years of Eli’s judicature, 1 Sam. 4:18, added to them, would just make up the sum Paul speaks of, four hundred and fifty years. But, as has been said, the times of the six oppressions must be made to run in amongst the times of the twelve judges, and some other supputation would be found out for the apostle’s account; otherwise, by unhandsome avoiding the inconvenience of that place we should meet with as great, if not greater, 1 Kings 6:1. There are, that make quick work, and think to salve up the matter, and solve the doubt, by blaming the scribe that copied out the Book of the Acts, and say he mistook, and wrote *τετρακοσίοις* for *τριακοσίοις*, four hundred, for three hundred: But this seems more haste than good speed, and rather a cutting than an untying the knot. Others understand that account, not of the times of the judges, but of the times before, from the birth of Isaac to the conquest of Canaan. Some reckon from the beginning of Moses’ government to the end of Samuel’s. See on Acts 13:20. But let us to our purpose. Concerning the judges mentioned in this book, we are to take notice, that they were not ordinary magistrates, but extraordinarily called by God in times of great extremity, when all things seemed deplorable and desperate, and endowed with heroic gifts of the Spirit fitting them for those services in which he was pleased to employ them; which was usually to fight his battles, and by vanquishing their enemies, to give his people deliverance from their grievous oppressions; as also to restore and rectify the government, when through the people’s insolencies and unruliness all things were brought to disorder and confusion. Neither had these judges supreme and regal power committed unto them (for all the while God was their King, and kept the *jura regalia* or royal rights in his own hand, unto whom as supreme they were to appeal in cases of greatest difficulty, Exod. 18:15, 19, till in Samuel’s time they rejected him and his government, 1 Sam. 8:7, and would have a king of their own to reign over them, like other nations) but they were only God’s lieutenants, to go out and in before the people at his command, to execute his designs, and at his appointment (when their work was done) to lay down their authority and rule, as we see in Gideon’s example, chap. 8:23. Now these were called judges, because they were God’s ministers appointed by him to execute his righteous judgments, both in subduing, punishing and destroying the wicked enemies of his church, and when his people were delivered from tyranny and oppressions, to administer justice unto them according to his laws, and preserve unto them God’s true religion in purity and sincerity, without any tainture of idolatry and superstition. The main scope of this history is to set forth, and leave to the use of all posterity, the state and condition of the church and commonwealth of Israel from their first settling in Canaan to the time of Eli; that all succeeding ages might learn by their example to imitate such things in them as are worthy imitation, and shun the contrary: For it discovers their prosperous success in all their enterprises, while they professed and maintained God’s true religion, and valiantly executed his commands in fighting his battles and driving out their enemies; but when out of sloth and love of ease, or cowardly fear, they neglected God’s charge, and suffered them to live amongst them, the Lord, as he had threatened, Exod. 23:33, Josh. 23:13, Num. 33:55, caused them to become snares and traps unto them, scourges to their sides, and thorns in their eyes, to vex and grieve them, as plainly appears by this history: For by conversing with these cursed nations, *they learned their works*, Psal. 106:35. *They took their daughters to be their wives, and gave their daughters to their sons*, and worshipped their gods, Baalim and Ashtaroth, chap. 3:6 and 2:13, and *served their Idols, which were a snare unto them*; yea, *they sacrificed their sons and daughters unto devils*, Psalm 106:36, 37. By which fearful apostasy they showed their horrible ingratitude unto God, who by such mighty wonders had delivered them out of the Egyptian bondage, subdued their many and mighty enemies, and performed all his gracious promises in bringing them into, and giving them possession of that good land of Canaan, which flowed with milk and honey; and also their shameful perfidiousness in breaking their solemn covenant with God, into which Joshua a little before his death had caused them to enter. By all which provocations they fearfully kindled God’s just wrath against them, as the story shows; so that he made them unsuccessful in all their enterprises, delivering them into the hands of spoilers that spoiled them, and *sold them into the hands of their enemies round about*, so that they could no longer stand before them, chap. 2:14, 15, but being subdued, were grievously oppressed by them. Yet in all this just severity the Lord was still mindful of his covenant, and did not utterly reject them, as their sins deserved, but in his great mercy and goodness did thus sharply correct them to bring them to repentance. Which was no sooner performed, but presently he raised up judges to deliver them out of the hands of their oppressing enemies. And though they often relapsed into their idolatrous and sinful courses, and were as often severely punished, yet returning unto God, who smote them, by serious repentance, they still found him alike gracious and merciful in pardoning their sins, and sending them deliverance. Lastly, by that horrible corruption of religion and manners which was at this time in the commonwealth of Israel, specially instanced in that bloody massacre of Gideon’s sons, and the sodomitical filthiness of the men of Gibeah countenanced and maintained by the whole tribe of Benjamin, it declared unto all posterity the miserable condition of such a state and people as live in an anarchy, or (which is almost as bad) under the government of wicked magistrates, who either out of fear dare not execute justice upon offenders, being overawed with the insolencies of a stiff-necked and rebellious people, or out of favor will not, because they like their courses, as being suitable to their own dispositions; or through negligence, or love of their own ease, will not trouble themselves to examine and take notice of any wickedness, that it may be punished, but, as if there were no king in Israel, suffer every one to do that which seems good in his own eyes.

## CHAPTER 1

**Verse 1.*** {Now after the death of Joshua}* i.e. Not many years after. For the first judge after his departure was Othniel, who married Caleb’s daughter, and lived in Joshua’s time.

*{the children of Israel}* Being deprived of an head and commander in chief, who might direct and lead them in all things concerning the war which they now intended.

*{asked the Lord}* This they did, I. Because the business in hand was of great weight and importance, seeing if they miscarried in their first attempts, it would be a great discouragement to themselves, and heartening to their enemies, who would be ready to think that all their good success in their former wars, had proceeded from the wisdom and valor of Joshua their general, and that he being dead, all their hopes died with him. II. Because they would hereby acknowledge God to be their sovereign Lord and King, who retained in his own hand all the regal rights and royalties, and therefore had the whole power and privilege to manage the war, and to appoint whom he pleased to be his lieutenants and deputies. III. Because they knew, he being infinite in wisdom was best able to direct them, and that there was little hope of prospering in their undertakings, unless he did guide and assist them. IV. They did it to prevent divisions and emulations amongst the tribes, which might have happened about precedence and preferment of one before another, if the Lord himself had not appointed and determined it. Therefore they would conclude nothing in this matter, but asked the Lord, namely by the mediation of the high priest, consulting with his oracle by Urim and Thummim, according to the law, Num. 27:21, Exod. 28:30. So chap. 20:18, 1 Sam. 23:9.

*{Who shall go up for us}* The people being now satiate with rest, and having enjoyed their fill of ease and pleasure, and beginning to find themselves somewhat straitened in their inheritances, because the Canaanites did still hold a great part of them in their possession, begin to think and resolve of renewing the war, and therefore do not here inquire whether or no they should go out to fight against their enemies, (for this God often had expressly commanded, and Joshua a little before his death had persuaded them to) but which of the tribes should first begin and be a precedent to the rest.

*{against the Canaanites}* i.e. Not those only who were properly and particularly so called, but all the cursed nations. For Judah and Simeon do by virtue of their commission attempt first the subduing of the Jebusites and Perizzites, and not the Canaanites that lived on the seacoast.

**V. 2.*** {And the Lord said, Judah shall go up}* i.e. Not as a captain over the other tribes, (for this was not the people’s inquiry) but shall begin this war against these nations, and so be a precedent unto all the rest. By going up, is either meant the preparing themselves to set upon the work, or because they were to march into an high and mountainous country. Now Judah was appointed to be first in this expedition. I. Because it was the most numerous, valiant, able and active of all the tribes; and God usually chooses the fittest means to serve his providence: yet sometimes he does otherwise, that by the weakness of the instruments he uses he may have the more glory, and keep men from boasting. II. Because he would honor this tribe above the rest, out of which the Messiah was to come, who should be King, and by his scepter rule all nations, Jews and Gentiles, according to Jacob’s prophecy, Gen. 49:8. And therefore here, as also in the war against the Benjaminites, chap. 20:18, and also when they pitched their camp in the wilderness, when they offered, when they marched, Judah still had the first and chief place, Num. 2:3 and 7:12 and 10:14.

**V. 3.*** {And Judah said unto Simeon his brother}* They were brethren not only in a general relation, as all the other tribes, but in a more special manner, because descended both of the same father and mother, and also because Simeon’s inheritance was taken out of Judah’s, Josh. 19:1, and so being intermixed one with another they were tied by this near bond of neighborhood to join together in their defensive and offensive wars.

*{Come up with me into my lot}* Before the land was divided, the tribes were bound to fight all together against the common enemy: But when every tribe had their own inheritance allotted unto them, they were to be at that care and charge that was requisite for expelling those enemies which lived amongst them, and held part of that which belonged unto them in their possession. And for the better enabling of them to perform it, they might call another tribe unto their assistance. Yea, but they had an express promise from God, that if they would attempt it they should have good success, and none of their enemies should be able to stand against them; what need then was there for Judah to call in Simeon to his aid? I answer, they might lawfully do it; because God’s promises of the issue and success do not hinder from using all lawful means for fulfilling of them; yea rather we are bound to use them as serving his providence to effect and bring them to pass.

**V. 4.*** {and they slew of them in Bezek}* i.e. In the city so called, and the territories belonging to it. This was part of Judah’s inheritance, and near unto Jerusalem. Here Saul mustered his army when he went to relieve Jabesh-Gilead against the Ammonites, 1 Sam. 11:8.

**V. 5.*** {And they found Adoni-Bezek}* Who was lord and king of Bezek, as his name signifies. See on Josh. 10:1.

**V. 6.*** {But Adoni-Bezek}* i.e. When he saw his army discomfited and ten thousand of them slain on the place, and the remainder flying to save their lives, he likewise fled with them.

*{and cut off his thumbs and his great toes}* In their ordinary way of execution they should have slain him outright, and not have reserved him alive thus mangled; yet, though they had, as it seems, no intention to let him live, (for they carried him away thus wounded to Jerusalem, and neglecting his cure he there died) they thus tortured him, contrary to their usual manner of dealing with their vanquished enemies, which was not like barbarians delighting in cruelty, to torture them to death, but to make a quick dispatch, by hanging, or slaying them with the sword: But it seems that either they had heard of the like cruelty, he exercised upon others, or were eyewitnesses of it, when sacking Bezek they might find some of those miserable kings thus used, and their hearts yearning at the sight of so horrid a spectacle, they might think themselves justly bound by the law of retaliation to requite like for like, Exod. 21:24. Or if none of these, then this came to pass by a special providence of God, secretly inclining their hearts to inflict upon him these unusual, but yet deserved, punishments. See on 2 Samuel 12:31.

**V. 7.*** {Threescore and ten Kings}* It is not at all improbable that this one king, being a great soldier, more mighty and powerful than all his neighbors, tyrannical, active and violent in his attempts should vanquish so great a number of kings. For in those times and countries (as now in the West Indies) almost every city had its king to govern it. So it is said that Joshua vanquished one and thirty kings in that little country of Canaan, besides all those who were not subdued by him. Yet it is not likely that Adoni-Bezek had seventy kings at once in this subjection and base slavery, but throughout the whole course of his conquests, some successively after others.

*{having their thumbs and their great toes cut off}* This cruelty he exercised upon them, not, as some think, that being made unserviceable for the wars they might not again rise and rebel against him: For this there might be some color, if he had set them at liberty to live in their own countries; but none at all, seeing they were kept still under his power to kill them at his own pleasure. He thus used them rather either out of an innate and more than barbarous cruelty, sporting himself in the calamities and miseries of others; or out of pride and vainglory, using them as trophies of his victories; or by such examples to terrify all other kings from making any resistance when he invaded their dominions. The like examples we find recorded in our latter stories: as of Sapores king of Persia, who having taken Valerian the emperor prisoner, used him as his footstool or stirrup to mount up on horseback; and of Tamberlaine the Scythian king, who having taken Bajazeth in battle, carried him about in an iron cage as a trophy of his victory, and used other kings to draw his chariot.

*{And they brought him to Jerusalem}* That he might be a public spectacle of God’s heavy and just vengeance upon barbarous and bloody tyrants to deter others from following his example.

**V. 8.*** {Now the children of Judah had fought against Jerusalem, and had taken it}* Here he gives a reason why they brought Adoni-Bezek to Jerusalem, because it was now in Judah’s possession. For Joshua and his army had fought against it, and taken it, as some gather from Josh. 10:42 and 15:63, that is, the southern part of the city, called Jebus but not the northern, called Salem, Josh. 15:8, 63 and 18:11, 28, and here ver. 21. For Jerusalem was a double city, besides the fort or castle, (and therefore its name is of the dual number) of which one part, namely the southern, was in Judah’s lot, and the other, to wit, the northern, with the castle, was in Benjamin’s. See on chap. 19:1, 10.

*{and set the City on fire}* Heb. *Cast the City into the fire*. *Hypallage*, for, *they cast fire into the City*. So Psal. 74:7. *They have sent thy Sanctuary into the fire*, for *they have cast fire into thy Sanctuary*. By this phrase of casting fire into the city is implied, that they burned not all, but only some part, and left the rest for their habitation. Now upon this occasion of speaking what was done in Joshua’s time for the winning of this city, he makes a digression from the former story in hand, and repeats divers other passages respecting the tribe of Judah, which are translated out of Josh. 15:13–19, with explanation of some particulars. The which digression is continued unto v. 16.

**V. 9.*** {went} [{went down to fight against the Canaanites that dwelt in the mountain}]* i.e. They descended from Jerusalem, situate on a mountain, into the valleys and champaign country, and then ascended to fight with the Canaanites which dwelt upon other mountains. For in that mountainous country, they must needs descend from one mount, before they could ascend up to another.

**V. 10.*** {And Judah went against the Canaanites that dwelt in Hebron}* To wit, under the conduct of Joshua and Caleb, Josh. 10:36 and 15:13, 14. For the things here related to v. 16 were done in Joshua’s time, but here in some passages expressed more fully and particularly.

**V. 11.*** {And from thence he went}* See on Josh. 15:15–19.

**V. 16.*** {the Kenite Moses’ father in law}* Moses’ father-in-law in the Scripture is called by divers names, Jethro, Hobab, Reuel, and here Kenite, which seems to be a patronymic, or his surname; or rather the name of his nation, Numb. 24:21.

*{out of the City of Palm trees}* i.e. Jericho, Deut. 34:3, so called from the plenty of palm trees that grew in that fruitful territory belonging to it. Here the posterity of Jethro first seated themselves. For howsoever Jethro himself would not be persuaded to accompany Moses unto Canaan in respect of his great age, and the difficulty of the action of conquering the land, but went back into his own country, and there died, Ex. 18:27, yet before his death it is probable that he gave in charge to his kindred and posterity, that they should follow the Israelites thither, and there plant themselves, that they might enjoy the benefit of the promise made unto him by Moses; Numb. 10:32. *If thou go with us, it shall be, that what goodness the Lord shall do unto us, the same will we do unto thee*. Or it may be when God had commanded Moses to destroy the Midianites, that he sent first for Jethro his posterity and kindred to come away to him from them, that they might not be involved in the common destruction; as Saul did in like case, 1 Sam. 15:6. By city are meant the territories, hamlets and villages belonging to the city: for Jericho itself was burnt, and not reedified, and therefore no fit place for their habitation.

*{with the children of Judah}* Unto which tribe the Kenites chiefly adhered, and being at liberty to choose the place of their habitation, Num. 10:32, and dwelling always in tents, which were easy to remove from place to place, they removed with them into the wilderness of Judah, or that mountainous country adjoining unto it. From these Kenites the Rechabites descended, Jer. 35:2.

*{which lieth in the South of Arad}* A certain place near to the land of Canaan and to the country of the Amalekites, whose king and people joining with the Amalekites, made war against Israel, Exod. 17:8, Num. 21:1, 2. Here the Kenites dwelt in the time of Saul, till he being appointed by God to destroy Amalek, gave them warning to prevent their destruction by departing from among them, 1 Sam. 15:6.

*{among the people}* i.e. With them of the tribe of Judah.

**V. 17.*** {And Judah went with Simeon his brother}* i.e. To assist him for the recovering of his inheritance by driving out the Canaanites, as Simeon in the like kind had formerly assisted him, v. 3.

*{slew the Canaanites that inhabited Zephath}* This city was in the dominion of king Arad, and was now anathematized and devoted to utter destruction, and therefore called Hormah; either because the Israelites had bound themselves by some present vow to destroy it utterly, if the Lord would deliver it into their hands; or rather, because they had vowed to do so, if the Lord would be pleased to deliver the king of Arad and his army into their hands; which accordingly they then did, Num. 21:1, 3, and now it being reedified, they do it again the second time.

**V. 18.*** {And Judah took Gaza, &c.}* These cities fell to the lot of Judah, Josh. 15:45, 47, and were in the land of the Philistims bordering on the Mediterranean Sea, and were afterwards recovered from Israel for their sins, ch. 3:3 and 14:19 and 16:1, 2 and 1 Sam. 6:17. For the Israelites and the Philistims having continual wars one with another, accordingly as either party prevailed, these cities were in the possession of the stronger side. Gaza had its name, not from the Persian word *Gaza*, as some would have it, signifying treasure, because Cambyses there laid his treasures when he intended a war on Greece; but from the Hebrew *Azza*, the first letter *Ajin*, as the Greeks are wont, being turned into *Gamma*. This city was so strong in Alexander’s time, that it cost him two months’ siege to take it.

**V. 19.*** {And the Lord was with Judah}* i.e. To prosper and assist them in all their undertakings in a special manner. So Gen. 39:21, 1 Sam. 18:14. Whereby as it is implied, that they had all their good success from God, so they are secretly taxed for their fear and cowardice, seeing they dared not attempt the driving out of the Canaanites from the valleys, because of their iron chariots, they having had so great experience of God’s gracious assistance.

*{because they had chariots of iron}* See Joshua 17:16. This was no just cause to deter them from following their conquest; for they had God’s promise of victory, notwithstanding those iron chariots, Josh. 17:18. It was only their want of valor, their sinful fear, and want of faith in God’s promises that hindered them, as also the neglect of God’s commands, who enjoined them to drive them out, Josh. 23:6.

**V. 20.*** {And they gave Hebron unto Caleb}* See the story of it, Josh. 14:6, &c. and the annot. on that place.

**V. 21.*** {And the Children of Benjamin}* See on Josh. 15:63.

*{dwell with the children of Benjamin in Jerusalem}* i.e. In their part of the city, and the castle or fort. For these remained unconquered till David’s time, 2 Sam. 5:6, 7.

**V. 22.*** {the house of Joseph}* i.e. The tribes of Ephraim and Manasseh.

*{went up against Bethel}* i.e. To fight against and take it, because it belonged to their lot, see on Josh. 16:2.

**V. 23.*** {was Luz}* i.e. A filbert nut; so called (as some think) because it resembled a nut in its figure. See Gen. 28:19.

**V. 24.*** {the entrance into the City}* Not the gate (which might easily be found without a guide, but it may be was now shut, bolted and blocked up, the enemy approaching near it) but the weakest part of the city, where it might most easily be assaulted, scaled and taken.

*{and we will shew thee mercy}* Namely, in saving your life, with theirs that belonged unto you, and also rewarding you for this service. For though the law commanded generally to put all the Canaanites to death yet the rigor of it might upon occasion be moderated by common equity and the law of nature, which requires we should do well to them that have dealt well with us, as we see in the example of Rahab. See on Josh. 6:25.

**V. 25.*** {But they let go the man, &c.}* i.e. Set them at liberty to dispose of themselves as they thought good.

**V. 26.*** {and the man went into the land of the Hittites}* i.e. Not those that dwelt in the land of Canaan, who were to be destroyed soon after, but others of that name further off and free from that danger. Others think that here are meant the Hittites of Canaan, who inhabited on the north side of mount Ephraim, where the Israelites permitted him to build and live for doing them this service.

*{and built a City}* Being potent and rich, either of himself, or by the reward given him for his service, and accompanied with his family, friends and allies, who were spared for his sake, he built a city for his and their habitation.

*{and called the name of it Luz}* Out of his love to his native country where he was born and bred, he called the city he built by the same name, that the memory of it might not perish, but by this means be preserved unto posterity; in which he had his desire, as appears by the words following.

**V. 27.*** {Neither did Manasseh}* Namely, that part of the tribe that dwelt in Canaan.

*{drive out}* Having disabled themselves by their sins, which had deprived them of God’s assistance. See Josh. 17:11, 12.

*{Beth-shean}* Called Beth-shan, 1 Sam. 31:13.

*{Taanach}* A royal city near mount Gilboa, Josh. 17:11, whose king Joshua slew, Josh. 12:21.

*{Dor}* A royal city with large territories, whose king was slain, Josh. 11:2 and 12:23.

*{Ibleam}* Both these places lay near the midland sea, and in the tribe of Manasseh, Josh. 17:11.

*{Megiddo}* A great and royal city, Josh. 12:21, in the lot of Manasseh, Josh. 17:11, in whose territories the good king Josiah was slain, 2 Kings 23:29.

*{would dwell in that land}* Either by force or composition.

**V. 28.*** {they put the Canaanites to tribute}* Either out of cowardice, because they dared not, or out of sloth and covetousness, because they would not drive them out, they made peace with them upon this only condition, that they should pay them tribute; and that when they were become strong, and so able to have driven them out if they had had an heart to do it: Which did much aggravate their sin of neglecting God’s express command, Exodus 23:32, Deut. 7:2.

**V. 29.*** {Gezer}* A city within the lot of Ephraim, Josh. 16:10, given to the Levites, Josh. 21:21, on the river Gaas near unto the midland sea, four miles from Nicopolis, i.e. Emmaus.

*{dwelt in Gezer among them}* i.e. Not intermingled one with another in the same city, but in the same province or territories. For the Canaanites alone possessed the city till Pharaoh killed them and burned it, 1 Kings 9:16.

**V. 33.*** {Beth-shemesh}* See on Josh. 15:10.

*{he dwelt among the Canaanites}* Not in the same cities or towns, but in the same province, wherein their cities were intermingled one with another.

*{the inhabitants of the land}* i.e. The ancient inhabitants of the land, and who now also held a great part thereof in present possession.

**V. 34.*** {forced the children of Dan into the mountain}* i.e. Though the children of Dan had at the first prevailed against the Amorites, and possessed themselves of their country, yet afterwards the Amorites recruiting their army, and getting more strength, recovered the best part of the country, namely the valleys, and forced the Danites into the mountains; where being much straitened, they took occasion to enlarge their possessions by making that expedition unto Laish, related ch. 18.

**V. 35.*** {But the Amorites would dwell in mount Heres}* i.e. They were not content to possess themselves of the fruitful valleys, but also took into their possession mount Heres in Aijalon, as being better (it seems) than the mountains which they left to the Danites, and so the more straitened them in their habitations.

*{yet the hand of the house of Joseph prevailed}* i.e. Assisting the Danites with their forces they thus far prevailed, that they became tributaries to the Danites, though they could not recover their possessions from them nor drive them out of their country.

**V. 36.*** {Akrabbim}* A mountainous place called, The ascent of scorpions, lying southerly on the salt sea, and easterly on mount Seir, and the uttermost borders of Canaan towards the south. But it seems to me that he speaks not here of that Akrabbim in the lot of Judah, Josh. 15:3, but of another in which the Amorites dwelt, near unto the tribes of Ephraim and Manasseh.

## CHAPTER 2

**Verse 1.*** {And an angel of the Lord}* Some understand a messenger sent from God, who was either prophet, or priest, or both. And indeed prophets in the Scriptures sometimes are called angels, Hag. 1:13, and so are likewise God’s priests and ministers, Mal. 2:7, 1 Cor. 11:10. And they think this angel was such an one, and not an angel sent from heaven, seeing it is said he came from Gilgal to Bochim, whereas if he had been sent from heaven, it would have been so expressed. Some particularly pitch upon Phinehas, and say he is here understood. Others think it was a created angel, who taking upon him a human shape had motions from place to place attributed unto him. But for my own part, I do not approve of either of these opinions, but rather think he was the angel of the covenant, even the Lord Jesus Christ, I. Because he spoke not, as an angel, or a prophet, in the name of the Lord, but in the person of the Lord himself, appropriating the works of the Lord, as done by him, namely the people’s deliverance from Egypt, and bringing them into Canaan, and so keeping his covenant with them. II. Because it is most probable, this was the same angel that appeared to Joshua at Gilgal, Josh. 5:14, (and therefore it is here said to have come up from Gilgal) and to Gideon, ch. 6:11, and to Manoah’s wife, ch. 13:3. But that was Christ the angel of the covenant, as shall appear hereafter in due place. III. Because he thus appearing, it is said, v. 5, they sacrificed there, that is, in Bochim, unto the Lord; as also Gideon and Manoah did in the like case. Now it was not lawful to offer any sacrifices saving in the tabernacle, and upon that one altar there, or at the apparition of this angel of the covenant, or only peace offerings and gratulatory oblations, but not burnt offerings, unless upon great and extraordinary occasions they had a special dispensation from God.

*{came up from Gilgal to Bochim}* This though here related, yet was done, as likewise all the rest of this story, to v. 11, in the time of Joshua and the elders that survived him, but is here inserted to make way for the following history concerning the people’s sins, apostasy and idolatry, God’s severe punishments in delivering them into the hands of their malicious and tyrannous enemies and his gracious deliverance of them upon their repentance. And this appears, because the sins for which the angel reproved them, were such as they had committed in Joshua’s time, namely leagues and familiar friendship with the cursed nations, and not demolishing their altars as God had commanded, though their good general Joshua had earnestly persuaded them unto it, and used all the endeavors, backed with the authority of his place, to see it duly executed. But not such as they fell into immediately after the death of him and the elders, namely apostasy and idolatry, which the angel surely would have reproved them for, had they now been guilty, as they were presently afterwards in the time of the judges. Secondly, because Gilgal is here mentioned, where he first appeared to Joshua, Josh. 5:13, and which was formerly the place of their standing camp, from which it seems they were not yet all come away, although the land was now divided by lot, seeing after this meeting they went every man to his inheritance, v. 6. And lastly because it is expressly said that Joshua dismissed the people from this assembly, that the people served the Lord all his days, and all the days of the elders also that survived him, and then presently after the death of Joshua and the elders they forsook the Lord, and committed idolatry with Baalim and Ashtaroth, verse 11, 12, 13.

*{I will never break my covenant with you}* To wit, if you will not break yours with me.

**V. 2.*** {And you shall make no league}* Exod. 23:33 and 34:15, Deut. 7:2 and 12:3.

*{why have ye done this?}* q.d. I will not condemn you till I hear what you can say in your own defense: Therefore stand forth and produce what reason you can to justify these your doings. But I am sure you have not so much as a color of any cause for it, being convinced and condemned in your own consciences of shameful breach of covenant with your God.

**V. 3.*** {as thorns in your sides}* Or, *as scourges*. See on Josh. 23:13 and Num. 33:55.

*{their gods shall be a snare unto you}* To entangle you in their idolatry to your perdition. See Exod. 23:33 and 34:12, Josh. 23:13.

**V. 4.*** {the people lift up their voice, and wept}* This speech of the angel convincing them of their sin, and threatening them with deserved punishment, did work so effectually upon them that it brought them to repentance; which they expressed in their prayers to God, and sorrow for their sins, partly arising out of true contrition and compunction of heart, because they had offended their good God, and partly out of fear of having those judgments here threatened inflicted upon them.

**V. 5.*** {And they called the name of the place Bochim}* i.e. Weepers; so called by anticipation, v. 1, and here from the event, because the Israelites did weep abundantly in this place.

*{and they sacrificed there unto the Lord}* In the place where the angel appeared, as Gideon and Manoah did: or, as some others would have it, in Shiloh, near unto this place, where the tabernacle at that time was.

**V. 6.*** {And when Joshua had let the people go}* i.e. As they were assembled by his authority, being the supreme magistrate, so they did not depart to their own places before he had dismissed them.

**V. 7.*** {And the people served the Lord all the days of Joshua}* From hence to v. 10 is repeated out of Josh. 24:28, &c. and here inserted to clear the way to the ensuing story. The sum whereof is the defection of the people from God to idolatry, whereby they provoked him in his just displeasure to afflict them grievously by giving them over into the hands of their malicious enemies; and then God’s grace and favor towards them upon their repentance, in sending judges to deliver them out of their grievous servitude. These things are summarily and in a general manner set down from this place to ch. 3:7, and afterwards amplified in many particulars to the end of the book. See on Josh. 24:28, 31.

**V. 9.*** {in Timnath-Heres}* Called Timnath-serah, Josh. 24:30.

**V. 10.*** {And also all that generation}* i.e. All the Israelites of Joshua’s age, which were under twenty years old when they came out of Egypt, and had seen God’s great works in delivering them out of bondage, in carrying them through the Red Sea, and the wilderness, and over Jordan, in granting them victories over their enemies, and giving the land of Canaan into their possession.

*{were gathered unto their fathers}* i.e. Were dead as all their ancestors were.

*{which knew not the Lord}* To wit, by his miraculous works, of which they had not been eyewitnesses, as their fathers were, nor had any holy impression of them in their hearts by their relation, nor had any effectual and experimental knowledge of God in himself, his goodness, greatness and power, by all that they had heard or seen, so as it worked their hearts to faith, love and obedience.

**V. 11.*** {And the children of Israel did evil}* i.e. In forsaking the true God, and adhering unto idols.

*{and served Baalim}* Baal signifies *a lord, husband, patron, protector*. In the plural number (as in this place) it was generally used by idolaters for all their gods; which being many, they were wont to distinguish them one from another by some addition or surnames; as Baal-berith, ch. 8:33. Baal-peor, Num. 25:3. Baal-zebub, 2 Kings 1:6. The original of which name and idol, Baal, or Bel, was taken from an image of Belus or Bel, king of Babylon, which Ninus his son and successor after his death caused to be worshiped. See on Isa. 46:1 and Jer. 2:23. Under this title the heathens worshiped the planets, to every of which astrologers attribute a certain proper lordship or rule in his own house in heaven.

**V. 12.*** {And they forsook the Lord}* Either because he reputes himself forsaken when joined with idols, seeing he will be worshiped alone or not at all; or because they were so besotted in their idolatry that they did indeed quite forsake him to follow their idols; herein worse than those Samaritans, who feared the Lord and served also their idols, 2 Kings 17:33. But the former is more probable, seeing the Israelites excused their idolatry under this pretense, that they worshiped the true God in idols, Exod. 32:4, Hos. 2:16.

*{which brought them out of the land of Egypt}* Under that main benefit all the rest are comprehended: and their apostasy and idolatry were exceedingly aggravated by their grand ingratitude for so many and great benefits.

*{and followed other gods}* Or, *strange gods*; i.e. Not the God of their forefathers, but the gods of the Canaanites, in whose land they dwelt, yea the gods also of other nations that dwelt in the countries round about them, as the Syrians, Chaldeans, and others which were altogether unknown unto their ancestors.

*{and provoked the Lord to anger}* This is spoken in respect not of the affection itself (for God is free from all passion) but of the effect. They provoked the Lord to punish; which is the effect of a man that is angry.

**V. 13.*** {Ashtaroth}* The heathens called this idol Astarte. And it was the common name of all their female deities, as Baal of the males, and is therefore also here expressed in the plural number. But more specially this was the idol of the Sidonians, 1 Kings 11:5, 33, 2 Kings 23:13, and of the Philistims, 1 Sam. 31:10, which they worshiped in the form of a sheep. And under this some think they worshiped Venus; others Diana, or the moon. Augustine says the Carthaginians, who came out of Phoenicia into Africa, and whose language held much affinity with the Hebrew, called Juno their tutelar goddess by this name.

**V. 14.*** {he sold them into the hands of their enemies}* i.e. He gave them over, as the seller the thing sold into the hands of the buyer. So ch. 4:2, 9, Psal. 44:12, 13, Isa. 50:1. Or, as they usually sell captives taken in war. And this he did, either by casting them out of protection, Psal. 91:1, or by giving their enemies occasion to rise against and afflict them, ch. 14:4, or taking away their courage and strength, Lev. 26:17. This their misery was much aggravated, in that they were thus sold and given up, not to strangers that came in as conquerors for glory or spoil, who out of common humanity might have showed some mercy, but unto those enemies, who being incensed against them by the former wars, breathed nothing but malice and revenge.

**V. 15.*** {the hand of the Lord was against them for evil}* i.e. They had no possible means to escape, seeing God himself opposed them in all their undertakings, and did cross them in all their enterprises at home and abroad, and did bring upon them all mischiefs threatened, Lev. 26 and Deut. 28.

**V. 16.*** {Nevertheless the Lord raised up judges}* Pitying his people for his own sake, he brought them by afflictions to repentance, and then raised up judges to deliver them out of the hands of their enemies, and that not in an ordinary way, but by the singular motion of his Spirit; nor for a settled course of government, but for special uses upon extraordinary occasions, God first fitting them for this service, and then calling them thereunto. And this he did by giving them gifts and graces necessary for that calling, as wisdom, magnanimity, courage, fortitude of mind, and strength of body; and then either endowing them with an heroic spirit, he called them immediately to set upon the work, as he did Gideon, Shamgar, and Samson; or else made them so eminent by his gifts and endowments above others, that the people taking notice of them, called them to be their captains and leaders, as we see in the example of Jephthah. See ch. 3:9, 10 and 8:23 and 11:5. And these judges were not continued by succession, so as that as soon as one was dead another was raised up to succeed him in his office, but were raised up upon extraordinary occasions, as the state of the people did require in respect of their miseries and oppressions. See on ch. 9:1.

**V. 17.*** {And yet they would not hearken to their judges}* Though they had by them deliverance out of all their miseries, yet they would no longer retain the purity of God’s true religion than they were under their government, and restrained from idolatry by their authority; but as soon as they were by their death let loose to their own liberty, they relapsed into their old courses, and became much worse than they were before.

*{but they went a whoring after other gods}* Though they were in covenant with God, and united unto him as it were by a conjugal bond, yet they broke this bond of wedlock, and like adulteresses defiled themselves with idolatry, which is spiritual adultery.

*{which their fathers walked in}* i.e. Who lived in the days of Joshua, Josh. 24:31.

**V. 18.*** {it repented the Lord}* i.e. Upon their altering their course, and returning unto God by repentance, he also altered his course, from executing judgment to showing mercy. See Gen. 6:6. And this was the continual course which they held with God, and God with them, as appears throughout this whole book.

**V. 19.*** {When the judge was dead, they returned and corrupted themselves more than their fathers}* i.e. Though their fathers, who lived after the death of Joshua and the elders had gone out of the way of their predecessors, and corrupted themselves by their apostasy and idolatry; yet these who were delivered by the judges, and had made some show of repentance and reformation, when the judges were dead, revolted, and became more abominable idolaters than they were before, or their fathers before them.

*{stubborn way}* In which they stubbornly persisted.

**V. 21.*** {I also will not henceforth drive out any}* i.e. Although they attempt to drive out the nations which Joshua left unconquered, it shall be all in vain, seeing I will not give them courage and strength to do it, but they shall remain still to vex them, as their abominable sins have justly deserved; which (as all other God’s threatenings) is to be understood upon condition of their continuing in their sins without repentance, Jer. 18:7, 8. Or though they did repent, yet God was so much displeased with their idolatry, that he would not cast out this people, and so ease the Israelites of this temporal judgment, so soon as otherwise he would have done, if they had continued constant in his true religion.

**V. 22.*** {That through them I may prove Israel}* i.e. Not that myself may better know them, who with one simple act of knowledge know all things past, present, and to come; but that I may make them better known to themselves and to all men, and thereby justify my righteous judgments, when I punish them according to their deserts and my former threatenings, Josh. 23:13. So Gen. 22:1, 2 Chron. 32:31. Or else these words may be referred to the end of the former verse, *Which Joshua left when he died*, i.e. The nations were left unsubdued by Joshua, that the Israelites might hereby be tried, whether they would continue in God’s pure worship, or fall into idolatry, according to the example of those cursed nations that lived among them.

*{as their fathers did keep it}* i.e. Those who lived in the days of Joshua and the elders that survived him.

**V. 23.*** {Therefore the Lord left those nations}* To wit, those mentioned ch. 3:1, &c.

*{without driving them out hastily}* i.e. Not subduing them to the Israelites presently, though he did it afterwards, in the time of David and Solomon, 1 Chron. 18 and 2 Chron. 8:7.

*{neither delivered he them into the hand of Joshua}* Not because, though he attempted it, he was unable to subdue them; for he prevailed in whatsoever he undertook, and was victorious over all those against whom he fought; but because the Lord did purposely restrain him from going on in his conquests, that by the remainder of those nations he might prove the Israelites, as v. 22.

## CHAPTER 3

**V. 1.*** {Now these are the nations}* Ch. 2:21 it is said, that the Lord would not cast out before Israel some of the nations that remained. And the ends of his so doing were, to punish Israel for their backsliding and idolatry, and when thereupon they returned unto the Lord, to make further trial of them, whether they would continue in faith and obedience, and still cleave close unto God in the profession and practice of his pure religion. And here is set down who those remaining nations were, and withal another cause or end added to the former, why God suffered them still to continue in the land, namely, to exercise his people in martial discipline.

*{as had not known all the wars of Canaan}* To wit in the time of Joshua; and that to this end, that God might keep the people in the exercise of faith and obedience, seeing they still needed his assistance against these enemies.

**V. 2.*** {might know to teach them war}* That by this continual exercise of war, they might be able to teach their posterity the use of arms and art of war, that so they might not grow effeminate by pleasure, sloth and ease, nor weakhearted with peace and rest and thereby be disabled to resist their enemies in time to come.

**V. 3.*** {Namely, the five lords of the Philistines}* Three of the countries over which these lords ruled, to wit, Gaza, Askelon, and Ekron, were formerly subdued in that expedition of Judah and Simeon, ch. 1:18, but were recovered again by the Philistims, for the sins of God’s people, with whom they had continual war, sometime the one party, and sometimes the other prevailing: Neither were these enemies wholly vanquished till the days of David and Solomon, ch. 15:11, 1 Sam. 4:3 and 6:17.

*{and all the Canaanites}* This is not simply to be understood of all, taken largely, but strictly of those Canaanites properly so called, or rather of those alone whose countries and habitations are here expressed.

*{and the Sidonians}* i.e. Those countries which belonged to the Sidonians: For the great city Sidon itself was never in the possession of the Israelites. But that a good part of the country adjoining thereunto was conquered by the Israelites under the conduct of Joshua, and that many Phoenicians the inhabitants thereof were, according to God’s promise, Josh. 13:6, driven out of their possessions, and forced to plant themselves on the seacoasts of Africa, appears by the two pillars which Procopius says were to be seen in that part of Mauritania that is called Tingitana, whereon there was an inscription in the ancient Phoenician language to this sense, We are those that fled from that robber Joshua the son of Nave. Procop. *in Vandalicis*, lib. 2.

*{Lebanon}* A great mountain on the north of Canaan, abounding with frankincense, from which it has its name both in Greek and Hebrew.

*{from mount Baal-hermon}* So called from the idol Baal there worshiped. It lay on the east of Lebanon, and northward from Bashan, 1 Chron. 5:23, and mount Hermon on the west end of Lebanon, Deut. 3:8 and 9:48. Some think Baal-hermon to be that mountain which by the Greeks is called Antilibanus.

*{unto the entering in of Hamath}* A city on the north of Canaan, Numb. 34:8, afterwards called Antiochia. See on Isa. 10:9.

**V. 4.*** {to prove Israel}* See ch. 2:22.

*{to know}* i.e. To make known. So Deut. 8:2.

**V. 5.*** {And the children of Israel dwelt}* Here, and v. 6, 7, are expressed the sins for which the Israelites were given into the hands of their enemies; and first, their familiar conversation and acquaintance with these cursed nations.

**V. 6.*** {And they took their daughters}* This was the second sin, making interchangeable marriages with them, contrary to God’s express command, Exod. 34:16, Deut. 7:3. And this was attended with many other sins.

**V. 7.*** {and forgat the Lord their God, and served Baalim}* Contrary to that duty which they owed to their supreme Lord, to the many obligements whereby they were bound to God’s service above all other nations, and to that covenant they had made with him in mount Sinai, and lately renewed and ratified in the days of Joshua.

*{and the groves}* i.e. The idols which they had set up and worshiped in thick and dark groves, under the goodliest shady trees, which they had consecrated to the honor of their false gods, contrary to Ex. 34:13, Deut. 7:5.

**V. 8.*** {Therefore the anger of the Lord}* i.e. Like a man that is angry being much abused and wronged, he resolved to punish them for their sins. See on ch. 2:12.

*{and he sold them}* See on ch. 2:14.

*{Chushan-Rishathaim}* This is a compounded name, and the latter, as some think, a surname to the other. See Hab. 3:7. This was the Israelites’ first servitude after their coming out of the land of Egypt.

*{Mesopotamia}* Heb. *Aram-Naharaim*; or, *the country of Syria situate between two rivers*, i.e. Tigris and Euphrates, whence it has its name, *Naharaim*, of the dual number, i.e. *Of the two rivers*. In this country Abraham lived with Terah, Nahor and Lot, before he went into the land of Canaan: And afterwards Jacob sojourned there with Bethuel and Laban, and from thence had his wives.

**V. 9.*** {the children of Israel cried unto the Lord}* i.e. Being brought into great miseries by reason of their servitude, and finding no help from their idols whom they had served, they returned unto the Lord by repentance, and cried unto him for mercy and forgiveness.

*{the Lord raised up a deliverer}* Or, *a savior and redeemer*; whom, when they were sold into the hands of their enemies, God sent to redeem them and give them deliverance. Herein Othniel, as also the rest of the judges, was a type of our Savior Christ, who was sent of God to deliver us out of the hands of all our spiritual enemies.

**V. 10.*** {And the Spirit of the Lord came upon him}* i.e. The Lord by an extraordinary instinct and motion of his Spirit stirred him up, and called him to undertake this great work, and fitted him for it, by endowing him with wisdom, fortitude and courage, to make war against this tyrannous oppressor, and having vanquished him, to govern his people in peace, according to his law, in the profession and practice of his true religion. So ch. 6:34 and 11:29. See on ch. 2:16.

*{and his hand prevailed against Chushan-Rishathaim}* To wit, for the subduing of him, and keeping him and his people in subjection.

**V. 11.*** {And the land had rest}* War and servitude being ended, true religion being restored, and the land flourishing in peace and plenty.

*{forty years}* Or, *to the fortieth year*, reckoning from the death of Joshua to the death of Othniel, as may be gathered from 1 Kings 6:1, where are expressed four hundred and eighty years from the coming of Israel out of Egypt to the building of Solomon’s temple. But if (as some do) we should take the years of the Israelites’ rest and peace, and the years of their oppressions under their enemies, and the years of their judges by whom they were delivered, severally and distinctly one from the other, the space between their coming out of Egypt and the building of the temple, would amount to about six hundred years. But we must necessarily maintain that computation of four hundred and eighty years to be true, and therefore not take the times here mentioned, of the people’s oppression under enemies, and of their rest under their judges, severally, but join themselves together in one, and reckon still from the death of the former judge to the death of him that next succeeded, letting the times of the oppressions for the most part run into the account of the judge that put an end to them; as Chushan-rishathaim’s eight years, into Othniel’s forty, and the Moabites’ eighteen, into Ehud’s eighty, v. 14, 30. For those eighty years are not so to be taken as if Ehud ruled so long; but that the death of Ehud happened in the eightieth year after the death of Othniel: The space between being spent by the Israelites after divers fashions. At the beginning, it may be, for a while after Othniel’s death they lived quietly and orderly of themselves; then they fell to idolatry, and continued some space therein, God in the meanwhile forbearing to inflict on them deserved punishments; then they lived in servitude under the Moabites for eighteen years; at last Ehud reduced them to liberty and order, and so kept them till his death. In like manner Othniel’s forty years here, and Deborah’s, ch. 5:31, and Gideon’s, ch. 8:28, are to be computed. Now if it be demanded how in this and other places it can be said that the land had rest for so many years, seeing the people spent some part of them under the oppression of cruel enemies, to this it is answered, That it is usual in the Scripture to denominate the whole from the best or the greatest part, and under it to include the residue. See Gen. 35:26, and compare Acts 7:14 with Gen. 46:27.

**V. 12.*** {And the children of Israel did evil again}* Notwithstanding the experience they had of the manifold miseries which they by the like sins had brought upon themselves, and of God’s great mercy in delivering them out of them.

*{and the Lord strengthened Eglon king of Moab}* By giving him courage and fortitude, and also willingness and resolution to fight against them; and contrariwise by weakening the Israelites, and making them cowardly and fainthearted through the guilt of their sin.

*{because they had done evil}* Because they had provoked the Lord to anger by their shameful apostasy and idolatry, therefore he raised up against them such malicious enemies as should bring them into most grievous servitude. For they were their ancient enemies, who sought their ruin before they had provoked them by making war against them: They were enemies to God and his true religion, and were excluded, even to the tenth generation, from coming into the congregation: And now they were incensed against Israel, because for hiring Balaam to curse them, and following his cursed counsel of prostituting their daughters, and drawing them thereby to idolatry, the Israelites by God’s command had made war against them, and therein prevailing, had slaughtered and destroyed a great part of them. In all which respects the Israelites could look for no mercy at their hands, now they had got them under their subjection.

**V. 13.*** {And he gathered unto him the children of Ammon and Amalek}* It may be understood, either that the Lord (of whom he last spoke, v. 12) gathered them to punish his people; or, that Eglon, as his instrument, did gather them, they being neighbor nations in confederacy with him, and in like enmity against God’s people, ch. 11:4, Exod. 17:8–16, 1 Sam. 15:2.

*{and possessed the City of Palm trees}* i.e. Jericho. See on ch. 1:16. For howsoever the city Jericho was burned and utterly ruined, yet in the territories belonging to it there might remain divers hamlets and villages fit for habitation. Or it may be, it being a place very fruitful and pleasant, he had seated himself there, and built some forts and strongholds near unto Jericho, to keep in them his garrisons to overawe the Israelites and hold them in subjection, and also to secure the fords and passages over against Jericho into his own country, not only for continual commerce, but also that he might have supplies from thence upon all occasions, or a safe retreat, if at any time he should be overpowered. Which was the reason that Ehud, when he was confident of victory, caused in the first place these fords to be taken, that so the Moabites might not escape by flight, v. 28.

**V. 14.*** {served Eglon the King of Moab eighteen years}* They served the king of Mesopotamia eight years; and now Eglon eighteen; and afterwards Jabin, twenty, ch. 4:3. So their sins increasing, their servitude also was increased; and when lesser corrections would not prevail to restrain them from sin and keep them in obedience, the Lord laid heavier upon them; and when they abused his mercy and readiness to deliver them out of their miseries when they cried unto him, the Lord would not hear them when again they called upon him, but prolonged their afflictions to keep them from presuming on his mercy for the time to come.

**V. 15.*** {a Benjamite}* Or, *the son of Jemini*; whose family belonged to the tribe of Benjamin.

*{a man left-handed}* Heb. *Shut of his right hand*: i.e. Not having the like use of it as others have that are right-handed, even as they have not the like use of their left. And this comes to pass either from some weakness and infirmity of nature; or (and that most ordinarily) from an ill custom in childhood, which in time becomes a second nature: and sometimes it is affected, and attained unto by practice, by those who are martially disposed, that they may with more advantage handle their weapons; as those Benjaminites, ch. 20:16. In many, to be left-handed, is a sign of force and courage.

*{and by him the children of Israel sent a present}* Not their ordinary tribute, but a gift of free bounty and homage to gain his favor, that he might not rule with rigor. With this present by God’s providence Ehud was sent, that he might gain the more free access, and execute his design without suspicion.

**V. 16.*** {But Ehud made him a dagger which had two edges}* That it might the more easily and speedily pierce, and make the deeper wound.

*{of a cubit length}* Which was long enough to do the deed, and short enough to be covered under his garment.

*{upon his right thigh}* Both because he was most likely that it would not be observed there where a weapon is not usually worn, and that being left-handed he might draw it out with more nimbleness.

**V. 17.*** {And he brought the present unto Eglon}* Though he had many associates for the gracing of the embassy, yet he was the man that presented the gift, to ingratiate him the more with the tyrant, and to gain access when he came again.

*{Eglon was a very fat man}* Therefore the more unable to make resistance, or avoid the stroke when he was assaulted.

**V. 18.*** {he sent away the people that bare the present}* After he had accompanied them as far as Gilgal, he sent them homewards, and he himself alone returned, that so he might do the fact with more conveniency, being more likely to have access without suspicion when he came alone, than if he had brought many in his company; and that he might himself run the hazard, and secure all the rest of his company from the danger which might attend such an execution; and that having none to take care for but himself, he might make the better shift to escape when the deed was done, and slip away with more secrecy.

**V. 19.*** {from the quarries that were by Gilgal}* Or, *the graven images*: i.e. Either a place where Eglon had set up his idols, the more to vex the children of Israel, or to allure them to embrace his idolatry; or the quarry out of which they dug their materials and stones whereof they made their graven images. Some conceive they might be those twelve stones erected, Josh. 4:20.

*{I have a secret errand unto thee}* Which Eglon understood should be delivered by word of mouth; but Ehud meant it of an handy act to be executed on him by commission from God.

*{who said, Keep silence}* i.e. If it be a secret from God, then hold your peace till the company be departed that are about me, and then privately acquaint me with it. Others think that he spoke this to his attendants, commanding them to withdraw till Ehud had delivered his secret message.

**V. 20.*** {and he was sitting in a summer parlour}* Heb. *A parlor of cooling*; wherein usually in those hot countries they did refresh themselves, as we in our banqueting houses. See on Jer. 36:22.

*{which he had for himself alone}* Into which he went when he desired to be private, and therefore now more fit for the hearing of a secret errand.

*{I have a message from God unto thee}* Therefore to be hearkened unto with more reverence and attention. This he speaks to amuse and amaze him, that he might be less heedful in attending to that action which he intended.

*{And he arose out of his seat}* He was (though an heathenish king) so much affected with the name of God, that though he were corpulent and unwieldy, he rose out of his seat and reverently stood upon his feet to hear his message, and thereby gave unto Ehud the fitter opportunity of giving him that mortal wound.

**V. 21.*** {and thrust it into his belly}* For this he had a special commission from God, who raised him up to deliver his people, v. 15. Therefore it is no precedent warranting any to murder princes, although they be heretics, idolaters and tyrants.

**V. 22.*** {And the haft also went in}* The wound was so deep and large that the haft followed the blade.

*{and the dirt came out}* Or, *it came out of the fundament*: i.e. Not out of the wound, which was closed up, but out of the common passage, as it usually does in, or a little after, the pangs of death, when nature has lost its retentive faculty.

**V. 23.*** {Then Ehud went forth through the porch}* i.e. He passed by the king’s servants, that attended there, with a composed countenance, and not overhasty gait, as one not guilty of such a fact, nor in the least measure to be suspected of it, being in this extremity of danger supported with an undaunted courage and good conscience, which bore him witness that he had done nothing but what God commanded, and that therefore he was safe under his protection.

*{and shut the doors}* Which having, it is probable, a spring lock, locked in the shutting: for otherwise it is not likely that he could have locked the doors and carried away the key without some suspicion, if any saw him. Others think he stole out secretly, none espying him, which made them think he was still with their lord: but this is not so probable.

**V. 24.*** {Surely he covereth his feet}* i.e. He does his easement: which the Hebrews modestly express by this phrase, 1 Sam. 24:3, because, sitting down, the feet were covered with the upper garment, which used to be long in those countries. Some others think that his servants supposed he lay down to sleep in his summer room, and on that occasion covered his feet, as Boaz did, Ruth 3:7, and that therefore they hastened not to come in for fear of awakening him; which opinion has the more probability, not only because it was the custom so to do in those hot countries, as we see in the example of Ishbosheth, 2 Sam. 4:5, and David, 2 Sam. 11:2, but because this better suited with a summer parlor and place of delight than the other did.

**V. 25.*** {And they tarried till they were ashamed}* i.e. Now beginning to fear, because he was so long before he opened the door, that some evil had befallen him, and that this might be imputed unto them as a fault, and neglect of their lord, they were ashamed of their long staying and not looking after him.

*{therefore they took a key}* It being usual in kings’ houses to have divers keys for the same door.

**V. 26.*** {and escaped unto Seirath}* Near unto mount Ephraim, not far from the place where Eglon was slain.

**V. 27.*** {and the children of Israel}* Not all, but as many as could be gathered together about mount Ephraim, and the places adjoining.

**V. 28.*** {took the fords of Jordan toward Moab}* To wit, that neither the Moabites in Canaan might escape into their own country, nor they in the land of Moab pass over Jordan to aid them in Canaan.

*{and suffered not a man to pass over}* Because the Israelites were a great and well-ordered body to make good the place, and the Moabites a scattered and routed army in flight.

**V. 30.*** {and the land had rest fourscore years}* i.e. Unto the eightieth year, reckoning from the death of Othniel, and so taking into the account the time of the people’s defection after his death, when they had no judge over them, wherein they prospered in their apostasy, and the eighteen years in which they were oppressed by the Moabites. See on v. 11. Some count from the death of Othniel to the death of Shamgar; others, from the division of the land, casting in the forty years, v. 11, into this sum. Others, instead of eighty years, read *eight years*.

**V. 31.*** {And after him was Shamgar the son of Anath}* Concerning this Shamgar the Scripture speaks very little. For it does not express of what family or tribe he was, only that he was the son of Anath, who it may be was so famous in those days that they which knew him knew also his tribe and family. Neither does it clearly show whether he were one of the judges or no; or, if he were, how long he held this place of government; but only that he succeeded Ehud, and delivered Israel from the Philistims when they invaded the land. Nor does it relate any more acts of his or victories obtained by him, although it is very probable there were divers more, though this only is mentioned, as being the most famous, if not miraculous. See on ch. 4:1. Josephus says he ruled about one year. If so, it is added either to Ehud’s or Deborah’s account. See ch. 5:6.

*{which slew of the Philistines six hundred men with an ox goad}* Or, as the vulgar Latin has it, *with the coulter, or share, of a plow*. But the best reading is, as we have it, *with a goad*. Which some, thinking too too weak means to achieve such a conquest, suppose that many of the country people joined with him, though he was their captain and commander, and so armed as is here said, for want of better weapons. See ch. 5:8, 1 Sam. 13:19, 22. But it is not likely, that bordering upon such enemies, and at this time not in their power, they were so slenderly provided of better weapons; and it is not safe to go from the words of the text upon weak and ungrounded conjectures. And therefore seeing when God is with us, one is sufficient to chase a thousand, and the weakest weapon strong enough when he puts strength into it, I see no reason why we may not take the very letter of the text, and think that Shamgar himself alone might as well slay six hundred Philistims with an ox goad, as Samson a thousand with the jawbone of an ass, ch. 15:15. And to this I the rather incline, because this here is recorded as a strange and miraculous victory, whereas there should be no such wonder if he being assisted with many others had achieved it.

## CHAPTER 4

**Verse 1.*** {And the children of Israel again did evil in the sight of the Lord}* i.e. Notwithstanding the manifold miseries they had found their sins had brought upon them, and the great mercy of God in delivering them upon their repentance, yet no sooner was Ehud dead, but they returned to their vomit, and sinned more heinously than before. No mention is here made of Shamgar, who was between Ehud and Barak, because of his short time, and the few things done in his days, which tended only to a preventing of such miseries and servitude as the Philistims were ready to bring the Israelites into, and not to any perfect deliverance out of them. See on ch. 3:31.

**V. 2.*** {And the Lord sold them}* See on ch. 3:14.

*{into the hand of Jabin}* We read of another Jabin king of Hazor, which was the head city in the north of Canaan, and their king the chief in that confederacy against Israel, Josh. 11:1, whom Joshua vanquished, slew, and burned his city to the ground. But this Jabin who perhaps was grandchild or nephew to the other, succeeding him, gathered strength and rebuilt Hazor, and making war against Israel, subdued them, and for their sins brought them into grievous servitude.

*{king of Canaan}* i.e. Either of that part of the land properly so called or of all the north part of the country, because his city was chief and head of all the rest, Josh. 11:10.

*{which dwelt in Harosheth of the Gentiles}* This was a vast woody strong place, whither the relics of the conquered nations resorted to inhabit as being most safe and defensible against the Israelites. And it being (as the Hebrews report) of a large and ample circuit, they built in it divers cities and strong castles.

**V. 3.*** {And the children of Israel cried unto the Lord}* No doubt they cried long before the Lord gave them deliverance, in the sense of their grievous miseries and oppressions, but not with their hearts, like those, Hos. 7:14, and all that while the Lord did not hear and deliver them; but now joining with their cries, faith and unfeigned repentance, he hears and sends deliverance. So ch. 10:10.

*{chariots of iron}* See on Josh. 17:16.

*{and twenty years he mightily oppressed the children of Israel}* Not only as a conqueror, but also as a malicious enemy, out of bitter and sharp revenge, because they had formerly cast his people out of their inheritance, slain his royal ancestor, and utterly ruined and burned his royal city. Moreover these his cruel oppressions were much embittered to the Israelites, because they suffered them under the Canaanites, that cursed nation, whom God had promised to cast out before them: for this was unto them a likely sign that God for their heinous sins had utterly rejected them.

**V. 4.*** {And Deborah a Prophetess, the wife of Lapidoth}* Some read, *a woman of Lapidoth*, taking it for the name of a place. Others, *a woman of lamps*, because, say they, she provided lamps for the service of the tabernacle, or because her face shone, as Moses’ did, Exod. 34:30, while she was prophesying.

*{she judged Israel at that time}* Not governing chiefly and properly as a judge, whose office was to hear and determine causes, and in these times principally, to make war against the enemies of God’s people for their deliverance; but, as a prophetess, counseling and directing the people that came unto her in hard and difficult causes, revealing unto them the will of God by the spirit of prophecy. And of her, who was of the weaker sex, God makes choice to effect this great work of the people’s deliverance, to manifest his power in the weakness of the means, that he alone might have the whole glory of it.

**V. 5.*** {palm tree of Deborah}* So called because she dwelt there.

*{between Ramah and Bethel in mount Ephraim}* i.e. Upon the edge or utmost border of mount Ephraim. For this Bethel was in the tribe of Benjamin, Josh. 18:13. Neither was this that Ramah or Ramathaim in which Elkanah and Samuel lived, 1 Sam. 1 and 2:11. For this was in Benjamin, Josh. 18:25, that in Ephraim, as some think.

*{came up to her for judgment}* i.e. To learn of her the will of God concerning things which themselves knew not, and at these times when public judicature failed by reason of oppression, sometimes also her judgment in some difficult cases, she being a prophetess.

**V. 6.*** {And she sent and called Barak}* In the authority of a prophetess, summoning him in the name of God, from whom she had special direction so to do, as appears by the words following.

*{out of Kedesh-Naphtali}* There were three cities of this name, one in Issachar, 1 Chron. 6:72, a second in Judah, Josh. 15:23, and this in Naphtali. It was seated between the waters of Merom and the lake of Gennesareth, and was a city of refuge and belonged to the priests, Josh. 19:37 and 20:7 and 21:32.

*{Hath not the Lord God of Israel commanded}* By this interrogation she enforces an affirmation; as is usual with the Hebrews: q.d. The Lord has assuredly commanded. So v. 14 and ch. 6:14, Gen. 13:9.

*{mount Tabor}* See on Josh. 19:12.

*{and take with thee ten thousand men}* This command does not strictly tie him to this number, nor to these tribes alone: for there came in to their assistance voluntarily of divers other tribes, as of Ephraim, Benjamin, Manasseh and Issachar, and the rest which withdrew are reproved, ch. 5:14, 15, and Meroz is cursed for neutrality, ch. 5:23: but these tribes more especially are called in to this service, first, because being near at hand, they were in greatest readiness to be suddenly raised; and secondly, because they were more interested in this war, seeing they were under greater oppression than the other tribes, especially Naphtali, unto which Hazor and Harosheth belonged: And unto this might be added, that Barak being of this tribe and in good esteem, they would be more willing to give him assistance.

**V. 7.*** {And I will draw unto thee}* Not by any force or outward compulsion, but by inclining his heart to make war against you. So Cant. 1:3, John 6:44.

*{with his chariots and his multitude}* Josephus relates he had three thousand chariots, ten thousand horse, and three hundred thousand foot.

**V. 8.*** {If thou wilt go with me, then I will go}* Hereby he does not show that he had no faith to believe her message from God, for the apostle commends him, with other worthies, for his faith, Heb. 11:32, but only that his faith was assaulted with doubting, and needed this help for its confirmation. And secondly, he would have Deborah to accompany him in this service, that being a prophetess she might on all occasions counsel and direct him, and also pray unto God for a blessing upon his endeavors.

**V. 9.*** {shall not be for thine honour}* i.e. Because you do hereby discover your diffidence, you shall not have that honor, that is due to a general for obtaining so great a victory, but a poor weak woman shall share with you in the glory of it, because out of your fear and weakness of faith you would not go out to battle, according to God’s command, unless supported by a woman. Yea, but God had promised, v. 7, that he would deliver Sisera into Barak’s hand, if he would go out to fight against him; and how was this promise verified, when as Jael killed him? I answer, There was a twofold kind of delivering; one, when he and his army were delivered by victory; another, when his person and life were delivered to death; and Barak should have had the honor of both, had it not been for his diffidence; but for want of faith and affiance he was deprived of the latter, howsoever he had the honor of the former.

**V. 10.*** {to Kedesh}* Here Barak was born; and here he mustered his army, as being near unto mount Tabor, whither he was commanded to go. See on v. 6.

*{with ten thousand men at his feet}* i.e. Who marched after him as their general in due order, and as it were treading in his steps. The like Hebraism we have, Exod. 11:8, 1 Kings 20:12.

**V. 11.*** {Now Heber the Kenite}* This digression is made to show how Jael the wife of Heber came to dwell in the land of Naphtali, and there did this exploit, seeing the Kenites dwelt in the lot of Judah, ch. 1:16, namely, because Heber, for some causes not expressed in Scripture, had severed himself from the rest of the Kenites, and here pitched his tent in the tribe of Naphtali.

**V. 13.*** {Kishon}* It arises out of the hills Tabor, and Gilboa, and afterwards divides itself into two parts, one whereof runs into the midland sea, the other into the lake of Gennesareth. See ch. 5:21, Psal. 83:9.

**V. 14.*** {Up}* i.e. Presently set upon the work, and fear not to enter into the battle in respect of the great strength and multitude of your enemies.

*{the Lord hath delivered Sisera}* i.e. He will as certainly deliver him into your hand as if it were already done. See on v. 9.

*{Is not the Lord gone out before thee}* i.e. Most certainly the Lord is gone out before you, as general of the army, to fight for you and give you the victory. See v. 6, ch. 5:20, 2 Sam. 5:24.

**V. 15.*** {And the Lord discomfited Sisera}* i.e. Howsoever he used Barak and the army of the Israelites as means and instruments to serve his providence in the obtaining of this victory, yet the Lord himself was the chief agent that effected it, Prov. 21:31, partly by taking away the courage and strength of the enemy, and possessing them with fear and astonishment, and contrariwise by putting courage and fortitude into the hearts of his people; and partly (as Josephus relates Antiq. l. 5) by fighting against the Canaanites from heaven, sending into their faces an impetuous storm of hail, as Josh. 10:11 and 1 Sam. 7:10, unto which Deborah alludes, ch. 5:20.

**V. 16.*** {there was not a man left}* i.e. In the field to make any resistance; but all were routed, scattered and slaughtered, Psal. 14:3 and 13:3, Rom. 3:12.

**V. 17.*** {For there was peace between Jabin—and the house of Heber}* i.e. No open hostility, Heber standing in a neutrality, in respect of martial undertakings, between Jabin and the Israelites, although he joined with God’s people in the profession and practice of the true religion; so he enjoyed peace with both, but not with the Canaanites by oath and covenant, for then Jael’s act had been faulty.

**V. 18.*** {Turn in, my Lord, turn in to me; fear not}* i.e. I will pass my promise to hide and secure you from them that pursue you. By which fair words she deludes him, intending nothing but his utter ruin, as the sequel shows. Therefore howsoever her cause was good for the matter of it, in that she sought to destroy an enemy to God and his church, who was already proscribed and destinated to death, and not out of any self end and respect; for he and she were in peace together, yet she in the manner failed, carrying on her design with lying and treachery. In which regard the same may be said of her that we say of the Egyptian midwives and Rahab, that God accepted her work, and blessed her for it, and of his free grace pardoned her sin and failings in the carriage of it.

*{she covered him with a mantle}* Pretending herein a double care, both to hide him from those that pursued him, if any happened to come into the tent, and also, he being now in a great heat, to keep him from catching cold; but indeed intending hereby to bring him the sooner to fall asleep, that with more conveniency she might execute her design.

**V. 19.*** {for I am thirsty}* To wit, through grief and vexation of heart, and through heat, faintness and weariness, by reason of his hasty flight on his feet.
