*{and she opened a bottle of milk}* i.e. Either because it was next at hand, or to show unto him the greater respect, or as fitter for him to drink than cold water, being in a great heat; but in her own intention to bring him the sooner asleep, that she might set upon her intended work. At this Deborah seems to aim in her song, ch. 5:25, as a special act of her wisdom and policy.

**V. 20.*** {thou shalt say, No}* This he requires; but she prudently gives no promise unto it.

**V. 21.*** {Then Jael Heber’s wife took a nail of the tent}* i.e. A long pin pointed with iron, used to fasten the cords of the tent, when they spread it abroad, unto the ground, to keep it firm against wind and weather, and therefore long and strong enough for her intended use.

*{and smote the nail into his temples}* This act proceeded from a singular zeal to God’s glory, and to the welfare of his church and people, which had been cruelly oppressed by this tyrant, who was now by God designed to destruction. Jael was inspired in an extraordinary manner and measure by God’s Spirit, seeing she is so highly commended, and pronounced blessed above women, ch. 5:24.

**V. 22.*** {Jael came out to meet him}* Namely to congratulate his victory, and to bring unto him the good news of Sisera’s death, who if he had lived and escaped might have renewed his strength, and raised a new war against God’s people.

**V. 23.*** {So God subdued on that day Jabin}* i.e. By the slaughter of his army, the destroying of his chariots and horse, and the death of his general, that he was now so far from having any power to oppress God’s people that he was not able to defend himself against them.

**V. 24.*** {And the hand of the children of Israel prospered}* Heb. *Going went and was hard*, i.e. Prevailed so long, until they had brought him and his people to an utter and final destruction.

## CHAPTER 5

**Verse 1.*** {Then sang}* After this great victory they sang praises unto God, the chief author of it; as Moses and Miriam did, when they saw the Egyptians drowned in the Red Sea, Exod. 15.

*{Deborah and Barak}* Deborah, as a prophetess, and a chief instrument also used by God for the obtaining of this victory, breaks out into God’s praises by singing this song composed by her, to the glory of God, who had subdued their foes before them; and Barak joins with her in this holy music, as chief commander when this battle was fought and the victory obtained.

**V. 2.*** {Praise ye the Lord for the avenging of Israel}* i.e. For executing his just judgments, in the destruction of Sisera and his army, and subduing the Canaanites and their king Jabin, who had been for twenty years grievous oppressors of his people.

*{when the people willingly offered themselves}* Hereby are chiefly understood the two tribes of Zebulun and Naphtali, v. 18 and ch. 4:10, but with them also the other tribes that came in to assist them in the day of battle, as Ephraim, Manasseh, Issachar, v. 14, 15. But Zebulun and Naphtali first appeared, coming in as volunteers at the sound of the trumpet, Barak having no authority to press them to the service.

**V. 3.*** {Hear, O ye Kings; give ear, O ye Princes}* In a rapture of joy poetically expressed she calls in kings to be her auditors, and spectators of those works of wonder which God had done for his people in avenging them on their mighty enemies, that they might learn not to glory in their own strength, seeing it is God alone that gives victory; nor presuming on their own power, use cruelty and pride in their oppressions, seeing there is one mightier than they, who can give deliverance unto the oppressed even when they are helpless and hopeless, Eccles. 5:8.

**V. 4.*** {Lord, when thou wentest out of Seir}* i.e. When the Edomites denying your people passage through their country, you did cause them to fetch a compass about it, and going before them in a cloudy pillar, did lead them in a long circuit into the land of promise, after they had subdued king Arad, Numb. 20:21 and 21:4.

*{the earth trembled}* i.e. Either the earth itself, in a poetical expression, or the nations inhabiting that part of the earth, i.e. The Edomites, Amorites, Canaanites, trembled for fear of the Israelites’ near approach. See Deut. 2:25, Josh. 5:1.

*{the heavens dropped}* With extraordinary showers and storms, Psal. 68:8.

**V. 5.*** {The mountains melted}* By these poetical and hyperbolical expressions is showed how the nations were affrighted and amazed when God showed visible signs of his power in protecting his people as they traveled towards the land of promise. So Psal. 68:15, 16, &c.

*{even that Sinai}* i.e. Even as mount Sinai had formerly done, when God gave and proclaimed his law. Others conceive these words are to be understood of those thunders, lightnings, earthquakes and tempests whereby God manifested his glory and majesty at the giving of the law upon mount Sinai, because then also he is said to have come from Seir, Deut. 33:2, and the shaking of Sinai is here expressed, and also Psal. 68:7, 8. But leaving every man to his choice, and submitting myself to better judgments, I still rather incline to the former interpretation, I. Because the former wonders were far more remote than these last, which were but of late, and being still fresh in the people’s memories, were fit to make the deeper impression. II. Because this dread, fear and trembling which fell upon the nations was not prophesied of, and promised at the giving of the law, which was long before, but when the Israelites took their journey towards Canaan, and had passed over the river Arnon, Deut. 2:24, 25. III. Because we read of much fire, smoke and darkness at the giving of the law, but of no storms, dropping of the clouds, and raining. But IV. This especially moves me to be of his mind, because this going out of Seir cannot be meant of Sinai in this place, seeing God is never said to have come out of Sinai, and the next words following do expound the former, and plainly show, out of what place God went, namely, *When thou marchedst out of the field of Edom, then the earth trembled*; &c.

**V. 6.*** {In the days of Shamgar}* i.e. Though Shamgar had begun a great deliverance by a wonderful victory, ch. 3:31, and though Jael, who lived at the same time, was accepted of God for her great zeal in seeking the good of the people, and freeing them all she might from cruel oppressions; the which now she has more clearly manifested, in putting herself upon that great and dangerous work of killing Sisera their chief enemy; yet in the time of both of them, notwithstanding their good endeavors, God’s people lay under the great oppressions of their enemies, so as the highways remained unoccupied, because there was no commerce nor intercourse of trading, men not daring to travel to and fro about their business for fear of enemies and robbers. See Lev. 26:22, 2 Chron. 15:5, Isa. 33:1, Lam. 1:4 and 4:18.

*{and the travellers walked through byways}* i.e. If their occasions were of that importance that they were necessitated to go out about them, they were fain to forsake the beaten roads, and find out secret and unhaunted bypaths, that the enemies might not discover them, and that they might escape robbers.

**V. 7.*** {the inhabitants of the villages ceased}* i.e. Unwalled towns lay waste and forsaken, because the country people not daring to dwell in them, as being undefensible against enemies, were constrained for the safeguard of their lives and goods to retire themselves into walled cities and places of strength.

*{until that I Deborah arose, that I arose a mother in Israel}* i.e. Till I, as a governess and a prophetess, teaching them God’s will, and with no less care seeking their good than a loving mother her dear children’s, have encouraged them to this war, which has freed them out of all their calamities.

**V. 8.*** {They chose new gods}* i.e. Because by their shameful apostasy they fell away from God and his pure worship, and worshiped heathen idols, therefore he raised against them on every side cruel enemies, and plagued them with all the miseries of war, chap. 2:10 and 4:1.

*{then was war in the gates}* i.e. War with the miseries thereof extended not only to their villages and unwalled towns, but even to their cities and places of chiefest strength; yea, their very gates, the place of their going out and coming in for commerce, and of their judicature, were not free from the enemies’ inroads: so as there was no exercise of trading, or execution of justice.

*{was there a shield or spear seen}* Either because their enemies had quite disarmed them of all their weapons and ammunition, 1 Sam. 13:19, 22, or else had brought them into such a slavish fear that not one among forty thousand of Israel dared be known to have them. And this is more probable, being implied in the text: For it is not said that they had not shield or spear, but that they were not seen among them: Levit. 26:36.

**V. 9.*** {My heart is towards the Governors of Israel}* i.e. From my heart I honor and love the governors and heads of the tribes, who offered themselves to be captains and leaders in this war, by their example encouraging the common people.

**V. 10.*** {Speak, ye that ride on white asses}* i.e. Such as were fat and fair, bright, slick and shining, upon which princes and governors used in those days to ride, as an ensign of their honor, state and power: let such bless the Lord, and speak of his great acts. See ch. 10:4 and 12:14.

*{ye that sit in judgment, and walk by the way}* i.e. You that are chief magistrates and judges among the people, and you also of the common sort who usually walk on foot about your business, and travel in the highways, do you all join together in praising the Lord for this great deliverance, by which you have recovered all your privileges; so that you, princes, who were abased by Jabin’s tyranny, may now again ride in honor; you, judges and magistrates, who had all your power and authority in executing justice and judgment taken from you, are now restored to your places, and have power to execute your office and jurisdiction; and you, common people, who dared not travel in the highways about your business for fear of cutthroat soldiers and robbers, may now securely travel from place to place according to your several occasions.

**V. 11.*** {They that are delivered from the noise of Archers in the places of drawing water}* i.e. They who were affrighted and endangered when they went out of their cities to their pits and wells to draw and fetch in water, (a commodity scarce, yet more necessary in those hot and dry countries) by reason that their enemies scouting and hiding themselves about those places, assaulted them when they came out to draw water with hideous outcries and fearful alarms, took them prisoners and brought them into slavery; or, if they fled before they could come so near, shot their arrows at them, wounded and killed them; so as they could not come to draw without eminent danger. From which they are now wholly secured by this victory. See on Neh. 4:23.

*{there shall they rehearse the righteous acts of the Lord}* i.e. In avenging his people on their enemies, and delivering them from their oppressions.

*{of his villages in Israel}* The villages were in most danger, because least defensible, v. 7, but now by this victory safe for habitation.

*{then shall the people of the Lord go down to the gates}* i.e. They shall now enjoy free liberty to go out and in by the gates of their cities; all sorts of people, both citizens and countrymen, may have free commerce one with another, and come without danger to their markets and courts of justice, whereas before this victory there was war in the very gates, v. 8.

**V. 12.*** {Awake, awake, Deborah}* In a rhetorical elegancy of speech she stirs all to praise and thanksgiving, and begins with herself rousing up her own spirit, that she might be a precedent and example for their imitation; like a cock who awakes himself by clapping his wings, before by his crowing he awakes others.

*{lead thy captivity captive}* i.e. Now after your victory lead them captive that kept you and your people in captivity, Psal. 68:18. Neither does that hinder, that the people of Israel were bound by God’s law to kill all of these cursed nations which they subdued, seeing after their triumphing over them they might put them to death, as appears in the example of the five kings of Canaan thus used by Joshua, Josh. 10:24, 26.

**V. 13.*** {Then he made him that remaineth have dominion over the nobles}* i.e. The Lord has now made the remainder of the children of Israel, rescued by this victory out of the tyranny of the Canaanites, to have dominion over their nobles.

*{the Lord made me have dominion}* i.e. Me Deborah, though of the weaker sex, he has made to have dominion over the mighty, and by my counsel and encouragement to subdue great princes and commanders.

**V. 14.*** {Out of Ephraim}* Here Deborah begins to relate how the several tribes acquitted themselves in this expedition, praising or blaming them according to their merit or demerit.

*{was there a root of them against Amalek?}* Some take this root to be Deborah, a Ephraimite, and dwelling upon mount Ephraim, ch. 4:5, the root and first mover in this expedition against the Amalekites the ancient enemies of Israel, who joined with the Canaanites in this war. Others, but with less probability, take it to be Joshua an Ephraimite, who subdued Amalek, Exod. 17:13. For the author’s scope here is not to commend Joshua for that which was done so long ago, but those who had engaged themselves in this present war, and amongst the rest, the Ephraimites, who were termed a root, because Deborah dwelling among them, they by her counsel and instigation, had an hand in the first beginnings of this war. Some will have the tribe of Judah to be here meant, whose root, or southern part, extended to Amalek, ch. 1:16, 1 Sam. 15:6. See on ch. 12:15.

*{after thee, Benjamin}* Some understand this as an apostrophe to the Lord; q.d. Benjamin, O Lord, came next after Ephraim, to assist your armies in this war. Others more probably take it as spoken to those of Ephraim, who came first out in this expedition, and were followed by Benjamin.

*{out of Machir}* i.e. The Manassites descending from Machir the only son of Manasseh, Josh. 17:1. This must be understood of that half of the tribe which dwelt within Canaan, and not of that beyond Jordan; for they are afterwards reproved for deserting God’s people in this war, v. 17.

*{came down governors}* i.e. Prime men, who were either magistrates in their own country, and therefore very praiseworthy, for hazarding themselves in such a dangerous expedition; or else such as were fit for the place of captains and commanders, and therefore men most necessary at such a time and for such a service.

*{and they out of Zebulun that handle the pen of the writer}* i.e. Not only such as by their profession were fit to be captains and soldiers, but even scribes and scholars, men addicted to studies, with unanimous consent joined with the rest in taking up arms to fight God’s battles, and help their brethren.

**V. 15.*** {And the Princes of Issachar were with Deborah}* i.e. Agreed with her to give her their assistance when they were required.

*{even Issachar, and also Barak}* i.e. Not only the princes, but also a great part of the tribe, together with Barak, who was their general and chief commander.

*{he was sent on foot into the valley}* i.e. Being so commanded by God, he left mount Tabor, a place of strength and advantage, and went down on foot into the valley; i.e. He with his army, being all footmen, were very willing to take any pains in this service, and content to fight with the Canaanites in a place of great disadvantage; for their iron chariots and horsemen were in the valleys of greatest use.

*{For the divisions of Reuben there were great thoughts of heart}* Or, *in the divisions of Reuben*. If we take the words according to the former reading, the meaning is, There were great reasonings among the other tribes that favored the cause, and much muttering and murmuring against Reuben, in that being a rich and powerful tribe, they should desert their brethren, and with them the cause of God, either for love of ease or cowardly fear. But if we take the latter reading, then the meaning is, The Reubenites had reasonings and disputes one with another; one party holding that they ought not to desert their brethren, and with them the cause of God and his true religion; and the other, which were the stronger and more prevalent, that they ought chiefly and in the first place to provide for their own peace and safety, which they could not do, if they engaged themselves in this war: Those, being weary of servitude under the cruel tyrant, thought they should take this opportunity of freeing themselves out of it; but these maintained they should run a desperate danger in rising against a mighty and potent king, unto whom also they had submitted themselves as tributaries and vassals; seeing if they miscarried in the action they should be utterly lost and ruined; therefore they held it better to forbear till they did see the issue, and in the meantime to enjoy their ease, with their wealth and pleasures in peace and safety, and not unnecessarily run themselves into such desperate hazards. Others think they deserted the cause out of the pride of their hearts; because they would not follow the conduct of a woman.

**V. 16.*** {Why abodest thou among the sheepfolds}* i.e. Why did you, O Reuben, desert your brethren, and stay at home under so poor and base a pretense as tending your flocks, because you are rich in them and abound in cattle, Num. 32:1, 2, 4, which must be carefully looked unto to preserve them from miscarriage.

*{to hear the bleatings of the flocks}* She upbraids them for being more affected with the bleatings of their sheep than with the outcries, groans and complaints of their brethren under Jabin’s tyranny, and therefore choosing rather by staying at home to let them continue still under grievous oppression, than by assisting in this war to hazard the loss of all, in case the tyrant prevailed: Which argued their covetousness and baseness of spirit, in that under so sordid a pretense they would desert the cause of God’s people, which was of such weight and concernment. See the like, Luke 14:18.

**V. 17.*** {Gilead abode beyond Jordan}* i.e. The tribe of Gad and the other half-tribe of Manasseh, unto whom, together with Reuben, Gilead was given, Num. 32:22, 40, Deut. 3:12, 14, Josh. 13:24, 25, 31, stayed at home under the same pretenses, and because living beyond Jordan they were not yet oppressed by Jabin, nor like to be, unless they provoked him by joining with their brethren in this war against him, which they might forbear to do under a fair pretense, that they were separated from them by the river, and in such a distance that they could not come time enough to their assistance.

*{why did Dan remain in ships}* Dan’s inheritance lay on the seacoast of Canaan, Josh. 19:40, having Japho or Joppa in their lot, an haven upon the Mediterranean, from whence they traded with other countries; which they pretended so wholly took them up that they had no leisure to assist their brethren. But one of the rabbins affirms this to be the true cause which made them hold back, because out of cowardice and fear they dared not assist them against so powerful a tyrant, but rather thought of fleeing away by sea, having such store of shipping, if he should prevail against their brethren; though hereby they did willfully banish themselves out of the promised land, and the place of God’s presence, where they enjoyed his holy ordinances.

*{Asher continued on the Sea shore}* Their inheritance also lying for the most part on the seacoast, Josh. 19:24, 29, they pretended the like excuses to desert the service.

*{and abode in his breaches}* Or, *creeks*. Either from thence to follow his merchandise, or under pretense of his necessary staying at home to mend those breaches which the sea floods had made, and which if neglected would be quickly enlarged, and put their country in danger of drowning.

**V. 18.*** {in the high places of the field}* i.e. Descending from mount Tabor, whither God had sent Barak to begin the war, into the valleys near the river Kishon, to fight the battle, they greatly hazarded themselves in the cause of God and for the good of his people, the plain champaign being far more fit for Sisera his horse and chariots than for their foot army: ch. 4:10, 14.

**V. 19.*** {The kings came and fought}* i.e. Jabin and his confederate kings of Canaan, or their generals and captains under their command.

*{in Taanach by the waters of Megiddo}* These two were rivers, in the tribe of Manasseh, which had two cities adjoining to them called by these names, Josh. 17:11. For Jabin’s army was so hugely vast and numerous that it filled all the champaign country: and therefore though it is said this battle was fought near Tabor and the river Kishon, ch. 4:6, 7, Psal. 83:9, and here, in Taanach by the waters of Megiddo, both may well stand, seeing these places were near one another, though in divers tribes, and the battle might begin in one place, and through the retreat or flight of the enemy end in the other.

*{They took no gain of money}* i.e. They voluntarily came to assist Jabin in this service, not expecting from him any pay, but merely out of malice towards God’s people, from whose overthrow they looked for much spoil; but they utterly failed in their hopes, for they got nothing by the bargain, but lost all they had and themselves also.

**V. 20.*** {They fought from heaven}* i.e. The meteors from heaven, as wind, thunder, hail, as it were conspired to work their ruin. See on ch. 4:15. Or it may be they were terrified from heaven with the noise of an approaching army, as the Philistines, 2 Sam. 5:24, and the Syrians, 2 Kings 7:6.

*{the stars in their courses fought against Sisera}* i.e. They in their stations, like soldiers ranged in a battle, as the host of God, with their influences raising storms and horrible tempests, conspired all together with earthly creatures, to work the ruin of Sisera and his army: By which kind of rhetorical expressions she would show, that not only the Israelites, but even the Lord himself, both from heaven and on earth, by all manner of means and creatures, did fight against them. See annot. 2 on Gen. 2:1.

**V. 21.*** {The river of Kishon swept them away}* i.e. This river famous (though not for greatness, yet) for antiquity did also help to destroy them, and swept them away, even as a broom, dust and filth out of an house. And this it did when by reason of the mighty storm of rain and hail being suddenly risen to a great height, it drowned those who in their flight sought to pass over it: or swelling over its banks, it did carry and sweep away many dead bodies that were slain in the battle.

*{O my soul, thou hast trodden down strength}* By this apostrophe to her soul Deborah excites herself to glory in the Lord, who had heard her prayer, and given her victory and triumph over her proud enemies, who gloried in their own strength.

**V. 22.*** {Then were the horsehoofs broken by the means of the pransings}* i.e. Though they had many goodly horses, so fed and pampered that for mere wantonness and greatness of spirit they could not stand still upon the ground, yet they became unserviceable, and could not hurt the Israelites, by reason that through their pride and stomach they broke their hoofs, and foundered their feet in that hot, hard and stony ground with their pawings and prancings. Others take it that they broke their hoofs in their headlong and furious posting away to save their lives: And so the vulgar Latin expresses it.

**V. 23.*** {Curse ye Meroz (said the angel of the Lord)}* What this Meroz was, no man can determine, seeing there is no mention to be found of it elsewhere, neither in former nor succeeding ages. Which seems to me to be one effect of this curse, that the very name and memorial of Meroz is wholly extinct and blotted out. So Prov. 10:7, Psal. 109:13. But it is apparent that it was some city near the place where this battle was fought, which being of considerable strength, and able to assist in this war, were called in to give their assistance, but refused to do it, and rather chose to stand in a cold neutrality, out of base and cowardly fear in respect of the power of their enemy. And this was an heinous sin: For the weaker God’s people were, the more need they had of aid. And their infidelity and distrust was the greater, in that they refused to help the Lord, that is, God’s people: for he himself being omnipotent needed not their help, and was able also to protect his servants, and them also, if they would have adventured themselves in his quarrel. And hence it is that whereas other tribes are only reproved for holding back, Meroz is cursed with a bitter curse: And that it might not seem to have proceeded from passion and spleen, it is added that it was backed by divine authority, and pronounced by the command of an angel, even the angel of the covenant, Jesus Christ, who is most zealous in the cause of his church. See ch. 6:11 and 2:1. Some think Meroz was a name transformed, to signify obscurely some city; as Mic. 1:10, 11, 12: or, that the three consonants, M, R, Z, were the initial letters of the names of three cities that being near to the place of this battle, yet afforded no assistance to their brethren.

**V. 24.*** {Blessed above women shall Jael—be}* i.e. She shall be magnified for this heroic act among all that shall hear of it, and God shall multiply his blessings upon her above others, as a gracious reward of this service.

*{in the tent}* By this is meant either that God would multiply his blessings upon her in her tent, i.e. In all domestical affairs; or, that she dwelling in a tent, as all the Kenites did, should be blessed above all the rest.

**V. 25.*** {she brought forth butter}* i.e. Either cream, in which the butter is; or else, after she had given him milk to drink, she gave him also butter to eat. All which kindness she used to make him rest the more secure under her protection.

*{in a lordly dish}* i.e. In a rich and fair bowl befitting a man of his honor and dignity; or else, so large as that he might drink his fill out of it.

**V. 26.*** {and with the hammer she smote Sisera}* Heb. *She hammered him*, i.e. With the hammer she struck the nail through his temples.

**V. 27.*** {At her feet he bowed, he fell}* Here are rhetorically expressed the postures and motions of a man that has received a mortal wound: Not that every particular could be said of Sisera in the very letter; for being fastened unto the earth with a pin driven through his temples, he could not rise, and bow, and fall; but that in the pangs of death he did something in respect of the substance suitable to all these, that is, he lay prostrate at Jael’s feet having the convulsions of death upon him.

**V. 28.*** {The mother of Sisera}* Here we have another figurative amplification befitting a poetical hymn, whereby is supposed how it was likely that Sisera’s mother and her ladies behaved themselves in their words and actions upon this occasion of his not present coming; i.e. She looked out of a window, expecting his approach; she cried out, when she could not discover him, as one impatient of his delay and reasoned with herself and her ladies what the cause might be why he stayed so long: And then is showed what was likely to be spoken, by her ladies or herself, to quiet their minds and remove these fears, namely the hopes they had of his prosperous success, and that the causes of his stay were the fruits of his victory and the dividing of the spoil. Where we have a lively and ironical representation of pride and carnal confidence, presuming of success in enterprises upon sensual grounds without any respect to God’s power and providence.

**V. 30.*** {Have they not sped}* i.e. They have surely sped and obtained the victory, and therefore cannot come so soon, because they must have some time allowed them to divide the spoil.

*{needlework on both sides}* i.e. Both sides alike, and of equal beauty.

**V. 31.*** {be as the Sun when he goeth forth in his might}* i.e. As the sun when it rises in glory and brightness, and from the morning to high noon therein increases more and more; so let them that love the Lord increase daily in glory and happiness: Prov. 4:18, Psal. 37:6, 2 Sam. 23:4, Dan. 12:3.

*{And the land had rest forty years}* i.e. Unto the fortieth year, reckoning from Ehud’s death, or those eighty years mentioned ch. 3:30. See ch. 3:11.

## CHAPTER 6

**Verse 1.*** {And the children of Israel did evil in the sight of the Lord}* i.e. They provoked God to just displeasure by their other sins, but chiefly by their idolatry, which was that eminent evil for which they are reproved v. 10, and for which God inflicted upon them this present judgment.

*{and the Lord delivered them}* From this expression and some others that follow, some gather that neither they were now fallen into so deep a degree of apostasy and idolatry as themselves formerly and their fathers before them, nor that they were now so severely punished as in times past. For it is not said, as ch. 3:8 and 4:2, that God sold them, as slaves and bondmen to the Midianites, but only that he delivered them, i.e. So withdrew his protection and assistance as that they prevailed against them, and pillaged their country. Neither did he suffer them to lie under these oppressions for eighteen or twenty years, as ch. 3:14 and 4:3, but only for seven. And for their sin, it is not said that they added unto their former wickedness, and corrupted themselves more than their fathers, as ch. 2:19, but simply, that they did evil only.

*{into the hand of Midian}* The great ancestor of this people was Midian the son of Abraham by Keturah, Gen. 25:2, who gave name to a city and country situate beyond Arabia towards the south, near the Red Sea. And likewise his posterity after him were called Midianites. Here Zipporah Moses’ wife was born, and the Kenites that descended from Jethro. And this (as some think) is the country from whence those whom we call Gypsies do come. In those days they dwelt in tents, and lived upon the spoil of other nations, removing themselves, their wives, children, families and cattle from country to country, as it best liked them. Against these the Israelites made war, and destroyed many of them, when as they labored to supplant them with their wiles: And now it seems, having gotten strength, they make that their quarrel of seeking revenge. See on Hab. 3:7.

**V. 2.*** {and because of the Midianites}* i.e. For fear of being surprised and plundered by them.

*{the children of Israel made them the dens, &c.}* Partly for secrecy to hide themselves and their goods from the Midianites, and partly for defense, if they happened to be discovered by them. These dens and caves remained unto Saul’s and David’s time, 1 Sam. 13:6, and, it is very likely, long after.

**V. 3.*** {the Midianites came up, and the Amalekites, and the children of the east}* i.e. Not the Midianites alone, but also the Amalekites, who were of Esau’s posterity, Gen. 36:12, and always malicious enemies to the Israelites and with them also the children of the east, that is, the Arabians, who lived easterly in reference to Canaan, ch. 8:10, 11, Job 1:3, Isa. 13:20. All these in a joint confederacy conspired together to make yearly inroads into the land of Canaan, bringing with them their wives, families and cattle, before their corn was ripened, partly that they might live upon the spoil as long as it lasted, or they listed; and partly out of malice towards the Israelites, spoiling all which they could not spend, and carrying away their sheep, oxen and asses, that they might have no sustenance to live upon when they were gone.

**V. 4.*** {till thou come unto Gaza}* Lying in the utmost border of Canaan near the Mediterranean: whereby it appears they wasted the whole country, even from the east, on which they bordered, to the very borders on the west side.

**V. 5.*** {and they came as grasshoppers}* By which hyperbolical expression is noted their multitude, their army consisting of an hundred thirty-five thousand able fighting men, besides women and children and the common rascality that followed the camp, ch. 8:10, and withal, the horrible spoil which they made in the country, seeing like grasshoppers, they fell upon every place, and where they came devoured all, and left nothing behind them.

*{without number}* An Hebraism: i.e. So many that they were hardly to be numbered. So ch. 7:12. Whereas a small company is expressed by the contrary phrase, that they are men of number. See Gen. 34:30.

**V. 6.*** {and the children of Israel cried unto the Lord}* i.e. When all the means in which they had trusted did fail them, and their dens and caves could not protect them, then seeing their folly they sought unto the Lord their only rock and refuge, and returning unto him by true repentance they cried unto him for help and deliverance.

**V. 8.*** {the Lord sent a prophet}* Heb. *A man, a prophet*. Before he would give them deliverance, he fits them for it, by using the ministry of a prophet to bring them to repentance. He is said to have been *a man, a prophet*, that it might not be thought he was an angel. For angels in Scripture are not called prophets, though prophets are called angels, Mal. 3:1. And it may probably be thought that this prophet was sent unto them before they cried to the Lord, seeing the words may be read in the more-than-perfect tense, as well as in the perfect, thus, *The Lord had sent a prophet*, namely, to exhort them to repentance, and to cry unto the Lord for pardon and help.

*{I brought you up from Egypt}* This he first minds them of, as being the prime and principal benefit upon which depended all the rest following.

**V. 9.*** {And out of the hand of all that oppressed you}* i.e. Not only the Egyptians, but also all those nations which raised war against you in the wilderness, as the Amalekites, Arad, and the Canaanites, with the Midianites.

*{and drave them out from before you}* i.e. The Amorites and Canaanites.

**V. 10.*** {Fear not the gods of the Amorites}* i.e. Do not worship and serve them, out of an opinion that they are able to do you hurt, seeing they are but stocks and stones, and are able to do nothing against those who serving me only are under my protection.

**V. 11.*** {And there came an angel of the Lord}* i.e. The angel of the covenant, the Lord Christ, the eternal Son of God. For he is called Jehovah, v. 14, and the Lord, v. 16, and to him Gideon built an altar, which is peculiar to God, v. 24. So chap. 2:1, Gen. 18:17 and 48:16. Thus Christ of old used to take upon him the shape of a man to typify his incarnation. And here appearing unto Gideon as a man, he also resembles a man in his postures and actions: For as a weary traveler he comes with his staff in his hand, and reposes himself under a tree to rest him.

*{in Ophrah}* There were two Ophrahs, one in Benjamin, Josh. 18:23, and this in Manasseh.

*{Joash the Abi-ezrite}* i.e. Of the family of Abiezer, and of the tribe of Manasseh, Josh. 17:2. See v. 24, 34.

*{threshed wheat by the winepress}* Which was under or near unto the oak where the angel sat. Here appears the simplicity and humility of those times, in that men of special note, such as Gideon was, being the son of a chief magistrate, and having many servants at his command, v. 27, thought it no disparagement to work with their own hands, and employ themselves in honest labors.

*{to hide it from the Midianites}* Heb. *To cause it to flee*, to wit, out of the Midianites’ sight, that they might not find it. To which end he made choice of this secret place, and there did not beat out the corn from the ears in the ordinary manner, by using oxen to tread it out, which would have been more subject to sight, but he beat it out with a staff, as being the more secret way whereby he might conceal it.

**V. 12.*** {The Lord is with thee}* An ancient form of salutation, whereby is signified and wished God’s presence to assist us in all our labors and endeavors, and to protect us from all evil and danger. So Boaz saluted his reapers, Ruth 2:4, and the angel the blessed virgin, Luke 1:28. The angel here used it to encourage Gideon to undertake with confidence this great and difficult work which he was now to persuade him unto.

*{thou mighty man of valour}* This is spoken not so much in respect of that fortitude that was naturally in him, as of that courage and valor wherewith the Lord would now endow him, that he might fit him for so great a work.

**V. 13.*** {if the Lord be with us, why then is all this befallen us?}* i.e. Why has he exposed us to all these miseries, and forsaken us in them? Which is the usual complaint of God’s dearest children in their extreme afflictions, and the cause of all their expostulations, Psal. 13:1 and 22:1 and 73:2–14 and 77:2–9, Jer. 12:1, 2.

*{and where be all his miracles?}* q.d. We have heard much speech of the wonders he formerly did in the deliverances of our forefathers; but ourselves in this our miserable condition can have no experience of them, or reason to hope that he remains the same to us that he was to them.

**V. 14.*** {And the Lord looked upon him}* i.e. Not in an ordinary manner, but virtually and efficaciously, as he did upon Peter, Luke 22:61, by his Spirit speaking powerfully to his heart the same things which he uttered to his ear.

*{Go in this thy might}* Which I now give you, and with it my promise to assist you in this great work which you are to undertake at my command, namely the deliverance of my people out of the hands of the Midianites.

*{have not I sent thee?}* i.e. I have assuredly called you to this work, therefore pretend no difficulties or discouragements, seeing I am all-sufficient to give you such wisdom and power as may enable you to effect it. See on ch. 4:6.

**V. 15.*** {O my lord, wherewith shall I save Israel?}* These words proceeded not from a total want of faith, but from weakness of it; for Gideon is one of those great worthies whose faith is commended, Heb. 11:32. Nor spoke he thus merely out of distrust in God, as though he would not perform what he had promised, but as a man apprehensive of his own weakness, he desired direction how to carry on the work, and what means he should use for the achieving of it, and the further strengthening of his faith in the assurance of good success. So Luke 1:34, Judg. 13:12.

*{behold, my family is poor}* Heb. *My thousand is the meanest*. i.e. As I am weak in myself, so also in my friends and allies. See Exod. 18:25, Mic. 5:2, 1 Sam. 9:21.

**V. 16.*** {thou shalt smite the Midianites as one man}* i.e. As easily as if you had to do but with one man, and not with a great army.

**V. 17.*** {then shew me a sign that thou talkest with me}* To wit, that you are sent of God thus to speak unto me, whereby I may be the better enabled to undertake this dangerous expedition with good conscience, as being called unto it by God, and with resolution and courage, being assured of good success by his assistance.

**V. 18.*** {and bring forth my present}* Or, *meat offering*. And the word may signify both, and so is diversely taken in divers places; but it cannot here be understood in the latter sense, as appears by the things here presented, namely, a boiled kid, and the broth in which it was boiled, fit food for a weary traveler, but not to be used in meat offerings; besides, here is no mention made of the fat, flour or oil required unto them, Lev. 6:12, 14. For Gideon thinking this person to be only a man, he desires to give him such entertainment as was fit for a traveler, namely, by setting meat before him, as Abraham, Lot, and Manoah in like case did, chap. 13:15, Gen. 18:5–8 and 19:3. See on 1 Sam. 9:7.

**V. 19.*** {presented it}* As a dinner prepared for his repast, and not as a sacrifice, seeing here is no mention of divers things necessarily required to a sacrifice.

**V. 20.*** {pour out the broth}* To wit, upon the flesh and cakes. This he required, that the miracle which he intended might appear to be the greater: For the more the flesh and cakes were moistened with the broth, the less apt they were to be consumed with the fire. So Elijah, 1 Kings 18:33, 34.

**V. 21.*** {there rose up fire out of the rock, and consumed the flesh}* So that which Gideon intended for a treat was by the angel miraculously turned into a kind of burnt offering, that thereby Gideon’s faith might be confirmed in this assurance, that the service unto which God called him should be accepted and have good success. See 1 Kings 18:38, 2 Chron. 7:1.

**V. 22.*** {And when Gideon perceived that he was an Angel}* i.e. By his fetching fire out of the rock to consume the things laid upon it, and his sudden vanishing out of sight.

*{Gideon said, Alas, O Lord God}* The causes of such like affrightments when God gave visible signs of his presence, were partly the guilt of sin and man’s corruption, Isa. 6:5, partly God’s purity and glorious majesty, which makes the angels themselves to cover their faces, Job 15:15, 16, Isa. 6:2, and partly God’s speech to Moses, Exod. 33:20. *There shall no man see me, and live*. For these reasons Gideon here, and Manoah, ch. 13:22, were so affrighted because they had seen the Lord, that they cried out through weakness of faith, as though they should have presently died, and thought it a wonder that having seen the face of God, their lives were preserved. But howsoever in this state of mortality man cannot live and see God in his glory, majesty and infinite perfections, yet he may see him in the face of a mediator, the angel of the covenant, Jesus Christ, by whom we have access unto God, and are accepted of him, Rom. 5:2, Eph. 1:6 and 2:18 and 3:12. And thus the saints of old saw God, and yet lived. See Gen. 16:13 and 32:30, Exod. 24:11, Deut. 5:24.

**V. 23.*** {And the Lord said unto him}* Namely, the same night after he was thus affrighted, v. 25. Some conceive he thus spoke as he was in departing from him.

*{Peace be unto thee}* i.e. Life, health, and all happiness; all which the Hebrews usually express by this phrase.

**V. 24.*** {then Gideon built an altar there}* Namely, upon the top of the rock, by God’s special command, v. 26. Of the lawfulness of building extraordinary altars upon extraordinary occasions, see on ch. 13:16 and 21:4.

*{and called it Jehovah-Shalom}* i.e. *The Lord send peace*. This altar Gideon built, and dedicated it to Christ himself, the angel of the covenant that had appeared unto him, which he called Jehovah-shalom, I. Because he is our peace, and Prince of peace, Isa. 9:6, and the author and giver of all our peace, Isa. 53:5, Luke 2:14, Heb. 7:2, John 14:27 and 16:33, Acts 10:36. II. Because he had peaceably saluted him in his affrightment and fear of death, and comforted him by promising that he should live, ver. 23. III. Because he had assured him of his deliverance and peace to him and his people by their victory over the Midianites. Now what is here said is said by anticipation. For Gideon built not this altar before he received a command from God for it, the night following the apparition, v. 26, when God gave him divers other directions for the whole carriage of the ensuing business. And this is usual in the Scripture, first to set down a thing in the gross, and then afterwards the manner how it was to be done. Such like titles as this here, see, Exod. 17:15, Jer. 33:16, Ezek. 48:35, then afterwards the manner how it was to be done.

**V. 25.*** {Take thy father’s young bullock, even the second bullock}* Or, *and the second bullock*. And if so, then two were commanded to be sacrificed. But v. 28 mention is made but of one; therefore the first reading is thought to be better. Now it is not apparent why he was called the second; only men diversely guess at it; as, because either he stood usually in the second place in the stall, or at the plow or cart; or, because he was second in age, or in price or worth; or second of those which were prepared and fatted to be sacrificed to Baal. And that this bullock was destinated, not for Joash his private service, but for the public use in the people’s idolatrous worship, seems the more probable, in that they complain of it as a sacrilegious act committed by Gideon, because having pulled down Baal’s altar, he had sacrificed this bullock on the altar which himself had built, v. 28, 30.

*{of seven years old}* Bred the same year in which the Midianites began their tyranny; and therefore, it may be, he was chosen the rather by the Lord for a sacrifice, to signify that now this tyranny should have an end.

*{and throw down the altar of Baal that thy father hath}* i.e. Standing in his ground, as in an eminent place, he being a chief magistrate.

*{and cut down the grove that is by it}* Dedicated to idolatry. Begin your calling with the rooting out of idolatry, and restoring of God’s true worship; that so you may go on against the Midianites with confidence, courage, and hope of victory, when you have begun a reformation in religion, and suppressed idolatry and superstition amongst your own people, which were the causes that moved God to give them up to their tyranny.

**V. 26.*** {And build an altar unto the Lord thy God}* First, idolatry must be abolished before God’s true worship could be set up, because they cannot stand together, seeing there is no concord between Christ and Belial, between the temple of God and idols, 2 Cor. 6:15, 16. If we will serve God, the service of Baal must be first rejected, 1 Kings 18:21.

*{upon the top of this rock}* On which God had showed that great miracle in causing fire to ascend out of it, v. 20, 21, thereby consecrating it to himself in a special manner for this service, i.e. To honor and praise him for delivering Gideon from that fright in which he was v. 22, and for promising his people deliverance from the Midianites, v. 16, and also to pray unto him to make good his promise, and to speed and perfect this great work.

*{in the ordered place}* Or, *in an orderly manner*. i.e. Either, in the place of the rock which was before appointed for the flesh, cakes, and broth to be laid upon, as fittest for this service; or, he was to build it as was commanded by the law, Exod. 20:24, 25, of earth or unhewn stones fitted for the sacrifice *in an orderly manner*, by laying the wood upon it, and the sacrifice upon the wood, that it might be burned thereon.

*{and offer a burnt sacrifice}* This office was proper to the priests; yet in a case extraordinary, Gideon, who was no priest, did lawfully execute it, having God’s special command for the same.

*{with the wood of the grove}* To wit, which grew near Baal’s altar. The patriarchs of old, and the heathens in imitation of them, had high places and shady groves, both for privacy, that they might not be interrupted by any worldly distractions, and also to make them more intent in their devotions, being affected with a kind of awful fear by that shady solitariness.

**V. 27.*** {Then Gideon took ten men of his servants}* i.e. He executed God’s command in a prudential manner, taking so many of his servants for the quicker dispatch, because it was to be done in that one night before the morning, making choice of such as were (like himself) affected with a detestation of the idolatry of those times. And though it seems he was a man of more than ordinary rank, seeing he had so many servants to attend his command, yet because both the city and also his father’s household were much corrupted with idolatry, in a provident fear and care to avoid danger and tumults, he does secretly in the night what God had commanded him, that he might not be hindered while he was doing it. For he did not act this work in the night that it might not be known who had done it, seeing that he could not possibly be long concealed which had so many hands in the acting of it.

**V. 30.*** {Then the men of the City said unto Joash, Bring out thy son, that he may die}* With a joint vote they complained to Joash, as being their magistrate, of his son, and would by no other means be satisfied than by his death, because the injury he had done concerned the public state, the idol of Baal, the altar, the grove, and the bullock belonging in common to the whole people and city, howsoever they were in the custody of Joash, as being chief amongst them. Nothing can seem unreasonable to men transported with an idolatrous rage, as we see in the example of the Ephesians all in a confused uproar about their goddess Diana, Acts 19:28, &c. and in these men here, who in their blind zeal to Baal, would have the life of Gideon before they would hear what he could say for himself, and required that the father should give up his own son to their rage and violence; having herein no more respect to Joash, though a magistrate or chief man amongst them than if he had been of ordinary and common rank.

**V. 31.*** {And Joash said—Will ye plead for Baal}* Some think he only prudently endeavored to stop them in this uproar and commotion (as the town clerk the Ephesians, Acts 19) by telling them that if there were a fault committed, it did not belong unto them to punish the offenders, being private men, but to the magistrates who were to judge of it.

*{he that will plead for him}* Namely, in this tumultuous way which disturbs the common peace.

*{let him be put to death}* Joash himself had been a worshiper of Baal, but now it seems his mind was changed by hearing from his son (as may be probably thought) that God had appeared unto him in that wonderful manner before related, and had commanded him to do all that which he had done: Or else his natural affection exceeding his idolatrous zeal makes him thus to plead for his son to preserve his life.

*{whilst it is yet morning}* i.e. Presently and without any further delay.

*{if he be a God, let him plead for himself}* i.e. If he has such a divine power as you imagine, he is able to maintain his own cause, and to right and revenge the wrongs that are offered him; but if not, then he is not worthy to be defended by you, who is unable to defend either you or himself.

**V. 32.*** {Therefore on that day he called him Jerubbaal}* i.e. Joash called his son Gideon by the name of Jerubbaal. For Jerubbaal signifies, *Let Baal plead for himself*. He is called Jerubbesheth, 2 Sam. 11:21.

**V. 33.*** {and went over}* To wit, Jordan. For they came out of the east to invade Canaan. See ch. 7:24.

*{and pitched in the valley of Jezreel}* There was two of this name; one in Judah, Josh. 15:56, and this upon the borders of Issachar and Manasseh, Josh. 17:16 and 19:18, on the other side of mount Gilboa, and ending at the city of Jezreel westward, where the kings of Israel had a royal palace, 1 Kings 21:1.

**V. 34.*** {But the Spirit of the Lord came upon Gideon}* And as it were clothed him with wisdom, zeal, and fortitude, and so fitted him for this work. So 1 Chron. 12:18, 2 Chron. 24:20, Luke 24:49.

*{Abiezer}* i.e. The Abiezrites, which were the posterity of Abiezer; of which family Gideon himself was: In which respect they were the more ready to follow him, being of his kindred and allies, and now convinced that God had called him to this great work, though formerly they were so zealous for Baal, that Gideon feared to let them know his intention to destroy his altar, lest they should hinder him, v. 27.

**V. 35.*** {And he sent messengers throughout all Manasseh}* The one half dwelling beyond Jordan, and the other in Canaan.

*{unto Asher, and unto Zebulun, and unto Naphtali}* Which three tribes lay together, and were nearest to him.

*{and they came up to meet him}* To wit, Gideon with the Abiezrites and Manassites.

**V. 36.*** {And Gideon said unto God}* i.e. By fervent prayer desired him to give him another sign. Unto which God gave him no verbal answer, but hearkened to his suit in a real and effectual manner.

*{If thou wilt save Israel by my hand}* He desires not more signs out of infidelity to tempt God, but being sensible of his own weakness, in all humility he desires these helps for the further confirmation of his faith concerning his calling from God to this present work, and the good issue and success of it. For though the apostle commends him for his faith, Heb. 11:32, yet he did not attain to the height and strength of it all at once, but by degrees, as he was more and more confirmed by God, and in the meantime he needed all these helps to support him in all his weakness and wants, and to strengthen him against all his diffidence and doubtings. Some think he required these last signs, not so much for himself, as for those who followed him in this expedition: And if we say, that it was both for them and himself also, I think we come nearest to the truth.

**V. 37.*** {and it be dry upon all the earth beside}* i.e. On all the floor round about the fleece, no dew falling upon it, but on the fleece only. By the dew, which at first fell upon the fleece only, and after upon all the ground, leaving the fleece alone dry, Ambrose, Augustine, and divers others hold, that the grace and favor and word of God was shadowed out and signified, which at first was vouchsafed to the Jews alone, but afterward was extended to all other nations, and the Jews only for their ingratitude and impenitence rejected and excluded.

**V. 38.*** {for he rose up early on the morrow}* i.e. As one longing to see a return of his prayers, and also because he might have a more sure trial of his experiment, by coming to the place where the fleece was spread before the sun was risen, which otherwise might have drawn up this morning dew, Hos. 6:4.

*{and wringed the dew out of the fleece, a bowl full of water}* Which quantity made the sign more wonderful, seeing in a natural course so much dew could not fall in the small compass of a fleece of wool.

**V. 39.*** {Let not thine anger, &c.}* See Gen. 18:32.

## CHAPTER 7

**Verse 1.*** {And pitched beside the well of Harod}* i.e. The well of terror; so called from the fear which seized either on the two and twenty thousand Israelites, v. 3, or on the Midianites, v. 21, or upon king Saul, 1 Sam. 28:5 and 29:1. This well lay upon the borders of Manasseh.

*{by the hill of Moreh}* i.e. One of the hills of Gilboa, so called from the seasonable rains which used to fall there, 2 Sam. 1:21, or, as some think, from the archers that wounded Saul, 1 Sam. 31:3.

*{in the valley}* To wit, of Jezreel, ch. 6:33.

**V. 2.*** {The people that are with thee are too many}* The whole army of the Israelites consisted but of two and thirty thousand, and the Midianites were at least an hundred thirty and five thousand; yet though they were above four to one, the Lord says the Israelites were too many for him to give the victory unto them; and the same is said when the number was abated to ten thousand, ver. 4, and the reason here is given, Because otherwise the Israelites would have boasted that they had gotten the victory by their own hand, i.e. Their own valor and strength, and so have robbed God of the glory which was due to him alone. So prone men are upon any good success to glory in themselves, rather than in the Lord from whom they have it.

**V. 3.*** {Whosoever is fearful and afraid, let him return}* According to the law, Deut. 20:8, lest by the example of the cowardly and fearful those who were in their own disposition valiant might grow fainthearted; as is usual in wars and battles.

*{and depart early}* i.e. Betimes and with all speed, as the word signifies; that so they might not be discovered by the Midianites, and endangered by their pursuit, nor the weakness of the army appear which still remained with Gideon.

*{from mount Gilead}* Or, as the word may signify, *towards mount Gilead*, beyond Jordan, whence the half-tribe of Manasseh came, chap. 6:35, and which did lie over against Gideon’s camp. Others think there were two mounts of this name, one beyond Jordan, frequently mentioned in Scripture, the other in the tribe of Manasseh within Jordan, near the valley of Jezreel, where the Midianites now lay, this (if there were any such Gilead) is likely to be here meant, seeing Israelites of all sorts and tribes might depart from this place, where they were now encamped all together; but it is not so probable that all these two and twenty thousand departed to Gilead beyond Jordan, seeing the most of this number were of those tribes that dwelled in Canaan. Of Gilead see on Obad. 19.

*{and there returned of the people twenty and two thousand}* Who though before they were willing to fight for their liberty and deliverance from their grievous oppressions, yet now seeing the mighty army of the Midianites, and the paucity and weakness of their own party, were grown fearful and fainthearted.

**V. 4.*** {And the Lord said unto Gideon}* i.e. Either by vision, or apparition of an angel in human shape.

*{bring them down unto the water}* i.e. Either some water running at the bottom of the hill, or to the well Harod mentioned (as some think) for this cause v. 1.

*{and I will try them}* Or, *purge or purify them*, as metals are tried by the fire and separated from their dross. i.e. I will give you a sign whereby you may try them, that those fearful and fainthearted may be severed from those valiants whom I have fitted for this service.

**V. 5.*** {Every one that lappeth of the water with his tongue, as dog lappeth}* i.e. Who bending his body a little, takes up water in the hollow of his hand, and so putting his mouth to it, catches or sucks it up in some small quantity, as a dog laps, he shall be set apart to go with you, v. 6.

*{likewise every one that boweth down upon his knees to drink}* i.e. Who first falls down upon his knees, and then prostrating himself flat, puts his mouth into the water, and so drinks as an ox or sheep, even to his fill, he shall be of those that must depart, as unfit for this service. Now if this were not only a mere sign which God ordered at his pleasure to sever these men, but had some reality and reason in itself in respect of men’s different dispositions, then we may guess it to be this, That the falling flat to drink, and putting the mouth to the water argued either faintness and weariness, or sloth and greediness of appetite, which could not leave till it had drunk its fill; the other, more temperance, being contented with a little, and more strength, courage and eagerness in prosecuting their intentions and designs, upon which they presently fall again as soon as they have a little relieved their present necessity. See Psal. 110:7. Some gather hence allegorical observations, That those only are fit to follow the Lord and to fight under his colors, who out of zeal to his service, like Gideon’s three hundred soldiers, curb their sensual appetite, and (as the dogs about Nile) settle not themselves to drink a full draught of the pleasures and profits of this life, but only lap and taste a little thereof as they pass along, rather out of mere necessity than any great delight, still using this world as if they used it not, 1 Cor. 7:31. Again, That Christ’s flock is but a little one, Luke 12:32. That *many are called, but few chosen*, Matth. 20:16 and 22:14. That few shall be saved, Isa. 26:13, Jer. 3:14, Matth. 7:14, Luke 13:24 and 18:8.

**V. 8.*** {So the people}* i.e. The three hundred chosen men.

*{took victuals}* i.e. So much as was needful for such a number and such an expedition.

*{and their trumpets}* i.e. So many as that every man might have one, i.e. Three hundred in all; some of which they had of their own, and the rest from them that were dismissed.

*{he sent}* i.e. Gideon.

*{in the valley}* Of Jezreel, on the other side of the hill.

**V. 9.*** {I have delivered it into thy hand}* i.e. You must be as certain of victory as if already you had obtained it.

**V. 10.*** {But if thou fear to go down}* i.e. If you fear to go down to fight against the Midianites, in regard your number is so small and they so many and mighty, then first go down privily unto the host, where you shall hear something which shall confirm your faith, and strengthen your hands and heart for the undertaking of the enterprise. Where the Lord knowing our human frailty and faintheartedness, of his own free largess, without Gideon’s suit, offers unto him another help for the strengthening of his faith.

*{go thou with Phurah thy servant}* i.e. If you are afraid to go alone for fear of the scouts and watch, take your servant with you, who though not able to protect you, yet may be some comfort by bearing you company.

**V. 11.*** {thine hands shall be strengthened}* i.e. You shall further be encouraged to fight against them.

*{unto the outside of the armed men}* Or, *ranks by five*. i.e. To the first sentinels of the host.

**V. 12.*** {like grasshoppers}* See on ch. 6:5.

*{and their Camels}* These nations abounded with camels, especially the Midianites, Isa. 60:6. And they brought these camels now with them, that they might feed and fat them in Canaan and make use of them both while they were there for carrying of burdens, especially their tents, from place to place, and also for carrying away the spoils of the Israelites, when they should leave Canaan and return back into their own country.

*{as the sand by the Sea side for multitude}* An hyperbolical expression, usual with the Hebrews. See on Isa. 48:19.

**V. 13.*** {and lo, a cake of barley bread tumbled into the host}* The analogy and similitude observable in this dream stands thus; That as it were a great miracle if so poor a thing as a barley loaf rolled down upon a well-pitched tent, should overturn and ruin it, so it was no less wonderful that so contemptible a man as Gideon, held lately under such vassalage that he dared not show his head, with a small handful of despised fugitives gathered together, should rout and ruin such a huge army as that of the Midianites; yet as he saw the one done in his dream, so the other represented by it should as surely come to pass.

**V. 14.*** {This is nothing else}* Divine dreams are always either clear and evident of themselves; or else opportunity interprised [sic.] for the benefit of God’s people.

**V. 15.*** {when Gideon heard—he worshipped}* i.e. He praised God for confirming him thus in his calling, and prayed unto him for prosperous success according to his omen and good sign.

**V. 16.*** {he divided the three hundred men into three companies}* That they might surround the camp in several places, as if they were environed with a great army.

*{And he put a trumpet in every man’s hand}* As though every one had a whole troop or regiment accompanying or following him.

*{with empty pitchers, and lamps within the pitchers}* Namely, to keep them from being blown out by the wind and weather, and from being seen of the enemy till they had occasion to discover them.

**V. 17.*** {Look on me, and do likewise}* i.e. Expect not any verbal directions, which cannot be given on a sudden all at once, and in such distance as we shall be one from another: but let my example serve for your directions.

**V. 18.*** {and say, The sword of the Lord, and of Gideon}* i.e. Of the Lord, as principal agent; and of Gideon, as his instrument: Where he ascribes the whole glory of the victory unto the Lord, but withal would have his own name used, because he perceived by the interpretation of the dream that his very name was grown terrible to the enemy. See on Exod. 7:17.

**V. 19.*** {in the beginning of the middle watch}* i.e. At midnight, when the watches used to be changed, And of this time he made choice, because then the army were soundest asleep, and therefore most subject to fear and amazement, when as they thus were suddenly awaked. See on Luke 12:38.

**V. 20.*** {And the three companies blew the trumpets}* Thereby making show that a great army was come to surprise them.

*{and brake the pitchers}* That they might terrify their enemies by that sudden noise of breaking and clashing.

*{and held the lamps in their left hands}* That they might affright them with those many lights suddenly appearing, seeing they might well think there was a great army come upon them, that needed the guidance of so many lights, hearing withal that loud cry sounding in their ears, *The sword of the Lord, and of Gideon*.

**V. 21.*** {And they stood every man in his place}* To show that the Lord only fought the battle and got the victory, as Exod. 14:13, 2 Chron. 27:17. And withal, to make the Midianites think they stood there to give light to that army which came with them to enter upon and surprise their camp.

**V. 22.*** {and the Lord set every man’s sword against his fellow}* i.e. The Lord struck them with such sudden terror and amazement, being frighted out of their dead sleep, that they knew not what they did, nor could discern friends from enemies, but mistaking the one for the other slew their friends instead of their foes. The like to this is related, 1 Sam. 14:15, 20, 2 Chron. 20:23.

*{and the host fled to Beth-shittah}* i.e. Out of the plain where they lay encamped towards Jordan, near unto which, on Canaan side, this city and the rest here named were situate. Of which see on 1 Kings 4:12 and 19:16.

**V. 23.*** {And the men of Israel gathered themselves together}* i.e. Hearing of this defeat and routing of the Midianitish army, they came presently in to assist Gideon in the pursuit; as they easily might, being so lately disbanded, and as yet haply but in their return to their own country, or at least but newly come home, and therefore still armed and in readiness to fight.

**V. 24.*** {And Gideon sent messengers throughout all mount Ephraim}* Not only to have their assistance for the completing of the victory, but also out of his humility and modesty, that they might share with him in the honor of the day, not being willing to engross the glory of the victory to himself alone.

*{take before them the waters unto Beth-barah}* Which lying near unto Jordan, as some conceive, but short of it, the Midianites must needs pass before they came unto it. And here it may be Oreb and Zeeb were taken in their flight, and many others with them. Others think that here are meant the fords and passages of Jordan even to Beth-barah: Unto which I rather incline, because we do not read of any such great rivers near Jordan as could hinder their passage. Some think this Beth-barah to be that Bethabara mentioned, John 1:28. But that seems to be on this side Jordan, and this is said to be beyond it. See on Josh. 3:16.

**V. 25.*** {And they took two Princes of the Midianites, Oreb and Zeeb}* Psal. 83:11, Isa. 10:26. These names show the nature and disposition of these tyrants, the one signifying a waster or destroyer; the other a wolf.

*{slew Oreb upon the rock Oreb, and Zeeb they slew at the winepress of Zeeb}* Places so called in aftertimes upon this occasion, and lying at the east end of mount Ephraim near Jordan.

*{and brought the heads of Oreb and Zeeb to Gideon}* It must not be understood that they brought them unto him presently as soon as they had slain them, but afterwards, when Gideon was passed over Jordan to pursue the remainder of the Midianites that were fled over with Zebah and Zalmunna, ch. 8:4. But it seems to me, that here and in the beginning of the next chapter to verse 4 there is some transposition in the order of the story as it is here expressed; which was done it may be to this end, that the acts of the Ephraimites might be continued without any interruption.

Some do wittily and profitably allegorize on this victory, as typifying Christ’s conquest over sin and Satan, with all other our spiritual enemies: For which they have good warrant, seeing the prophet himself, prophesying of Christ’s victory over them, does elegantly and largely allude unto it, Isa. 9:1–6. In both these victories there are divers resemblances one with another. The Midianites, in respect of their multitude and malice, resemble the many and malicious enemies of our salvation; Christ, like Gideon, overcoming and triumphing over them; the broken pitchers resemble the breaking of his body with the sorrows of death, and his crucifying on the cross, Isa. 53:5, Heb. 2:14. The shining of the lamps when the pitchers were broken, the glory of his Godhead gloriously shining forth, after he was broken by death, in his resurrection, Rom. 1:4. Now the benefit of this victory by Christ’s death and resurrection is conveyed unto us by the light of the Gospel, which is hid in pitchers or earthen vessels, 2 Cor. 4:7. And the ministers of the Word, lifting up their voices like trumpets, proclaim this war against our spiritual enemies, and put into our hands *the sword of the Spirit, the word of God*, whereby we put all our enemies to flight and obtain the victory, Eph. 6:17, 2 Cor. 10:4. Their crying out, *The sword of the Lord*, resembles our fervent prayers unto God, by whose strength alone we do prevail, and their standing still in their places, our waiting and expecting salvation from God, acknowledging that we have no strength in ourselves to resist and overcome so many and mighty enemies, but that all our might and hope of prevailing is from the Lord, that he alone may have all the glory of his own work, according to that, 2 Cor. 4:7. *But we have this treasure in earthen vessels, that the excellency of the power may be of God, and not of us*.

## CHAPTER 8

**Verse 1.*** {And the men of Ephraim said unto him}* i.e. When they brought the heads of Oreb and Zeeb unto Gideon on the other side Jordan; and some think, before he had vanquished and slain Zebah and Zalmunna with their army, because he does not mention that victory v. 2, 3, which would have made him equal with the Ephraimites in the number of kings slain, who was already much superior to them by his routing the whole host, and so exposed him to greater envy. But if they had come unto him beyond Jordan, and their anger were appeased by his humble speech, it is more than probable they should have accompanied him in his second expedition (which they did not), seeing this quarrel was, because they were not called to assist him in the former.

*{Why hast thou served us thus?}* Heb. *What thing is this that thou hast done unto us?* They were not contented with Gideon’s victory, in which they had equal benefit with the rest of their brethren, being delivered thereby from the tyranny of the Midianites, because they were not sharers with him in the glory of it. This their pride and ambition did arise partly from the greatness and power of their tribe, and partly from that priority and precedency that they had above Manasseh by Jacob’s blessing, and also in their encamping, Num. 2:18, 20, where Manasseh is marshaled under the standard of Ephraim. Moreover, it is probable they assumed no small honor to themselves, for having had Joshua the famous general of the Israelites, descended from their tribe, and for having the tabernacle and ark seated at Shiloh, in their inheritance; and no doubt they were now extraordinarily puffed up with the good success they had in stopping and slaying the Midianites with their two kings when they sought a passage over Jordan. And this made them emulate the tribe of Manasseh, of which Gideon was, as unworthy to be preferred before them, by having the chief honor of that expedition. And thus afterwards they proudly quarreled with Jephthah upon the same ground, ch. 12. See the like, 1 Sam. 19:41.

**V. 2.*** {What have I now done in comparison of you?}* He appeases their wrath with an humble and meek answer. Prov. 15:1, 18, 23 and 25:15.

*{Is not the gleaning of the grapes of Ephraim}* i.e. Is not your rich gleaning to be better esteemed than our poor vintage? In which allegory he compares the fight and the success of it to a vintage; the victory himself had gained by the assistance of the Abiezrites, his allies and friends, to the first gathering of the grapes, at least in the Ephraimites’ esteem; and the Ephraimites’ slaughtering of the Midianites in their flight, to the gleanings after the vintage: and he prefers this before the other, both because it was acted by their own valor, whereas he and his friends were only lookers on while the enemies massacred and killed one another; and because also the Ephraimites had taken their two kings and slain them which had escaped out of his hands. And withal, by the very naming of the actors in this expedition, Ephraim and Abiezer, he seems to magnify and extol them, as a great and famous tribe, and to abase and vilify his own party, as springing out of the poor family of Abiezer, ch. 6:15.

**V. 4.*** {yet pursuing them}* i.e. Zebah and Zalmunna, with fifteen thousand of their army, that were fled, and had gotten over Jordan towards their own country.

**V. 5.*** {And he said unto the men of Succoth}* Not that Succoth in the land of Egypt, Exod. 12:37, Num. 33:5, but a city in the tribe of Gad, Josh. 13:27. Situate at the river Jabbok near Jordan, so called because Jacob coming homewards from Laban built his booths there, Gen. 33:17.

*{for they be faint}* He enforces his request by two strong arguments; I. Because he and his followers were faint and weary, and therefore it would be a work of mercy to relieve them, though they had been strangers and not brethren, in such distress: II. Because they were in pursuit of the common enemy; and therefore it would be a work of justice to strengthen their hands in such a design. See on Gen. 14:18.

**V. 6.*** {And the princes of Succoth said}* i.e. They refused to relieve them partly because they saw such weakness and weariness in Gideon’s few men, and such number and strength in the army of their enemies; and, partly because they feared the Midianites would, after they had recruited their army, return again, and revenge themselves upon them for relieving their enemies.

*{Are the hands of Zebah and Zalmunna now in thy hands}* q.d. Are they already in your power as your prisoners, that we should respect you as victor, and not rather fear their revenge? Thus they incense Gideon by rejecting his request with contemptuous scorn. See the like, 1 Sam. 25:10.

**V. 7.*** {I will tear}* Heb. *Thresh*. i.e. I will revenge this foul scorn and proud contempt which you have upon me with extreme, yet just, severity, seeing you refuse to relieve my faint and wearied followers fighting in a common cause against God’s enemies, and herein grievously sin against God, the commonwealth, myself, and all my followers your brethren; and do all this in a proud, scornful and contemptuous manner against us, and in favor of our tyrannous enemies.

*{your flesh with the thorns of the wilderness}* i.e. Lying between Succoth and Penuel, at the brook Jabbok, Gen. 32:2, 22, 30, is called also the wilderness of Mahanaim, as lying near unto it, 2 Sam. 17:27, 29. This wilderness abounded with thorns and briers. This threatening Gideon denounced against them, being as confident through God’s assistance of prevailing against his enemies as if already he had obtained the victory.

**V. 8.*** {And he went up thence to Penuel}* A city also in the tribe of Gad, near the brook Jabbok, so called by Jacob, because there he saw God and wrestled with him, Gen. 32:30, 1 Kings 12:25.

*{and the men of Penuel answered him as the men of Succoth}* i.e. Denied his request with like scorn and contempt: And this they the rather did, presuming on the strength of their tower, which caused Gideon to threaten the pulling of it down, when he returned with victory.

**V. 9.*** {when I come again in peace}* i.e. Safe and victorious.

**V. 10.*** {in Karkor}* A city beyond Jordan towards the east.

*{an hundred and twenty thousand men}* To wit, those which were slain before.

*{that drew sword}* i.e. Able men for service in war. So chap. 20:15, 17, 25, 46, 2 Sam. 24:9, 2 Kings 3:26.

**V. 11.*** {of them that dwelt in tents}* i.e. The Arabians, who were called by the Grecians *Scenitae*, that is, tent dwellers. See Jer. 49:29.

*{Nobah, and Jogbehah}* See Numb. 32:25, 42.

*{for the host was secure}* Not seeing themselves pursued by the Israelites, not fearing it, seeing they were now come so far on the way towards their own country.

**V. 12.*** {and discomfited}* Heb. *Terrified*, i.e. Coming upon them on the sudden, they were frighted, routed, put to flight, and so slaughtered and utterly ruined.

**V. 13.*** {Before the Sun was up}* Whereby is implied that he came upon them in the night, as he had done before, ch. 7:19, and so being awakened out of their dead sleep, they were frighted and amazed, so as they could not stand in their own defense: Which could not have so succeeded, if he had attempted them in the day: for the light would have discovered the weakness of Gideon’s army and their own strength.

**V. 14.*** {and he described unto him the princes of Succoth}* Heb. *Writ*. i.e. He gave to Gideon their names in writing who were chiefly guilty. For in so weighty a matter he would not trust his memory, lest he might haply punish the innocent with the guilty.

**V. 15.*** {Behold Zebah and Zalmunna}* q.d. I have purposely reserved them alive, and here brought them unto you, to convince you of your confidence in the arm of flesh, and your diffidence in God, as though he had not been able to have delivered them into my hand in respect of their strength and my weakness.

**V. 16.*** {And he took the Elders of the city, and thorns}* Hereby is meant that he tortured them to death with these thorns and briers, by causing them to be cast naked, and so trampled upon, or dragged them amongst them, or else to be scourged with them till they died. For being equal in guilt with the men of Penuel, their punishment was alike capital with theirs.

*{with them he taught the men of Succoth}* Or, *he threshed*; or, *he made to know*. i.e. By this severe punishment he made these princes and elders an example to all the rest, and so taught them more wit or experimental knowledge for the time to come, than to commit again the like sins which were so heinous and offensive against God and their brethren.

*{beat down the tower}* See on 1 Kings 12:25.

**V. 17.*** {and slew the men of the city}* i.e. The princes and elders, who were chiefly or only guilty; as v. 16.

**V. 18.*** {What manner of men were they whom ye slew}* When they slew these men is not plainly expressed, whether in this or in some former invasion: but it is more likely it was in this, seeing though Gideon thought his brethren were slain, yet he understood not the manner and circumstance of their death; or else hearing that the Midianites had slain divers of the Israelites in those holds and caves of mount Tabor where they had hid themselves, ch. 6:2, and missing his brethren, and not knowing whether they might not be in the number of those who were there slain, he puts these kings to describe what manner of men they were, and upon their description finds that they were his brethren.

*{at Tabor}* See on Josh. 19:12.

*{As thou art, so were they}* q.d. They were like you, and both you and they like the children of a king, goodly and majestical persons. And this they said (as it is like) to humor him and get favor.
