**V. 19.*** {if ye had saved them alive, I would not slay you}* i.e. Had they been merciful unto his brethren, where they fell into their hands it would have been lawful for Gideon to have pardoned them, not being Canaanites, though they were enemies; but now he could not, seeing by the law of God he was bound, being next of blood and also a magistrate, to punish them with death that had murdered his brethren, Numb. 35:19.

**V. 20.*** {And he said unto Jether his firstborn, up, and slay them}* This he did to hearten him to proceed against God’s enemies with all severity, and also to make him share with himself in the honor of the victory. See Josh. 10:24.

*{But the youth drew not his sword}* Not out of neglect of his father’s command, but merely out of fear, being but a youth.

**V. 21.*** {Rise thou, and fall upon us}* This they desire, I. That they might die nobly, by the hand of one like themselves, and not by a boy, in an ignominious manner. II. For a more quick dispatch, which could not be made by the weaker hand of a boy, who would have haggled them with many blows, and so put them to more torment, whereas a man of strength striking them would quickly rid them out of their pain.

*{and took away the ornaments that were on their Camels’ necks}* Or, *ornaments like the moon*. Their camels (on which in those countries men ride, as we here do on horses) being according to their dignity adorned with chains and jewels, he seized upon them as a lawful prize taken in war, and as an ensign or memorial of his victory.

**V. 22.*** {Rule thou over us}* Namely, as a king that has sovereign power, and leaves it successively to his posterity.

**V. 23.*** {And Gideon said, I will not rule over you}* i.e. I will not take upon me the place and power of a king over God’s people, nor entail it to my children after me; but the regal rights shall still, for me remain in God’s hand, till he shall be pleased to change the government, and according to his promise choose a king for you, Deut. 17:14, 15. Hereby it appears that the judges in this book were not absolute lords and kings over the people, but extraordinarily raised for their defense and deliverance when oppressed by enemies, and to govern them as God’s vicegerents (they still retaining their liberties entire) according to the prescript rule of God’s law. And this made Gideon here to reject their offer, because it trenched upon God’s royal prerogative, and there accepting him for their king would have been to reject God that he should not reign over them; as it is plain, 1 Sam. 8:6, 7, and 12:12. See on ch. 2:16 and 9:1.

**V. 24. ***{give me every man the earrings of his prey}* i.e. The golden ornaments, whether worn in the ear or on the forehead: for the Hebrew word is used for both. See Gen. 24:22, 47, and 35:4. Neither does he desire all, but that every man should give him one of those which he had gotten for his prey, as the Hebrew text has it.

*{because they were Ishmaelites}* The Midianites and Ishmaelites were so intermingled both in their habitation and also in their conversation and intercourse of dealing and trading, that their names are promiscuously used, as if they had been both but one nation. See Gen. 37:25, 28, 36. These frequently used to wear golden earrings; which is here rendered as the reason how there were so many of them taken as a prey.

**V. 26. ***{a thousand and seven hundred shekels of gold}* See Gen. 24:22 and Numb. 7:14.

**V. 27. ***{And Gideon made an Ephod thereof}* An ephod was a vestment that covered the shoulders and compassed the breast, like a short strait coat without sleeves, put utmost upon the garments to keep them close. And there were two sorts of them; one, a rich garment, peculiar to the high priest, made of blue, purple, scarlet, and twined linen, cunningly wrought and embroidered with gold, in which was a breastpiece beset with precious stones, wherein was put the Urim and Thummim whereby the high priest inquired of God, Exod. 28:4. The other was made in the like figure of fine linen, used not only by the priests, but by the Levites, and others also in solemn divine service; as we see in the example of Samuel, who was only a Levite, and of David, who was of another tribe, 1 Sam. 2:18 and 17:16, and 2 Sam. 6:14. Of the former sort was this rich ephod which Gideon caused to be made and set up.

*{and put in his City, even in Ophrah}* In which himself was born, and dwelt; namely, that it might there remain as a monument of this great victory and deliverance. Now if herein he aimed at God’s glory, the act was the more excusable; but not so, if he intended thereby to perpetuate his own fame. Howsoever it is mentioned as an aspersion cast upon the story of his former acts, whether we consider the thing done, or the event and issue of it. For I. An ephod was no fit monument to represent a victory, but rather a stone or pillar erected to this end, 1 Sam. 7:12. II. There could be no right or lawful use of such an ephod in such a place; seeing it was proper for the tabernacle and the use of the high priest. And it may be that he set it up in his own city, that there upon all occasions he might by some priest using this ephod inquire of God without going to the tabernacle, as ch. 18:5, 6, not considering that this was a privilege belonging unto the high priest alone, and peculiar to Aaron’s ephod and breastplate, in which was put the Urim and Thummim. III. Because it was a scandal given by Gideon to the people, to draw them to superstition, unto which they were naturally so much addicted. The event also showed it was a great fault and failing, in that it proved a means to draw the people to gross idolatry; and besides, in that it moved God in his just displeasure to make it a snare to Gideon and his whole family, both in respect of sin and of punishment, seeing it was the cause of their superstition and of their ruin.

*{and all Israel went thither}* i.e. After the death of Gideon they did grossly abuse it to will-worship (superstition and idolatry, either resorting unto it to inquire of it in all their doubts and difficulties concerning God’s will, or using it as the place where they offered sacrifices, at least peace and meat offerings; or being taken with the glory and beauty of it, they used to fall down before it and worship it; and in process of time growing worse and worse, they abused it to the grossest idolatry in the worship of Baal, v. 33. See Lev. 17:7 and 20:5. So was Moses his brazen serpent abused, 2 Kings 18:4.

*{a whoring after it}* See on ch. 2:17.

**V. 28. ***{so that they lifted up their heads no more}* i.e. They no more recovered such strength as to be able to invade and vex the Israelites in their land.

*{And the country was in quietness}* i.e. The inhabitants of the country enjoyed peace and prosperity, and were not disturbed and disquieted with the noise and clamors of soldiers, the sounding of trumpets and alarms of war, the clashing of arms and weapons, the shouts of the victors, and the hideous outcries of the vanquished.

*{forty years}* i.e. Unto the fortieth year, reckoning from the death of Barak, or from those former years mentioned ch. 5:31, to the death of Gideon. See on ch. 3:11.

**V. 29. ***{went and dwelt in his own house}* i.e. He reigned not, as king, v. 23, nor yet led altogether a private life, but (though somewhat retired) as a judge he maintained God’s true worship, defended the laws and liberties of the people, and did all good service to the commonwealth. V. 28 and 23. See on v. 22 and ch. 2:16.

**V. 30. ***{of his body begotten}* Heb. *going out of his thigh*: which is added to show that they were his own natural children, and not sons by adoption: see Gen. 46:26.

**V. 31. ***{And his Concubine}* i.e. His wife, though in an inferior degree, married unto him when he had another wife before, who was mistress of the family, and it seems advanced to this honor out of a mean condition, having been his maidservant; as Zilpah and Bilhah, Jacob’s concubines, had been to Laban.

*{that was in Shechem}* i.e. That was born in that city, and (as it seems) still lived in it among her kindred, even after she was Gideon’s concubine. Shechem was situate upon a hill in Ephraim near Samaria. See Gen. 12:6 and 33:12, Josh. 20:7.

*{whose name he called Abimelech}* That is, a king’s father; the usual title of the Philistine kings. This some conceive Gideon’s concubine out of pride and ambition persuaded him to give, and in the event it proved to be a sad omen and a fatal name to all his family. Here it is mentioned as an introduction to the following story of Abimelech’s usurpation and tyranny to the utter ruin of Gideon’s house.

**V. 32. ***{died in a good old age}* i.e. Lived out his time allotted unto him in the course of nature, being not cut off by an immature and unnatural death; as also his life was prosperous, accompanied with health, strength, riches, honors, and the rest of God’s blessings, which make the life truly vital and to be desired: see Gen. 15:15.

**V. 33. ***{And made Baal-berith their god}* The idol of the Shechemites (signifying the lord of the covenant, so called it may be, when making choice of him to be their idol-god they did enter into covenant with him to do him service: and so by adhering to him they broke their covenant with God, and prostituted themselves to commit spiritual whoredoms, which the Lord had so often and earnestly forbidden, and so severely punished.

**V. 34. ***{remembered not the Lord their God}* Neither his goodness, in multiplying his blessings upon them, nor his just judgments in punishing their former sins, especially their apostasy and idolatry, nor his mercies in giving them deliverance out of their oppressions, when they turned unto him by true repentance: but either polluted his pure worship by worshiping God in idols, or by joining them together with him, of which he is impatient, as not enduring any corrivals; or finally they were so besotted with idolatry, that they worshiped idols only, and forgot the only true God altogether, and wholly neglected his service.

**V. 35. ***{neither shewed they kindness to the house of Jerubbaal}* i.e. They did not requite his love with love, nor showed themselves grateful unto him according to his deserts, in seeking the good and welfare of his posterity.

## CHAPTER 9

**Verse 1. ***{And Abimelech the son of Jerubbaal went to Shechem}* In the former chapters we have seen one while the heavy hand of God upon his people, punishing them for their apostasy and idolatry, by raising up against them both foreign nations, and also the Canaanites which lived among them, by whose exactions and oppressions they were grievously afflicted; another while, his mercy and goodness towards them in sending deliverance upon their repentance. Now, after the death of Gideon, they returning to their former wickedness, God punishes them with a civil war, wherein they murder and massacre one another, imbruing their hands in the blood of their brethren, and sheathing their swords in their bowels. To this end he gives up Abimelech to be ruled with an evil spirit of ambition, whereby he thirsted after sovereignty, and regal power, which having been offered to his noble father for his well deserving of the commonwealth, had been by him modestly refused. For the attainment hereof Abimelech presently after his father’s death and funerals goes to Shechem, the city in which he was born; having no hope to compass and effect his project and design by staying in his father’s family among his many brethren, the meanest and youngest of them having a better title unto regal power than himself had: though indeed neither he nor they had any right to it, seeing the Lord had established the present government, in which he ruled himself, and kept all the royal rights in his own hand. Unto him the people were to resort in all their cases and causes of moment in which there was difficulty, and were therein to be ordered by the judgment of Urim and Thummim; and in ordinary matters, more plain and of less weight, by those judges whom when he pleased he extraordinarily raised up and furnished with wisdom and fortitude, to administer justice and judgment, to protect them from their enemies, and to give deliverance when for their sins they groaned under their oppressions. This government was to continue till the Lord himself was pleased to change it. And when he thought fit to place a king over them, he retained power in his own hand to choose one according to his own mind, and would not leave it to the people’s choice, much less permit any out of their own ambition to affect and aspire unto sovereignty.

*{unto his mother’s brethren, and communed with them}* He made choice of his mother’s kindred, allies and friends, in whom (as being strangers unto them) his own brethren, the sons of his father, had no interest, to promote his plot, and he advised with them what might be the likeliest means to further him in his design of attaining unto the kingdom.

**V. 2. ***{Speak, I pray you, in the ears of all the men of Shechem}* i.e. Seeing this business is not to be carried on by a few, but by the common vote of the whole people, communicate your reasons, by which they may be persuaded to further my suit, unto all, one as well as another.

*{Whether is better for you, either that all the sons of Jerubbaal, which are threescore and ten persons, reign over you, or that one reign over you?}* Here he in the first place secretly and cunningly implies that they were weary of the present government, and would have a king, as appeared by their offering it to Gideon his father, ch. 8:22, which though he refused to accept, yet in all reasons they ought to have their desire. Secondly, he falsely and slanderously insinuates, that the threescore and ten sons of Gideon would not let this motion fall, but would aspire unto royal sovereignty, and divide the kingdom amongst them; whereas the contrary is plainly implied in Jotham’s parable, that should it be offered unto them they would all refuse it. And thirdly, upon these false suppositions, he puts them to judge, whether it were not better to be governed by one in a free monarchy, than in such an aristocracy to be forced to serve and please so many lords, yea under such a multiplied tyranny, groan under their oppressions, and hazard the kingdom to be rent and ruined by civil wars, out of their mutual jealousies one of another, whilst they sought to preserve their own states from the intrusions of an ambitious neighbor, or were themselves ready to encroach upon them.

*{Remember also that I am your bone and your flesh}* This is spoken sometimes of a whole nation who have descended from the same grand and common parent, as Israel from Jacob, 1 Chron. 11:1, sometimes of the same city, in which oftentimes brethren and kindred co-inhabited, as I think here it is to be understood; sometimes of kindred who were of the same blood, as Laban and Jacob, Gen. 29:14, David and Amasa, 2 Sam. 19:13: and thus many of the Shechemites were of Abimelech’s flesh and bone. And this argument he uses to persuade them to make him their king, seeing they might have much honor and many privileges above others, who had a king to rule over them who was knit unto them in so near relation. Whereas whilst he seeks to wind himself into their affection for his own ends, he discovers his corruption, partiality, and unfitness to be a king, who ought to govern and execute justice and judgment equally to all men without respect of persons or relations.

**V. 3. ***{And their hearts inclined to follow Abimelech}* i.e. To choose him before any other to be their king, because he was their kinsman.

**V. 4. ***{pieces of silver}* See on Gen. 20:16.

*{out of the house of Baal-berith}* i.e. The idolatrous temple of this idol, which stood upon a high hill near Shechem. Now the treasure here mentioned was reserved there, either because it was raised out of oblations to the idol, or because that place was the city’s treasury. For in some places they did in those and after ages make their idol-temples their treasure-house, as the Romans did the temple of Saturn. Here it is observable, that the same money which was consecrated to idolatrous uses, through God’s providence became the instrument to bring upon the idolaters the punishment of their sins, by embroiling them in a civil war, which caused their utter ruin.

*{wherewith Abimelech hired vain and light persons}* i.e. A desperate, beggarly and rascally crew, sons of Belial, who had no fear of God. And none but such were fit to execute such a barbarous and bloody design and such an horrid massacre as Abimelech intended. So 2 Chron. 13:7. See on Gen. 49:4.

**V. 5. ***{slew his brethren—being threescore and ten persons}* The whole number designed to the slaughter is named, though one of them, namely Jotham, escaped. And this is usual in the Scripture. So Gen. 42:13, Numb. 14:32, 33, 1 Cor. 15:5.

*{upon the stone}* They were not killed in several places as they found them, but being attached, they brought them out publicly to be executed all in one place. Which formality shows that Abimelech caused it to be done under some pretense of justice, as if they had conspired together to commit some notorious wicked fact tending to the ruin of the whole state. See the like cruelty and ambition, 2 Kings 11:1 [...].

**V. 6. ***{And all the men of Shechem gathered together}* i.e. They and all their friends whom they could persuade to join with them out of other tribes: neither is it likely that they dared presume of themselves to make a king without the consent of many others, especially seeing it was an act expressly contrary to God’s word, he having appropriated this choice unto himself, Deut. 17:14, 15.

*{and all the house of Millo}* Hereby some understand some small city adjoining to Shechem; others, the stronghold spoken of v. 20 and 46, 49, used for the town-house, where the elders and full council met together to debate of and order affairs of state, being guarded with a garrison for their better security in those troublesome times. See 2 Sam. 5:9.

*{and made Abimelech King}* Whereby I conceive, that howsoever the Shechemites’ intention was that he should reign over all Israel, yet being but one city, though backed with many friends, they dared not attempt it at the first, but chose him only for their own king, to rule over that city and the parts lying about it. For the reason moving them to hearken to his suit reached no further, *I am your bone and your flesh*, and Jotham says that they had made him *King over the men of Shechem*, because he was their brother, v. 18. But afterwards observed that all Israel were weary of the present government, and desiring a king, were willing to accept of him, especially having thus far proceeded, and gathered daily more strength to crush and quell all that should oppose him, he usurped the title of being king over the whole nation and took upon him to reign over all the tribes; whence it is that he is said to have reigned three years over Israel, v. 22.

*{by the Plain of the Pillar}* Or, *by the oak of the pillar*, i.e. in that field near Shechem where Joshua erected the great stone under an oak; it being the custom of those times to anoint their kings in some famous noted place by reason of some tree or fountain, or stone erected for a monument, for the more solemnity of the action. See Josh. 24:26, 27.

**V. 7. ***{in the top of mount Gerizim}* See on Josh. 8:30, 33. Of the top of this mountain Jotham made choice, because it seems the Shechemites were assembled at the bottom of it to inaugurate their new-chosen king, and because speaking aloud he might from thence best be heard and make an escape if he were pursued.

*{lift up his voice, and cried, and said unto them}* By divine inspiration and the spirit of prophecy, as the event showed.

*{Hearken unto me, ye men of Shechem}* Namely, who am sent of God to convince you of your heinous sin in committing these horrible murders and massacres, that so you may repent, and avert God’s heavy judgments which hang over your heads.

*{that God may hearken unto you}* To wit, when you call and cry unto him in the time of your distress: which he will not do if you go on in your sins, shutting your ears and hardening your hearts when by his messenger he gives you warning to turn unto him by unfeigned repentance.

**V. 8. ***{The trees went forth on a time to anoint a King}* He uses a parable, that he might more effectually convince them of their folly and sin, seeing they might impartially judge of it when as it was not directly charged upon them, but shadowed under this veil. So Nathan dealt with David, 2 Sam. 12:1, and our Savior Christ often with the Jews, the priests, scribes and Pharisees. In this parable Jotham I. taxes the Israelites generally, and more especially the Shechemites, for their being weary of God’s government, and desirous to be under the rule of a king. II. He sets forth the modesty and piety of his father, who did not accept of it when offered unto him; and of himself and his brethren, who rested contented with their condition, and did never ambitiously affect or aspire unto kingly sovereignty. III. He shows the pride and ambition of Abimelech, who, though wicked and worthless, so thirsted after it, that he made way unto it through a field of blood. IV. He foretells the manifold miseries and mischiefs which both he and the Shechemites would bring upon themselves in the close and epilogue of this woeful tragedy. See the like parable, 2 Kings 14:9.

*{and they said unto the olive-tree}* By the olive and the rest of the fruitful and useful trees here named are meant both, the judges who ruled in former times, and also Gideon and his seventy sons, who never affected, but utterly refused, this kingly governance.

**V. 9. ***{wherewith by me they honour God and man}* i.e. By the oil which comes from me, God is honored, seeing oil is used in his service, which tends to his honor, both in oblations and sacrifices, and also for the nourishing of the lamps and lights in the sanctuary. And men also are honored by it, seeing it is used in the anointing of kings, priests and prophets, and is profitable unto all sorts and conditions, both for meat and medicine, food, face, and feature, Psa. 104:15.

*{go to be promoted over the trees?}* Or, *go up and down for other trees*. The kingly office is no easy calling but obliges to great pains and cares: for kings, like the sun, must oft visit the regions under them, and go from place to place to keep all in good order.

**V. 11. ***{should I forsake my sweetness, and my good fruit}* When men leave their private places and callings wherein they are fruitful and serviceable to God and men, and ambitiously affect high preferments and sovereignty, they usually grow fruitless and barren in grace and goodness.

**V. 13. ***{which cheereth God and man}* Not that God uses wine, as man does, to cheer him by drinking of it, but because also it was used in sacrifices and oblations, in which God delighted, as being duties of his service. But man is cheered by it when temperately used, as being a singular means to renew and refresh his spirits, especially when they sink with sorrow, or droop under the pressure of any great calamity, Psa. 104:15, Prov. 31:6.

**V. 14. ***{Then said all the trees unto the bramble}* i.e. A base, worthless and fruitless shrub, good for nothing but to stop gaps, and with its pricks to keep men and beasts from entering into the pleasant fields, and afterwards to be burned, and with its blaze to kindle a fire. By the bramble Jotham means all proud and ambitious men, and particularly Abimelech, who was base in his birth, vicious and wicked in his life, barren in all grace and goodness, and tyrannical and cruel in his disposition and nature; and by the trees, the Shechemites and other Israelites who offered to make him their king.

**V. 15. ***{come and put your trust under my shadow}* i.e. Rest and rely wholly upon me for shelter, safety and protection. Such specious shows tyrants make when they sue and seek after sovereignty, yet do nothing less when they have attained unto it; like a bramble, which promises shelter to the silly sheep that comes under it in a storm, but scratches off his wool and tears his skin, when desiring to be at his liberty he would go from under it; or like a rider, who desiring to back a wild skittish horse, clips and strokes him at his getting up, but spurs and galls him when he finds himself sure in the saddle. And such an one was this cruel tyrant Abimelech, and also Nebuchadnezzar, Dan. 4:12.

*{Let fire come out of the bramble}* Some write that this shrub being violently shaken by the winds, by reason of its dry substance apt to kindle of itself and burn, and that thereby sometimes whole woods are set on fire. See Ezek. 19:14, Jam. 3:5, Psa. 58:9.

*{and devour the Cedars of Lebanon}* i.e. The most illustrious nobles, and chief elders and magistrates, when they stand in his way, and refuse to be instruments of his tyranny and cruelty. These are compared to the cedars of Lebanon, because they were the goodliest and tallest in all those countries.

**V. 16. ***{Now therefore}* In these words he unfolds and applies the parable.

*{if ye have dealt well with Jerubbaal}* i.e. If you have gratefully requited him for all the benefits received from him.

*{his house}* i.e. His sons and posterity.

**V. 17. ***{adventured his life far}* Heb. *cast his life*, i.e. neglected and despised his life for the common good. So chap. 12:3 and 5:18.

**V. 18. ***{because he is your brother}* i.e. Not because he was the son of Jerubbaal, or for any goodness in himself which might make him worthy of such an honor, but merely out of self-love, because he is of your kindred and alliance.

**V. 19. ***{then rejoice ye in Abimelech}* i.e. Live happily and joyfully with him, as with a king justly elected, and let him have all joy and content in you, as in his lawful and loyal subjects. This Jotham wishes, not as thinking such happiness would befall them (for he knew they had done wickedly in all their proceedings) but by way of irony and reproach.

**V. 20. ***{let fire come out from Abimelech}* That cursed bramble fit for nothing but to be burnt and to burn others, and *let fire come out from the men, &c.* i.e. Let a wicked spirit of dissension, hatred and revenge possess them, and carry them on to ruin and destroy one another.

**V. 21. ***{And Jotham ran away and fled, and went}* This variety of words expressing the same thing implies his great haste in flying.

*{to Beer}* This some conceive was a town in the tribe of Ephraim; others, and that more probably, think it was Baalath-beer in the tribe of Simeon, as being in a greater distance from Shechem, and so further out of the reach, and it may be out of the power and government of Abimelech. See Josh. 19:8.

**V. 22. ***{When Abimelech had reigned three years}* To wit, as a usurping tyrant, and not as a judge delivering the people from their enemies, or exercising judgment and justice among them, or doing any other good to the commonwealth; then, when he least thought of it, and supposing that he was securely settled in his government, God began to execute upon him the curse threatened by Jotham, and to make him an example of that common maxim, that there is no tyranny of long continuance.

*{over Israel}* Not that he had the same dominion over the whole nation that he had over the Shechemites; but some authority and sway he might have over all, being looked upon as the heir and successor of Gideon. See on v. 6.

**V. 23. ***{Then God sent an evil spirit between Abimelech and the men of Shechem}* i.e. Either Satan himself, the chief author of all disorders and divisions in a commonwealth, as 1 Sam. 10:9 and 19:9, or the evil affections of malice, envy and discord. And this he did, not by infusing into them these sinful corruptions, but as a just judge punishing their former wickedness, especially that horrid massacre of Gideon’s sons, he gave them up to their own vile affections and the devil’s temptations, to be hurried on in wicked courses to their own mutual ruin and utter destruction. Deut. 2:30, 1 Sam. 2:25, 1 Kings 12:15 and 22:22, 23, 2 Chron. 18:19, and 20:22. See on Hos. 10:2.

*{and the men of Shechem dealt treacherously with Abimelech}* i.e. They broke their covenant with him, and rebelling against him shook off the yoke of his government.

**V. 24. ***{That the cruelty done}* i.e. The just revenge of that cruelty.

*{and their blood be laid upon Abimelech}* i.e. The guilt and punishment of their blood unjustly shed. So 2 Sam. 1:16, 1 Kings 2:31, 32, etc. Unto which, though they had not the least respect in their mutual murders, yet it so came to pass by a just and overruling hand of God’s providence punishing them for their sin.

**V. 25. ***{And the men of Shechem set liers in wait for him}* i.e. Resolving to rebel and cast him off from being any longer their king, they laid a plot by way of ambush to surprise his person, if he happened to come that way weakly attended.

*{and they robbed all that came along}* Chiefly those whom they knew to be of Abimelech’s party, thereby to weaken them, or revenge themselves upon them for their master’s sake; or, if others with them, then to supply their present wants whilst they lay there in wait.

*{and it was told Abimelech}* i.e. That the Shechemites had rebelled and laid ambush to surprise him. Whereupon he prepared for war, and sent some of his army into those parts, which restrained the Shechemites from going out of their city to gather their vintage, till Gaal came in to their assistance.

**V. 26. ***{And Gaal the son of Ebed}* Who this Gaal was appears not, but it seems he was some famous commander, seeing the Shechemites put such confidence in him that they made choice of him to be general of their forces. See on v. 28.

*{with his brethren}* i.e. He, his kindred and allies, came in to their assistance, with an intention it may be, after he had overcome and cast out Abimelech, to take the government upon himself.

**V. 27. ***{they went out into the fields, and gathered their vineyards}* To show how much, out of their confidence in Gaal, they now slighted Abimelech, whom before they so much feared that they dared not go out of the city.

*{and made merry}* Or, *sung songs*; as they used to do in the time of vintage, Isai. 16:10, Jer. 25:30, Psa. 4:7.

*{and went into the house of their god}* Namely, to praise Baal-berith for delivering them from the yoke of Abimelech, and to offer sacrifice, and feast in his temple, as it was the custom of those times.

*{and cursed Abimelech}* In their merry mood they reviled Abimelech, wishing him all mischiefs and mischances.

**V. 28. ***{Who is Abimelech, and who is Shechem}* i.e. How base is he and unworthy of sovereignty? and how unfit is it that so ancient and famous a city as Shechem should be enslaved under his tyranny? Others think this is not spoken of the city Shechem, but of Shechem the son of Hamor, who in Jacob’s time was governor of it, Gen. 34, and make this to be the meaning: What have we to do either with the one or the other, seeing neither can lay any just title or claim unto it? Not Shechem, or any of his posterity, seeing they are cast out, and their inheritance given to God’s people; nor Abimelech, who has got the sovereignty over it by his own tyranny and usurpation, which in itself is a free city, and to be governed by no other than its own magistrates.

*{Is not he the son of Jerubbaal?}* His son by a concubine and so has less title unto it than any of the rest of his sons that came of his wives. And though Jerubbaal was worthily esteemed for his worthy acts, yet what is this to Abimelech, seeing he refused to be king when it was offered unto him? And yet his very name Jerubbaal may make his memory of less esteem, who being but a mean private man got his name by that act of his, in destroying the altar of your god, whom you now worship and serve with unanimous consent.

*{and Zebul his officer?}* Whom he in his absence has constituted his deputy. This he speaks to upbraid them of their base servitude, seeing they had not only enslaved themselves to Abimelech the son of a concubine, but also to Zebul his servant, and so to any other whom he in his proud humor should be pleased to set over them. And of this he purposely minds them to work Zebul out of favor, that they might cast him out of his government, and make choice of him to rule in his room.

*{Serve the men of Hamor the father of Shechem}* This he speaks by way of irony and scorn, q.d. If you will needs make yourselves, who are a free people, to become bondmen and slaves; you had better seek out some of the progeny of the noble and ancient lords of this city than vassalage yourselves to such a base and new upstart as Abimelech. And thus persuading them to make choice of some one of Hamor’s progeny, some think he pointed to himself, whom he pretended to be descended of him. Others, more probably, thus: If you will needs serve, then choose such as will not tyrannically rule over you, like Abimelech, but in a fatherly manner, as Hamor did of old.

**V. 29. ***{And would to God this people were under my hand}* i.e. Under my sole government. Whereby it appears that howsoever many adhered to Gaal, as having great confidence in him, yet there were others that cleaved still to Abimelech, and were content to submit unto Zebul as his officer.

*{then would I remove Abimelech}* i.e. I would quickly dispatch him out of the way, and not suffer him any longer to be king over you.

*{And he said to Abimelech, Increase thy army}* In a bold, bragging and thrasonical manner he speaks unto him being absent: Make yourself as strong as you can with all your allies and friends, and then come out and fight with me, for hereby my victory over you shall be so much the more glorious.

**V. 30. ***{And when Zebul}* When he had heard Gaal reproach his lord, and also disgrace him who was his officer and lieutenant, howsoever he had made before some semblance and show that he would not cross the Shechemites in their courses against Abimelech, yet now he was so much incensed with Gaal’s insolence, that he could no longer endure him, but falls, though secretly, to practice against him.

**V. 31. ***{Privily}* Lest being discovered to be wholly for Abimelech, the Shechemites should rise up against him, and either kill him or wholly cast him out of his place of government; and also that they not knowing of it, Abimelech might come upon them suddenly and find them unprovided. Hebr. *craftily*: or, *to Tormah*. And some conceive Tormah to be the name of the city which v. 41 is called Arumah, where Abimelech dwelt.

**V. 32. ***{Now therefore up by night}* That your coming may not be discovered; and lay secretly an ambush against the Shechemites, that they may fall into it in the morning, when some of your company appearing they shall set upon them, and they making their retreat, as though they were not able to maintain the fight, shall draw them into the ambush.

*{and the people that is with thee}* i.e. Stay not to gather more forces, but come with those you have already, seeing they will be sufficient to execute this design.

**V. 33. ***{as thou shalt find occasion}* Heb. *as thine hand shall find*; i.e. as you shall get opportunity in fighting against Gaal and those that shall come out of the city with him. See the like phrases, having the like signification, Lev. 25:28, 1 Sam. 10:7 and 25:8, Eccl. 9:10.

**V. 34. ***{and they laid wait against Shechem in four companies}* Namely, that but one of the four appearing to their view, the Shechemites might be drawn out of the city, and being come out to fight with this one, might fall into the ambushments that were laid by the other three.

**V. 35. ***{and the people that were with him}* i.e. One of the four companies.

**V. 36. ***{And when Gaal saw the people, he said to Zebul}* Who came in his company, counterfeiting himself to be of his party, that he might encourage him to go out and fight, and so fall into the hands of the liers in wait.

*{Thou seest the shadow of the mountains as if there were men}* He jeers and derides him, as though his sight so failed him that he could put no difference between mountains and men, or rather that he was so fainthearted that he was afraid of very shadows.

**V. 37. ***{by the middle of the land}* Heb. *Navel*: a metaphorical speech borrowed from the resemblance: for as the navel stands out above the belly, so the mountains above the ground about them.

**V. 38. ***{Then said Zebul unto him, Where is now thy mouth}* i.e. Your former great brags and boastings when you saw no enemy? q.d. Is your courage so soon quailed, that your heart faints and fails before you come to fight? And with these scorns and reproaches he puts him on to adventure the fight for very shame, and not dastardly to return into the city, and there stand on his defense.

**V. 39. ***{And Gaal went out before the men of Shechem}* Being partly put forward by Zebul’s scorns, and partly conceiving some hopes of prevailing upon sight of Abimelech’s small army, supposing it to be no more but those two companies which he had discovered, whereas indeed two others lay still in ambush.

**V. 40. ***{he fled before him, and many were overthrown}* i.e. More were slain in the flight than in the fight, seeing they pursued them to the very gates of the city, and slaughtered them all the way.

**V. 41. ***{And Abimelech dwelt at Arumah}* A city near Shechem southerly. Hither Abimelech retired himself with his army, either to refresh themselves after their fight, or to get some better opportunity of surprising Shechem.

*{Zebul thrust out Gaal and his brethren}* Taking the advantage of his defeat, which had wrought him out of the hearts of the people, because they thought it had happened either through his cowardice or his ill managing of their martial affairs.

**V. 42. ***{And it came to pass on the morrow}* i.e. After they had thrust out Gaal and his brethren, as thinking his insufficiency to act the part of general to have been the cause of their great defeat, they having made choice of a new commander, in whom they had more confidence, and recruited their army, go out again against Abimelech to revenge upon him their former loss, and, if it were possible, to drive him out of the country, that they might not still be endangered by having so malicious an enemy so near a neighbor.

**V. 43. ***{and divided them into three companies}* Appointing two of them to fight with such as sallied out of the city, and keeping the third with himself, he lay in ambush near the gates of the city, that when their chief strength were issued out, he might assault and surprise it, being but weakly guarded, or at least might keep them within from going out to second those who were engaged in the fight, and them that were without in the field from making any retreat, if put to the worse.

**V. 44. ***{and stood in the entering of the gate of the city}* That he might slay them that being escaped out of the battle thought to secure their lives by getting into the city.

**V. 45. ***{beat down the city, and sowed it with salt}* i.e. Did not only wholly demolish the walls without and the buildings within, but also sowed it with salt. This ceremony was used by conquerors being highly provoked by the reproaches and insolencies of their enemies, not only to raze their cities, but also to sow them with salt, in sign of an utter vastation and perpetual desolation. For salt is taken to be both a cause and sign of barrenness, Deut. 29:23, Psa. 107:33, 34, Zeph. 2:9. Yet I have heard there are some kind of soils, even in our own land, of that nature, that they use to manure them with salt, as with dung or marl, and by experience find it makes them fruitful. But salt had the quite contrary operation in those hot and dry countries, making the earth more hot and dry; and therefore Abimelech used it as a sign of barrenness and utter desolation. But herein he failed of his end; for Shechem afterwards was rebuilt by Jeroboam, 1 Kings 12:25, and inhabited, 2 Chron. 10:1, Jer. 41:5. After the captivity it was named Sychar. See on John 4:5.

**V. 46. ***{they entered into an hold of the house of the god Berith}* Called Baal-berith, v. 4. Hither they went from the tower of Shechem for better security against the power and rage of Abimelech, trusting both in the strength and also the religion of the place, using it as their sanctuary in this extremity of danger. This hold is probably thought to be the same with the house of Millo, v. 6, unto which Jotham’s prophetical curse extended, as joining with Abimelech in that horrid conspiracy against Gideon’s sons, v. 20. It was the strongest and most impregnable fort, both by nature and art, near unto Shechem, like the Roman Capitol, in which was the temple of Jupiter, and which was of such strength that it was able to hold out against the enemy when all the city was taken; even as also the temple was the strongest hold, unto which the Jews retreated when Jerusalem was besieged by Titus Vespasian.

**V. 48. ***{mount Salmon}* This lay westward from Shechem, towards Jordan. On it were store of trees, and snow that seldom melted, Psa. 68:14.

*{What ye have seen me do, make haste, and do as I have done}* This he did, knowing that princes’ examples are more prevalent with the people than their commands.

**V. 49. ***{died also}* i.e. Were all burnt and destroyed, as well they in the sacred fort as they in the tower.

**V. 50. ***{Thebez}* Situate in the territory of Shechem northerly, between the mountains of Samaria and Gilboa. Some think it was the place where Elijah was born. See on 1 Kings 17:1.

**V. 51. ***{and shut it to them}* i.e. Made fast the gates with locks, bolts and bars.

**V. 52. ***{fought against it}* i.e. Besieged and assaulted it.

**V. 53. ***{And a certain woman cast a piece of a millstone}* Which, with the like materials, they had purposely carried up to the top of the tower to beat off the assailants. So Pyrrhus was slain by a woman casting a tile upon his head at the taking of Argos.

**V. 54. ***{that men say not of me, A woman slew him}* Thus his wicked life was attended with as bad a death. For with a soldier-like bravery he took care for his credit, but none for his soul; yet all in vain: for by this means whereby he thought to smother it was the more divulged: 2 Sam. 11:21. See the like passage, 1 Sam. 31:4.

**V. 55. ***{the men of Israel}* i.e. Which followed Abimelech in this expedition.

**V. 56. ***{the wickedness of Abimelech which he did unto his father}* In murdering his sons, his own brethren.

**V. 57. ***{And all the evil of the men of Shechem}* i.e. Their assisting Abimelech in all his bloody practices against the house of Gideon: wherein they showed themselves as impious against God, so barbarously ungrateful towards Gideon for all his well deserving. These abominable wrongs and outrages God sharply revenged both upon him and them according to the prophetical curse denounced by Jotham v. 20.

## CHAPTER 10

**Verse 1. ***{To defend Israel}* Heb. *save*, or, *deliver*, i.e. to preserve them in peace, liberty, and purity of religion against all that did oppose them.

*{Tola}* Some think he was not, as the other judges, called extraordinarily by God, but after the death of Abimelech chosen by the people to be their governor, because there is no mention made of any enemies that oppressed them, out of whose hands he should deliver them. From whence they also gather, that throughout all his time they lived in peace. But seeing it is said, v. 2, he judged Israel, and seeing all the former judges were called extraordinarily by God to deliver the people when they were under oppressions, and being delivered to govern them in peace, to abolish idolatry, and to restore God’s pure worship, I conceive that he had the like calling, though not immediately, as Gideon, by personal appearance yet by inward instinct and the collation of heroical gifts, as courage and fortitude, wisdom, zeal, and the rest. Neither does it follow that they had no enemies, from which Tola delivered them, because none are mentioned, seeing there might be enemies and oppressions, though not so notorious and vexatious as in former times. See on v. 11. And though there had been none at all, yet the people in those times needed a judge, to reduce them to the former government, which was suppressed by Abimelech’s tyranny, to abolish idolatries which much reigned and increased in his licentious, profane and anarchical monarchy, to restore religion and God’s worship to its native purity and perfection, and vindicate the seat of judgment from arbitrary injustice and oppression.

*{And he dwelt in Shamir}* A town of Issachar bordering on Ephraim. There was another Shamir in the tribe of Judah, Josh. 15:48. Or suppose this Shamir was in mount Ephraim, as it is in the letter of the text, yet Tola, though of another tribe, might inhabit there, being judge, for more convenience, as being near the heart of the land, and not far from the tabernacle. Though I incline rather to the former exposition, seeing it is expressly said he was also buried in Shamir, v. 2, which strongly implies that it was in his own tribe, and bordering only on mount Ephraim, seeing it was the custom of those times to be buried in their own inheritance. See on Josh. 24:32.

**V. 3. ***{And after him arose Jair a Gileadite}* i.e. Dwelling in Gilead in the tribe of Manasseh beyond Jordan, Josh. 17:1, 5, Numb. 32:39, Gen. 31:48. This was not that Jair mentioned, Numb. 32:41 and Deut. 3:14, though he carried the same name, and also got and possessed towns or villages called Havoth-jair. For that Jair lived above three hundred years before this, and had twenty-three cities, 1 Chron. 2:22, whereas this man’s sons had thirty, the old number being thus increased either by themselves, their father, or grandfather. And this Jair being descended from the other, both he and his inheritance kept the old names.

**V. 4. ***{And he had thirty sons, that rode on thirty ass colts}* This is added to show that he was a man of great wealth and worth, and that his sons were also of an honorable condition, seeing riding on ass-colts was a token of dignity and authority. See on chap. 5:10 and on Exod. 13:13.

*{Havoth-jair}* Or, *the villages of Jair*, so called by Jair the son of Manasseh, Numb. 32:41, and continued by this judge in the same name.

**V. 5. ***{in Camon}* A city belonging to Manasseh, in the land of Bashan lying north from mount Gilead.

**V. 6. ***{served Baalim and Ashtaroth}* These are especially named above the rest, because the chief and most common idols. See on chap. 2:13.

*{and the gods of Syria}* This is added to tax the Israelites of their abominable idolatries, who were not satisfied by committing spiritual whoredom with one or two idols of this or that people, but gave themselves up to commit unsatiable uncleanness with all the idols of every nation that lived about them. So Ezek. 16:25, etc.

*{and the gods of Sidon}* i.e. Astarte. See ch. 2:13.

*{and the gods of Moab}* i.e. Chemosh, 1 Kings 11:33.

*{and the gods of the children of Ammon}* i.e. Milchom, or Molech, 1 Kings 11:5, 33, and Chemosh, ch. 11:24.

*{and the gods of the Philistines}* i.e. Dagon, 1 Sam. 5:2, and Ashtaroth. See ch. 2:13.

*{and forsook the Lord, and served not him}* i.e. They were so addicted to idolatry that they joined not idols with God in his service, or served him in idols, like their forefathers, but utterly renounced God’s service, and gave themselves wholly up to the worship of idols.

**V. 7. ***{the anger of the Lord was hot}* See on ch. 3:8.

*{and he sold them}* See on ch. 2:14.

**V. 8. ***{And that year they vexed and oppressed [or crushed] the children of Israel eighteen years, all the, &c.}* That is, as some think, in that year wherein they fell from the true worship of God to this idolatry, they vexed them; and so from that time to the end of eighteen years. Others reckon these eighteen years from the death of Jair. But this cannot stand with that chronology, 1 Kings 6:1. Others read, *they vexed them that year*, that is the eighteenth, or the last year of the eighteenth. But this is as far short of the time of that chronology as the other above it. The right sense of the place, as I take it, is this: that the Philistines and Ammonites vexed those two tribes and a half that lay without Jordan for the space of eighteen years, with inroads and incursions into their country, wasting and spoiling all that came in their way: the greatest part of which time they living under Jair’s government, their enemies still by his prudence and prowess were beaten out again. But now in that year wherein Jair died, growing more bold and presumptuous upon the death of the judge, they extend their vastations and oppressions, and make them more general and universal: contenting themselves with the spoils of the Israelites that dwelt beyond Jordan, they crossed also upon the land of Canaan, and distressed Judah, Benjamin, and Ephraim, v. 9.

*{which is in Gilead}* See on Obad. 19.

**V. 10. ***{And the children of Israel cried unto the Lord}* No doubt they often cried unto the Lord before, being so long time under such grievous oppressions, but he regarded them not, because their prayers were not joined with true repentance, seeing they still continued in their gross idolatry; but now they repent, not only of their sins in general, but also of their special and bosom sins, their neglect of God’s worship and serving of idols, and humbly confess them with detestation. So chap. 4:3.

**V. 11. ***{And the Lord said}* i.e. Either the Lord Christ appearing in human shape, as ch. 2:1 and 5:23, and 6:12, or by some created angel, or some prophet, sent with this message; or finally by his oracle related unto them by the high priest.

*{from the children of Ammon}* Divers of these deliverances here expressed we do not find related before in this holy history: whereby it appears this people received at God’s hands many more favors and blessings than are here recorded.

**V. 12. ***{and the Maonites}* Some read, *the inhabitants of the land*, to wit, Canaan. Others understand hereby the inhabitants of a city called Maon, upon the south of Judah, Josh. 15:55, to which the wilderness of Maon adjoined, 1 Sam. 23:25. From whence the Canaanites which did inhabit it, and in the tract of Cushan-rishathaim it may be did afflict and oppress the Israelites, are here called Maonites.

**V. 13. ***{Wherefore I will deliver you no more}* This threatening was not absolute, as the sequel shows, but conditional, namely, if they did not forsake their idolatry, and embrace God’s pure worship; unto which he provokes and quickens them by this threatening. So Jer. 18:7, 8.

**V. 14. ***{Go and cry unto the gods which ye have chosen}* This is no real command (for the holy and righteous God does never require any man to commit sin) but an ironical upbraiding of them for their folly in committing idolatry, which now they found so fruitless in their greatest need; their idols being no ways able to send them deliverance, though they did cry unto them never so loud. So Deut. 32:37, 38. Thus Elijah derided the Baalish priests, 1 Kings 18:27. See on Jer. 2:28.

**V. 15. ***{do thou unto us whatsoever seemeth good unto thee}* i.e. Punish us hereafter as you please, if we do not persist in your true and pure worship, and utterly renounce our idolatry and all our false gods. Only make trial of us this once, by giving us deliverance out of the hands of our enemies.

**V. 16. ***{his soul was grieved}* Heb. *shortened, straitened, or straightened*. For as the heart is dilated and enlarged with joy, so it is contracted and straitened with grief. Not that God can properly be said to joy or grieve, who in his simple and perfect nature is free from all human passions; but that we may the better conceive of his dealing with us, by a figurative speech grief is ascribed to him, as if he acted after the manner of men, who usually grieve for those that are in misery before they give them help and deliverance. See on Isa. 63:9.

**V. 17. ***{encamped in Gilead}* Which they now challenged as their ancient inheritance, ch. 11:13, and also because it was nearest to their country.

*{in Mizpeh}* The Hebrew word signifies a high place convenient to set watches or watchtowers upon, both to see and to be seen afar off, like our beacons seated on the top of high hills. There were many places of this name; as in Judah, Josh. 15:38, in Benjamin, Josh. 18:26, of which see on ch. 20:1, and Mizpeh of the Moabites, 1 Sam. 22:3. But this did lie upon mount Gilead, ch. 11:29, Gen. 31:49. See on Hos. 5:1.

*{what man is he that will begin to fight}* i.e. Who will undertake to be our leader in this expedition against Ammon? and for his better encouragement, he shall ever after be our general, judge and governor, ch. 11:8, 9. And when none would accept of it, as thinking the service dangerous and desperate, they resolved to send unto Jephthah, being of known valor and a skillful commander. This is here spoken as an introduction to the following story.

## CHAPTER 11

**Verse 1. ***{Now Jephthah the Gileadite}* See on ch. 10:3. He is said to be a Gileadite, not because his father’s name was Gilead but because he was born in Gilead. But of what tribe he was, it appears not, though it be very probable he was of Manasseh, seeing the greatest part of that country was in their possession, and the old Gilead was the son of Machir, Josh. 17:3, 1 Chron. 7:14.

*{was a mighty man of valour}* Both in respect of fortitude of mind and strength of body, whereby he was enabled to achieve noble acts, and to endure those hard labors that are incident to a warlike profession.

*{and he was the son of an harlot}* Heb. *A woman, an harlot*. Bastards by the law of God were forbidden to come into the congregation unto the tenth generation, Deut. 23:2. Which is not so to be taken as though they were not to be present in the assemblies met together for God’s public worship, but that they would not be admitted to any public office, either sacred or civil, though they were the bastards of priests or princes. But this case of present necessity dispensed with the strictness and rigor of this law, the end and equity whereof was only to show how much God abhorred whoredom and uncleanness, that the people might avoid it; and not to inflict a punishment upon the person thus begotten, if he abhorred and forsook his parents’ sin, and served God in holiness and righteousness.

*{and Gilead begat Jephthah}* i.e. One of the posterity of Gilead mentioned Josh. 17:3, and of his name.

**V. 2. ***{they thrust out Jephthah}* From having any part not only in their father’s inheritance, in which by the law he had no right, but also in his goods for his present maintenance, in which he might have lawfully shared. And this he counts as a wrong done unto him, and this not acted in a violent course by his brethren alone, but also in a legal way, being countenanced and assisted by their elders and magistrates, as he charges them v. 7.

**V. 3. ***{in the land of Tob}* A part of a country lying along mount Gilead, not far from the Ammonites, at the entrance of Arabia the desert, 2 Sam. 10:6, 8. Some think Tob to be a proper name of the man, who was owner of that country. See 1 Macc. 5:13, and 2 Macc. 12:27.

*{vain men}* i.e. Idle fellows, that had no calling nor means of maintenance, or such as were in debt and danger, and in a desperate condition, 1 Sam. 22:2. Unto such Romulus opened sanctuaries; and Julius Caesar said such stood in need of a civil war. See on ch. 9:4.

*{and went out with him}* Namely, as their captain in military expeditions, to fetch in prey and booty, on which they might live; wanting other means; and it is likely from the Ammonites; and that with such good success, that the Gileadites were moved to think him the fittest man to be their general, having already approved himself in those wars against them. See the like 1 Sam. 27:8.

**V. 4. ***{The Elders of Gilead went to fetch Jephthah}* i.e. Either by God’s special command, or some extraordinary instinct, or in regard of present necessity; for otherwise a bastard might not bear any public office. See on v. 1.

**V. 7. ***{expel me out of my father’s house}* i.e. Gave sentence against me at the suit of my brethren, that I should be expelled.

*{and why are ye come unto me now}* q.d. You never made me any amends for the wrong you then did me, nor would do it yet out of any respect of justice or love towards me; but all you do is out of mere necessity, because you are in distress, and nobody else will run the danger to help you out of it. See the like Gen. 26:27.

**V. 8. ***{Therefore we turn again to thee now}* q.d. We cannot deny but that you do justly accuse us of unjust proceeding against you: but let that be forgotten, seeing we are now come to make you amends. For by turning again they do not mean that they had been with him before about this business, but that they repented of their former actions of unkindness, and now returned unto him with their hearts and affections.

*{and be our head over all the inhabitants of Gilead}* At first he was chosen to be their general only; but afterwards, having so well acquitted and approved himself in this warlike expedition, he was chosen to be judge over all Israel, ch. 12:7.

**V. 9. ***{shall I be your head?}* i.e. Not only your general in this service, but also judge and chief magistrate over all your country forever after, or as long as I live. This he does not speak as a man that ambitiously thirsted after rule, but out of mere equity and honesty, that having adventured his life in such a desperate and dangerous service, he might be sure of that just reward which of their own free accord they had offered unto him, and being become a public person might no more be exposed to the injuries of his brethren.

**V. 10. ***{The Lord be witness between us}* Heb. *be the hearer between us*. i.e. We call God as a witness, who is present everywhere, and hears all our discourse, and is a just judge to punish us if we do not keep our covenant with you, that we will be unto you as good as our words, and make you our head in time to come as well as now.

**V. 11. ***{and Jephthah uttered all his words}* Which had passed between him and the elders of Gilead in the land of Tob.

*{before the Lord in Mizpeh}* i.e. In a solemn and religious manner, as in God’s presence; before the whole assembly of the people encamped in Mizpeh, ch. 10:17, he repeated the covenants agreed upon between them, that hereby they might be the more strictly bound to observe and keep them.

**V. 12. ***{And Jephthah sent messengers unto the king &c.}* i.e. Proceeded according to the law of God, of arms, and of nations, which require first to seek peace upon lawful conditions before the proclaiming of a just war, and if this cannot be obtained, then to proclaim it before they made an entrance into it. This was the custom of the ancient Romans, and, before them, the practice of Jephthah in this place: Deut. 20:10, 11.

*{What hast thou to do with me?}* i.e. What just cause of war have you against me, and of invading my country? He speaks this in the name of all the people, as their general. See the like expression, 2 Sam. 16:10.

*{come against me to fight in my land}* i.e. The land which is now under my protection, being entrusted unto me by the general consent of the people, who have chosen me to be their captain and governor.

**V. 13. ***{Because Israel took away}* This was a false claim, seeing he had no right to the land he challenged: for it belonged first for the most part to the Moabites; and that part which the Ammonites had was won from them by the Amorites, in whose possession it was by right of conquest until it was gotten out of their hands by the Israelites, who had now held it in their possession for a long time; not only by right of conquest but also by God’s free donation, who being Lord of the whole earth has power to dispose of it according to his good pleasure. See Numb. 21:24, 25, 26, 29, etc., Deut. 2:19, 37 and 3:11, and on Josh. 12:25.

*{my land}* Speaking in the name both of the children of Ammon and Moab, over which it seems he at this time reigned as chief king. See on v. 24.

*{from Arnon even unto Jabbok, and unto Jordan}* i.e. That tract that lies upon these rivers, from mount Gilead eastward unto Jordan westward.

**V. 14. ***{And Jephthah sent messengers again}* i.e. Further to clear his cause, for the quieting of his own conscience, when he was assured that he fought in a just quarrel, the encouraging of his army, and the convincing of his enemies, that their war was unjust and naught.

**V. 15. ***{Israel took not away the land}* Jephthah lays open the untruth of the king of Ammon’s plea; and shows that the Israelites at their coming out of Egypt did neither meddle with the Moabites nor the Ammonites’ land, God having expressly forbidden it, Deut. 2:9, 19.

**V. 16. ***{Kadesh}* Called also Zin, to difference it from Kadesh-barnea, Num. 13:26 and 20:11, 14 and 13:26.

**V. 17. ***{Then Israel sent messengers unto the King of Edom}* Numb. 20:14. Jephthah relates their ancient passages, to show how fairly the Israelites had carried themselves to all the nations round about them, when they traveled towards the land of Canaan, nor had offered no wrong to any of them.

*{and Israel abode in Kadesh}* i.e. They quietly bore this unkindness and injury, and did not seek to revenge themselves by taking up arms.

**V. 18. ***{Then they went along through the wilderness, and compassed}* i.e. They put themselves to a tedious and troublesome journey by compassing about their country, that so they might enter into the promised land, rather than they would give them the least cause of offense.

*{and pitched on the other side of Arnon}* But were so far from invading the Moabites’ country that they would not make entrance so much as within the border of it, Numb. 21:13, 24 and 22:36.

**V. 19. ***{And Israel sent messengers unto Sihon}* When they could not prevail either with Edom or Moab for obtaining a passage into Canaan, they in a fair course assayed Sihon.

*{King of the Amorites}* So called, not because he was king of all the Amorites (for there were many Amorites on both sides of Jordan that were not under his dominion) but because all those over whom he ruled were Amorites.

*{unto my place}* i.e. The land of Canaan, which God has given me for an inheritance. For unto this only they pretended, and by this right; and therefore laid no claim to Sihon’s country, which lay without Canaan, but in a peaceable manner desired only a passage through his land.

**V. 20. ***{but Sihon gathered all his people together}* i.e. Not only denied them passage, but also in a hostile manner came out against them, the Israelites not having provoked by any injury which they had offered to him or his people.

**V. 21. ***{So Israel possessed all the land of the Amorites}* i.e. Having vanquished them in a lawful war, they had just title unto it by law of arms, as being gotten by conquest.

**V. 22. ***{And they possessed all the coasts of the Amorites}* i.e. All that region which now the Ammonites unjustly challenge, or all the country within these coasts: Arnon on the south, Jabbok on the north, the wilderness of Arabia on the east, and Jordan on the west, Deut. 2:36.

**V. 23. ***{So now the Lord God of Israel}* This he alleges as another argument, that they had right to this land, because God the owner and Lord of the whole earth had given it unto them, and that not only in an absolute way of interest and right, but by giving them victory over the Amorites in a lawful war, Deut. 2:30, 31, 33.

**V. 24. ***{Wilt not thou possess that which Chemosh thy God}* So he calls him, not because he was a god in truth, but only in their opinion. He was the god or idol of the Moabites, Numb. 21:29, 1 Kings 11:7, Jer. 48:13, over whom he seems that the king of Ammon now reigned, and in that respect Chemosh is here said to be his god. For otherwise Molech or Milchom was the chief idol of the Ammonites, 1 Kings 11:7, 33 and 2 Kings 23:13.

*{giveth thee to possess}* The Lord has given unto the children of Moab the Emims’ land for a possession, as the land of the Zamzummims to the children of Ammon, Deut. 2:9, 10, 19, 20. But they ascribed their victories to their idols, and possessed their land as their gift.

*{So whomsoever the Lord our God shall drive out}* i.e. As you think you may justly possess all that which your idol Chemosh (as you falsely conceive) has given you, so much more may we lawfully possess all, that Jehovah the only true God has given unto us.

**V. 25. ***{art thou any thing better than Balak}* i.e. Have you better right to this land, or more power and policy to maintain it, than Balak who never yet made any such claim unto it? And this he uses as another reason to evince their right to the land, seeing they possessed it not only by right of conquest in a lawful war, but also by long prescription of divers preceding generations.

*{Did he ever strive against Israel?}* i.e. Though he hired Balaam to curse Israel, and, if that had succeeded, would have raised war against them, thinking (though falsely) that they intended to invade his land; yet when the Israelites had conquered the country of the Amorites, and were settled in it as their just possession, he never went about to recover it out of their hands, nor so much as made any claim unto it, notwithstanding it did in former times belong to the Moabites: Numb. 21:24, 26 and 22:2, Deut. 23:4.

**V. 26. ***{While Israel dwelt in Heshbon}* He reckons up some chief places of the country which the Israelites after they had conquered it did peaceably possess, and by them understands all the rest. Heshbon was the metropolis and chief city of Moab, in which Sihon dwelt; and Aroer, another city towards the east of this land. Of these see on Josh. 13:9, 16, 17.

*{three hundred years}* The time in a just computation, taking it from judge to judge, came not to so much by above thirty years. But it is usual in the Scriptures to make the sum round, and not to respect odd numbers, more or less: and of this custom Jephthah here made use, it being for his advantage to speak of the longest time when he pleaded prescription. But some conceive Jephthah reckons from the departure out of Egypt, seeing there began his narration, v. 16, and then his account rather falls short of, than exceeds, the just number.

*{why did ye not recover them within that time?}* i.e. If he had any such right to these towns and country as you now pretend, why did you not all this time challenge and plead it, but suffer the Israelites quietly to enjoy them?

**V. 27. ***{Wherefore I have not sinned against thee}* To wit, by detaining your cities and land from you, seeing I have a just title unto them, by lawful conquest, by God’s donation, and by legal prescription allowed of among all nations.

*{but thou dost me wrong to war against me}* To wit, by picking against me an unjust quarrel, that you might by war cast me out of my lawful possession.

*{the Lord the Judge be Judge}* i.e. Show himself a righteous judge in determining this quarrel between us, by prospering the innocent and giving them the victory, and punishing them that offer the wrong by bringing them to utter destruction.

**V. 29. ***{Then the spirit of the Lord came upon Jephthah}* i.e. The spirit of prudence and fortitude. See on ch. 3:10.

*{he passed}* To wit, with all his army.

*{and Manasseh}* i.e. Bashan; in which half the tribe of Manasseh inhabited.

**V. 30. ***{And Jephthah vowed a vow}* i.e. Before he undertook the execution of his design, he made a solemn vow to God, thereby to move him to prosper his expedition. This was usual among heathen emperors and great commanders, when they undertook warlike enterprises; and was also the practice of God’s people, Numb. 21:2. Of vows see on John 1:16.

**V. 31. ***{whatsoever cometh forth of the doors of my house to meet me}* This is a perplexed and confused vow, altogether unlimited to any subject, saving that it seems he intended some reasonable creature; for none else could propound to itself this end of coming forth, to meet him.

*{shall surely be the Lord’s, and I will offer it for a burnt offering}* Or thus; *or I will offer it for a burnt-offering*. This vow seems to consist of two branches; the first general, that he would consecrate and devote it to the Lord howsoever, the second more special, intimating the nature of the subject and the manner of devoting it, namely, that if it were fit for sacrifice, as clean beasts, and the turtledove, and young pigeon, then he would offer it up for a burnt offering. Or if his intention was to limit his vow to a human creature, as is supposed, then his meaning was that it should be devoted and consecrated to the Lord, but not in the way of a burnt offering, which was abominable to God, but set apart and sequestered from the world, in a special manner to perform unto him more special and religious service. For if he meant by these words, *shall surely be the Lords*, that it should be sacrificed for a burnt offering, then are the branches of this vow both one and the same, and no difference between them. But take this vow in what sense we will, we must needs confess, as it is here propounded, that it is very confused and perplexed, rash and unconsiderate. For howsoever it was most likely that a human creature should come out first to meet him, yet it was possible that a dog or some other unclean creature might have done so; and to have offered such in sacrifice, or otherwise devoted it unto God’s service, would have been abominable. Concerning the performance of this vow has risen a grand question amongst the learned, whether Jephthah offered his daughter for a burnt offering, or only consecrated her to the service of God in a state of virginity. And surely the parties are very strong on both sides, both in their parts and proofs. Of the former opinion we have Josephus, most of the ancient rabbis, the best of the Latin fathers, as Ambrose, Augustine, etc., with divers learned and godly men amongst our late writers: of the latter we have divers of the later rabbis, and many judicious divines above exception. It were therefore well worthy our pains, if thereby we could add some light to the deciding of this intricate and difficult question. To which purpose I will a little more largely insist upon it than will well stand with our intended brevity. And I will first premise some propositions, as *postulata* and necessary requisites, grounded on equity and charity, which being granted will much tend to the clearing of the point in controversy. The first is, that we do not involve Jephthah in a deeper degree of sin than the Scripture does evidently charge upon him, nor aggravate his faults by ungrounded surmises. And this I suppose will amount to no more than to render him blameworthy first for making an inconsiderate vow out of blind ignorance and superstitious devotion, and afterward for performing it. The second is, that we willingly entertain all advantages the text will afford us to clear him from having committed such an abominable wickedness as is laid to his charge, seeing the Holy Ghost has been pleased so much to honor him as to enroll him amongst his greatest worthies, and to eternize his name for his exemplary faith, Heb. 11:32. These being granted, as I cannot excuse him for his making and keeping a rash vow (though the best men have their failings, and there is not one who lives and sins not) so I cannot but acquit him of that horrid act of offering his daughter for a burnt offering; and that upon these reasons. I. This had been such a wickedness as is not only condemned with all detestation in the word of God as the most heinous and transcendent kind of idolatry, but also is against even the light of nature, and therefore abhorred by the very heathens, saving some few who were so blinded with a devilish zeal that they put off all humanity and natural affection. II. Though Jephthah should have made such a wicked vow, yet he was no way bound to keep it, when he saw the result quite contrary to his intention. For vows bind only to that which is good: and if the thing vowed seemed good, but afterwards proves evil, they do not bind; but as it was a sin to make them, so it is a greater to keep them, the one proceeding from rashness, the other from mature deliberation. Again, God in his law had plainly expressed upon what conditions he would dispense with such vows, and how the thing vowed might be redeemed, and at what rates, Lev. 27:3, 4. But whereas it is answered, that Jephthah by profession a soldier, and much living and conversing among heathens, was ignorant of this law, I reply though he were, yet he had time enough between the making and executing of his vow to have informed himself, if not by his own reading, yet at least by asking counsel of the priests, which it is not any way probable he would neglect, when it concerned him so near as the preservation of his only child. And whereas it is further objected, that by that law of redemption a thing devoted of men could not be redeemed, but must be put to death, Lev. 27:28, 29, and that this was it which might bring Jephthah into his error; to this it is answered, that the things there spoken of were only such as were anathematized and accursed for execrable wickedness, and therefore devoted to utter destruction, as Jericho and the spoils of it, Josh. 6:17, 18, and the Amalekites, 1 Sam. 15:3, and so that place concerned not Jephthah’s daughter, who was, as it appears, an innocent and pious virgin. III. There is nothing clearly expressed in the text which may make us conclude, that he either vowed to sacrifice his daughter as a burnt offering, or performed his vow in that sense, seeing the word might be taken not only conjunctively, *and I will offer*, but also disjunctively, *or I will offer*. So it is used Gen. 26:11, Exod. 20:10 and 21:15, and in divers other places. And if anywhere the word may be so taken, then surely charity itself binds us to take it so here. And then the sense of the vow will be no more but this: if it be such a thing as may lawfully be offered, then I will offer it for a burnt offering; or if not, it shall howsoever be consecrated unto God. But by the law not only human sacrifices were forbidden, as abominable, but all things that were unclean, Lev. 27:11, 12, 13, Deut. 12:31, Isai. 65:3. IV. It was not in Jephthah’s power to have offered up his daughter as a burnt offering, unless he could have involved others also in the same wickedness: he could not have offered her up himself, seeing it belonged only to the priests to offer sacrifices: and what probability is there, he could persuade the priests to join with him in such an horrid action? Again, what legal power had he to kill an innocent, and take away the life of his dear daughter without any offense by her committed against God or man? Or though it should (which indeed ought not) be supposed he might do it, having a right to dispose of his daughter at his pleasure, yet how could he have offered another, if another had come out to meet him, over whom he had no such color of right, as his wife, or the wife of one in his family? Or doing it, how could he be excused of willful murder? But it is objected, that if we should understand this vow to be a devoting of his daughter to God’s service in a state of perpetual virginity, it would much countenance monastical vows of single life, for which we find no warrant in Scripture before or after the days of Jephthah, yea, the clean contrary in Samuel, who though devoted to God’s service yet had wife and children. And this I conceive the chief reason that has induced godly learned men to think Jephthah’s vow should rather be understood of a burnt offering than of devoting his daughter to perpetual virginity. But why may they not as well fear, that if we take it in their sense, it might be abused to countenance human sacrifices, which were far more frequent than the other? For in truth there is no better ground from hence to countenance this rather than that. For seeing there is in Scripture neither precept nor precedent for this monastical vow of virginity, this example of Jephthah can be but a poor sandy foundation to build it upon, who lived in times of superstition and ignorance, and was himself infected therewith. In which respect his example may well be thought much fitter to serve as a sea-mark to make us keep off from falling upon these rocks and sands, than to be a precedent for our imitation. Other reasons and objections might be added, but seeing we shall have occasion to consider them in the latter part of this chapter, I will refer them to be spoken of in due place.

**V. 33. ***{And he smote them from Aroer}* See on Joshua 13:9, 36. Here Jephthah began to invade the land of Ammon.

*{to Minnith}* A town beyond Arnon eastward, in the borders of Ammon, near Rabbah. See Ezek. 27:17.

*{the plain of the vineyards}* Or *Abel*; a city in the country of the Ammonites famous for excellent vines.

**V. 34. ***{his daughter came out to meet him with timbrels and with dances}* Being accompanied with many other virgins. For it was the custom of those times for women and maids after great victories to sing songs of triumph, Exod. 15:20, Judg. 5:1, 1 Sam. 18:6. See on Psa. 68:11, 25. But though many came out with her, yet his vow reached not to any but one of his own family.

*{beside her he had neither son nor daughter}* Or, *he had not of his own either son nor daughter*. Heb. *of himself*. Which is added as a reason why he made such great lamentation.

**V. 35. ***{he rent his clothes}* i.e. In sign of his bitter grief. See on Gen. 37:29, Numb. 14:6, 2 Sam. 1:2 and 13:19, Job 1:20.

*{thou hast brought me very low}* i.e. I am not so much exalted by my great victory as humbled and depressed with this sad and unexpected accident, whereby I am become utterly hopeless of any posterity. From this passionate grief of Jephthah some conclude she was offered for a burnt offering, and not only devoted to serve God in a state of virginity. But if we consider how great a blessing it was counted in these times to be fruitful, and how great a curse the barren womb was esteemed, and that all hope was cut off from Jephthah, the chief magistrate in the commonwealth, from living in his posterity, we cannot but confess he had great cause of bitter grief. See Gen. 30:23, 1 Sam. 1:6, Luk. 1:25.

*{I have opened my mouth unto the Lord}* i.e. I have vowed.

*{and I cannot go back}* i.e. I dare not break my vow; not knowing in those days of ignorance and superstition that the law of God gave him liberty in this case to have redeemed his daughter with thirty shekels of silver, Lev. 27:4.

**V. 36. ***{And she said unto him}* Namely, in all probability, when he had first acquainted her with the nature and substance of his vow.

*{do to me according to that which hath proceeded out of thy mouth}* i.e. Seeing you have consecrated me as holy to the Lord, to live a virgin and a Nazarite all my days, I willingly agree unto it, and ratify my vow, devoting myself wholly unto God. So Anna, Luke 2:36. See 1 Cor. 7:34, 36, 37.
