Neither can we probably think, that Jephthah, commended for his faith, Heb. 11:32, should offer his daughter for a burnt offering, seeing that would have been much more odious to God than to have offered unto him swine’s blood, or a dog’s head, Isa. 65:4, and was expressly forbidden as most abominable, Deut. 12:31, and seeing God required that the firstborn children, which were his, Exod. 13:12 and 22:29, should not be offered, but redeemed, Numb. 18:15, Exod. 13:13.

**V. 37. ***{Let me alone two months}* Why, say some, does she desire two months, if at the end of them she was not to be offered up in the state of virginity, seeing still living she had time enough to do it afterwards? Because this lamentation was then most seasonable when she was first designed and adjudged unto this condition, and now ready to enter into it.

*{that I may go up and down upon the mountains}* As being solitary places, and therefore the fitter for mourning and the expressions of her grief in her bitter and passionate lamentations for this great affliction laid upon her, which cut off all hope of having any posterity. Capellus, *De voto Jephthae*, compares Jephthah with Agamemnon, whose daughter Iphigenia should have been sacrificed, had not Diana turned her into a hind. He fetches her name from Jephthah, qu. *Jephthigenia*; and says the fable of her transmutation arose from the story here, of Jephthah’s daughter wandering on the mountains. He shows also that Jephthah and Agamemnon lived much about a time.

*{and bewail my virginity}* Here note, that she speaks not of bewailing her approaching death, or being sacrificed, but her virginity and barrenness.

**V. 39. ***{who did unto her according to his vow}* i.e. Did not redeem her, according to the law, but consecrate her unto the Lord, as a virgin to serve him in single life, 1 Cor. 7:32.

*{and she knew no man}* He does not say, *and she was offered for a burnt offering*; but, *and she knew no man*, i.e. lived a virgin, as her father had vowed and she assented. See on Gen. 4:1.

*{And it was a custom}* Or, *an ordinance*.

**V. 40. ***{went yearly}* Heb. *from year to year*.

*{to lament the daughter of Jephthah}* Or, *to talk with her*: that is they did go to visit her four days in a year, that they might commune and discourse with her, comfort and cheer her in this her solitary condition. See ch. 5:11, where the same word is used to signify speaking or relating.

## CHAPTER 12

**Verse 1. ***{And the men of Ephraim gathered themselves together}* Heb. *were called*. i.e. Being moved with vainglory, and envying Jephthah’s and the Gileadites’ victory and good success, as they had done Gideon’s, ch. 8:1, and on the same ground, because they were Manassites, whom they emulated, and disdained that they should in anything be preferred before them, in a tumultuous way they assemble themselves together to quarrel and make war against them; whereas if they had not been wholly transported with those vicious and hellish furies of pride and envy, they would with all thankfulness have congratulated their victory, whereby they as well as others were delivered from the grievous oppressions of a common enemy.

*{and went northward}* i.e. After they had passed over Jordan, they marched with their army into Gilead (which was on the north of Ephraim), where Jephthah lived. See on 2 Sam. 18:6.

*{wherefore passedst thou over to fight}* i.e. Were not content to drive out the Ammonites out of your country, but also did invade their land, without making us acquainted with it? This was but a silly quarrel, had it been true; seeing herein they had been the more beholding unto them, for doing the work themselves, and saving them the labor, and freeing them from the dangers of it. But they backed their insolence and ingratitude with a lie: for v. 2, Jephthah tells them he had at the first called them unto their assistance, and had they come, they might have accompanied them in the whole expedition; but they refused.

**V. 2. ***{I and my people were at great strife}* i.e. The quarrel was chiefly between the Ammonites and us, and not between you and them; and out of my respect to you, as being our brethren, and out of the opinion I had of your valor, which was in such a case so needful for us, I called you in to our aid: but you helped us not, either out of your neglect of us, or else your fear to run the same hazard with us.

**V. 3. ***{I put my life in my hands}* Heb. *in my palm*. i.e. I exposed my life to great danger. A metaphor borrowed from one that carries a precious jewel in his open hand, which is easily snatched away; or a glass that is apt to fall out of it, and be broken. So 1 Sam. 19:5 and 28:21, Job 13:14, Psa. 119:109.

So Jotham expounds this metaphor, ch. 9:17, *My father fought for you, and adventured his life far*, etc., neither did Jephthah in this sense put his life into his hands, to lay it down, or deliver it up to the Ammonites, but only by fighting with them upon great disadvantages, in respect of his small army and their great multitude, he put it upon an hazard of a dangerous war.

*{and the Lord delivered them into my hand}* i.e. Confirmed my calling by crowning my attempts with victory: and why then do you, having no reason for it, quarrel against my proceedings, which God himself has approved by giving me such good success?

**V. 4. ***{Then Jephthah gathered together}* i.e. When no persuasions would move them to lay down their unjust quarrel, he prepares to stand in his own defense. But why did not Jephthah’s speech prevail with the Ephraimites as well as Gideon’s? Surely it was not because it was less prevalent in itself, or not backed with a strong reason; but partly because Gideon’s was more meek and humble, and so better pleased their proud humor, and partly because they had some glory of that victory by taking of the two kings, Zeeb and Oreb, and in that respect had the chief honor of the day by Gideon ascribed unto them, chap. 8:2, 3.

*{Ye Gileadites are fugitives &c.}* i.e. The refuse and scum of Ephraim and Manasseh, and so a mongrel generation out of the mixture of both, and therefore cast out of Canaan, as unworthy the privilege of dwelling there, and so fain to have your habitation upon the passages of Jordan, just between these two tribes, but with no esteem with either. And in this regard you ought not to have undertaken a matter of such importance as this war was, without our knowledge, you being so base, and we so noble and superior unto you.

**V. 5. ***{And the Gileadites took the passages of Jordan}* i.e. After the Ephraimites, being entered their country, were beaten in battle, and wholly routed and put to flight, they took these passages to hinder them from escaping into their own country, as the Ephraimites before had done on the other side Jordan to Zeb and Oreb, chap. 7:24.

*{Let me go over}* i.e. Pretending they were passengers of some other tribe going that way on their business.

*{If he said Nay}* To escape the danger, and save his life.

**V. 6. ***{Then said they unto him}* i.e. That they might put him upon the trial, and discover whether he were an Ephraimite or no, by his proper dialect and manner of pronunciation.

*{Say now, Shibboleth}* Which signifies a stream, ford, or watercourse, and so was a word fit for the present occasion, they desiring to pass over the fords of Jordan.

*{And he said, Sibboleth}* Speaking upon the sudden according to the dialect of his own country, unto which they were so accustomed and habituated that they could not well pronounce it otherwise; especially not knowing what they intended in putting him upon this trial. And thus it is with other nations, as the French and Irish, at this day, who in most words cannot pronounce the aspirate letter. But this was but a hard trial, especially upon life and death, seeing an innocent that had the infirmity of lisping or stuttering might so have perished.

**V. 7. ***{in one of the cities}* Hebr. *in the cities*. The like ellipsis we have 2 Chron. 35:24, 1 Sam. 18:21, Psa. 1:3, Zech. 9:9, John 1:5, Matth. 27:44, John 6:45, Rev. 18:17, 16.

**V. 8. ***{And after him Ibzan of Beth-lehem}* There were two cities of this name; one, in Judah, called Beth-lehem-judah; another, on the north borders of Zebulun, Josh. 19:15, which it seems is meant here; but if (as some think) this Ibzan were Boaz, then the other Bethlehem is here meant.

**V. 9. ***{thirty daughters, whom he sent abroad}* Out of his own family, bestowing them upon husbands in other families.

*{and took in thirty daughters from abroad for his sons}* To be their wives, and live with them in their father’s family. For the husband being the wife’s head, she is to follow him, and not the husband the wife.

*{And he judged Israel seven years}* Ibzan, it seems, as also the two judges following, living in peaceable times, and doing nothing of special note in the time of their government against foreign enemies, nothing is recorded of them; only they are thought worthy to be mentioned as judges, because they administered justice and judgment to the people, and maintained the purity of God’s true religion.

**V. 12. ***{in Aijalon}* Where the moon stood still, Josh. 10:12.

*{in the country of Zebulun}* This is added to distinguish it from other cities of the same name, as that in the tribe of Dan, ch. 1:35, and some others, 1 Chron. 6:69 and 8:13, and 2 Chron. 11:10 and 18:18.

**V. 13. ***{a Pirathonite}* Pirathon was a city upon a hill westward from Samaria and Shechem, where Benaiah David’s worthy was born, 2 Sam. 23:30.

**V. 15. ***{in the mount of the Amalekites}* The Amalekites’ own land lay without the borders of Canaan southeast, over against Egypt, at the Red Sea, by the desert of Havilah, as we may see by comparing 1 Sam. 15:7 with Exod. 17:8, but it seems some of that nation had their dwelling in Ephraim, if not, as some will have it, till the days of Saul, yet at least before the conquest of Canaan. And hence this mount bore their name. Some gather from chap. 5:14 that the inheritance of Ephraim extended to the country of the Amalekites.

## CHAPTER 13

**Verse 1. ***{Did evil again}* Heb. *added to commit*. i.e. Apostatized from God’s pure worship, and fell to idolatry. See on ch. 2:11 and 3:7 and 4:1 and 6:1 and 10:6.

*{into the hand of the Philistines}* To be subdued, vexed and oppressed by them.

*{forty years}* These forty years must not be so computed as to make an interregnum of that space between Abdon and Samson; for that would not stand with the chronology, 1 Kings 6:1. See the preface to this book, and on ch. 3:11. Neither can they be all meant of Samson’s time, who judged Israel only twenty years, ch. 15:20. But one twenty of them being allowed to Samson, the other twenty must be taken out of the times of other judges. And some will have them taken from the years of the precedent judges: and so the account reaches upward to the fifth year of Ibzan. Others, more probably, make them run down into the times of Eli and Samuel: for which they have these reasons: I. The over-numbers in this book are still reckoned to the following judges, who delivered the people from their troubles and oppressions. II. It must needs be that the Israelites’ sin, for which they were delivered into the hands of the Philistines forty years, was after the days of Abdon, seeing it is so plainly said, after the death of Abdon, *the children of Israel did evil again*. III. Under these three last judges it seems the people fell not into idolatry or troubles but lived in peace, and were not delivered into the hands of their enemies; whence it is that there is no mention made of any war in their times, nor of their victories, or deliverances of the people. IV. It is manifest that the Israelites were under the oppressions of the Philistines a great part of the time wherein Eli judged Israel, even till after his death Samuel succeeded him, 1 Sam. 7:9, 10, 11, 12. There is yet another way of computing these forty years of the Philistines’ tyranny, and that perhaps no less probable than any of the others; namely, by making them to begin at the fourteenth year of Jair, and end at Samson’s wedding, ch. 14. After which account Samson’s twenty years are no part of these forty, but immediately succeed them, and all that is related in this chapter happened in the days of the former judges.

**V. 2. ***{A certain man of Zorah}* A city on the west side of that hill which parts Judah and Dan, Josh. 15:33 and 19:41, in whose fields or territory it seems Manoah dwelt, and not in the city itself, v. 25 and ch. 16:31.

*{of the family}* i.e. Of the tribe. So Amos 3:1, Jer. 1:15 and 8:3 and 10:25, Mic. 2:3.

*{of the Danites}* Who bordering upon the land of the Philistines were most subject to their incursions and oppressions, and therefore the Lord raises up a judge out of this tribe to defend their country, and to quell their enemies; which he did with such courage and success that some think Jacob had respect to his victories, Gen. 49:16.

**V. 3. ***{And the angel of the Lord appeared}* Some think he was a created angel, because he would not have Manoah sacrifice unto him, but to the Lord, v. 16. Which reason is not good; for he says this, not because he was not God, but because Manoah conceived he was but a man. So our Savior taxes the man for calling him good, because there is none good but God, Matt. 19:17, speaking according to his opinion of him, who thought him to be a mere man. But it is very probable this was the Angel of the covenant, the eternal Word of his Father, who usually in those times appeared in human shape: for he is afterwards called *Elohim* by Manoah, and his wife calls him *Jehovah*, v. 23, and himself says his name was *secret* or *wonderful*, which is one of the names or titles given to our Lord Christ, Isai. 9:6. See on ch. 6:11.

*{Behold now thou art barren, &c.}* i.e. Unfruitful and unfit to bear or conceive a child in course of nature; but you shall have a son by virtue of God’s promise and covenant.

**V. 4. ***{and eat not any unclean thing}* Unclean meats were forbidden to all, Lev. 11, but wine and strong drink, and all that comes of the grape, moist or dry, from the kernels even to the husk, were forbidden to the Nazarites, Numb. 6:2, etc., by which was signified their renouncing of all sensual pleasures and worldly delights, that they might wholly devote themselves to the service of God. And these meats and drinks were forbidden to Samson’s mother, both while she was with child of him and while she gave him suck, because all that time he had his nourishment from her, and God had called him even from his conception to be a Nazarite unto him, unto whom such meats and drinks were unlawful. By which is implied that he himself much more should abstain from them when he came to his own diet, seeing they were inhibited to his mother only for his sake.

**V. 5. ***{no razor shall come on his head}* This law was given the Nazarites, Numb. 6:5, 1 Sam. 1:11, that the hair, which was always in their sight, might still put them in mind that they were consecrated to God, and therefore must not do anything unbeseeming their calling; and also to signify that they were mortified to the world, and contemned all earthly delicacy, and neglected the trimming and tricking of their bodies, that they might wholly intend God’s spiritual service.

*{For the child shall be a Nazarite}* The word signifies *one separate*, i.e. such an one as is separated from worldly and earthly things, that he may in a special manner attend God’s service. The name Pharisee is of the same signification, but not given of God, but arrogated out of pride and hypocrisy. Now the Nazarite was thus devoted either perpetually from the beginning of his life to his death, or only for a time. The former sort were set apart either by God himself, as Samson in this place, and John the Baptist, Luk. 1:15, or by their parents, as Samuel. The other were consecrated by their own special vow only for a time, as some months or years, at their own choice: and of these those laws concerning Nazarites, Numb. 6, are chiefly to be understood. In both we may observe the legal ceremonies required to their consecration, their outward carriage in their calling, and a moral equity consisting in this, that in special manner they did dedicate themselves to God’s service; and this did perpetually bind them to all due observance, even to the end of their life. Now Samson was a Nazarite by God’s special resignation and command, that he might begin to save his people. And herein he was above all Nazarites a singular type of Christ, and that in many respects. I. He was called, sealed and sanctified even from and in the womb, Luk. 1:35, Heb. 7:16. II. The end why he was thus set apart and designed was, that he might save and deliver his people out of the hands of all their enemies, Matth. 1:21. III. He performed (like Samson) this work of salvation in his own personal strength alone, without the help and assistance of any other, Isai. 63:1, 2, 3. IV. As Samson prevailed against his enemies in a manner without arms or weapons, so did Christ as was foretold, Hos. 1:7. V. Christ resembled Samson both in his birth and also in his death. For as Samson’s birth was above the course of nature, by virtue of God’s power and promise: so Christ also was born of promise, conceived in a supernatural and miraculous manner by the Holy Ghost; and as Samson slew more of his enemies at his death than he had done in all his lifetime: so likewise did Christ. See on Matth. 2:23.

*{and he shall begin to deliver Israel out of the hand of the Philistines}* Samson did only begin this deliverance, seeing the Philistines did lord it over the Israelites all his life, and it was continued by Samuel and Saul, and not perfected before David’s time. See 1 Sam. 7:13, 2 Sam. 21:22, 2 Kings 18:8. So Christ at his first coming began to redeem us from our enemies; at his second he shall complete his victory, 1 Cor. 15:25, 26, and our redemption, Rom. 8:23.

**V. 6. ***{A man of God}* i.e. An holy prophet sent of God. For the Scriptures usually honor God’s prophets and messengers with this title, Josh. 14:6, 1 Sam. 2:27, 1 Kings 13:1, etc., 1 Tim. 6:11.

*{very terrible}* i.e. Glorious, majestical, and fearful to frail mankind.

**V. 8. ***{and teach us what we shall do unto the child}* q.d. He has already showed what my wife shall do whilst she is with child; but our humble desire is, that he may again appear unto us to instruct us more fully and perfectly how we shall order the child when he is born.

**V. 10. ***{And the woman made haste and ran}* i.e. Knowing how earnestly her husband had desired and prayed, that the angel, or prophet, should come again to give them further instruction how to carry themselves towards the child according to the will of God, now when he appeared unto her again, she made all possible speed to acquaint her husband with it, that he himself might come and speak with him about it.

**V. 12. ***{Now let thy words come to pass}* This is a prayer of faith laying hold on the former promises.

**V. 14. ***{All that I command her let her observe}* i.e. Let her abstain from all those things which I forbade her whilst she is with child and gives suck, and afterwards take care that her son refrain from the use of them, seeing God has called and consecrated him as a Nazarite, unto whom in his law all those things are forbidden: therefore what has been inhibited unto her, he must also abstain from, seeing they are interdicted to her but for his sake. See v. 4, 5.

**V. 15. ***{until we have made ready a kid for thee}* Heb. *before thee*. Manoah thinking him to be a prophet, desiring to show him due respect by giving him entertainment and feasting him. The which he utterly refuses; not because angels appearing in human shape did not sometimes eat; for we see the contrary in those that appeared to Abraham, Gen. 18:8, and to Lot, Gen. 19:3. But he moves Manoah, that if he would needs be at that cost, he should bestow it in such a way as would be more acceptable to God, and profitable for himself, namely, by offering his kid for a sacrifice to God, to show his thankfulness for so great a benefit as he had promised unto him. And this he does also, both that he might take occasion hereby to show himself, that he was not a mere man, but the Angel of the covenant that had appeared unto him, by ascending up to heaven in the flame of the sacrifice, and withal that he might further confirm Manoah’s faith in the assurance of God’s promise.

**V. 16. ***{of thy bread}* i.e. Of any of your meat which you shall provide for me. For bread in Scripture usually signifies all manner of food fit for man’s nourishment. So 2 Kings 6:22, 23, Matth. 6:11.

*{thou must offer it unto the Lord}* Q. How could Manoah do this, seeing he was no priest, unto whom only it appertained to offer sacrifices? R. Because the Lord commanded him to do it, who might dispense with his own law. How was it lawful for Manoah to build an altar for sacrifice, seeing the law appointed there should be but one only altar for this use? R. One only was commanded in ordinary, but in extraordinary cases there might others be erected, as when God gave a special command for it, either by vision, as ch. 6:26, or by visible apparition, as here; or by a prophetical instinct of his spirit, as 1 Kings 18:30. Some think that command of offering upon one altar took not full effect, whilst the ark of God was in the tabernacle in a flitting condition from place to place, till it was settled in Solomon’s temple at Jerusalem; and they ground their opinion on Deut. 12:13, *Take heed to thyself that thou offer not thy burnt offerings in every place that thou seest, but in the place which the Lord shall choose in one of thy tribes, there thou shalt offer thy burnt offerings*. See on ch. 21:4.

**V. 17. ***{that when thy sayings come to pass we may do thee honour}* i.e. That we, and others unto whom we shall relate it, may do you honor, by acknowledging you a true prophet sent of God, and by presenting you with some gift to testify our thankfulness (so 1 Sam. 9:8, 1 Kings 14:3), which we cannot do unless we know what you are, where you dwell, and what your name is.

**V. 18. ***{Why askest thou thus after my name, seeing it is secret}* Or, *Wonderful*. The Hebrew word signifies both, and may here be taken in either sense. *My name is secret*, therefore not curiously to be inquired after, Deut. 26:29, and *wonderful*, i.e. so far above the reach of human understanding, that it is rather to be adored than searched into. This is not so true of any created angel as of the Angel of the covenant who is entitled *Wonderful*, Isa. 9:6, and whose name and nature, as he is God, is secret, wonderful and incomprehensible. And therefore he tells Manoah, that his name and nature were not curiously to be pried into, seeing he is not thus to be known, but only, and that according to our shallow capacity, by his word and works. And so he revealed himself unto Manoah and his wife in his wonderful actions presently afterwards, v. 19, 20. See Gen. 32:29.

**V. 19. ***{So Manoah took a kid}* See on v. 16.

*{unto the Lord}* As the Lord had directed him, v. 16.

**V. 20. ***{when the flame went up toward heaven}* Some think this flame was supernatural and miraculous, like that which at Elijah’s prayer came down from heaven, 1 Kings 18:38, or like that which arose out of the rock and consumed Gideon’s sacrifice, ch. 6:21, because nothing is said of fire or wood prepared by Manoah for this service. Others think (and I suppose more probably) that it was ordinary fire and wood from which this flame ascended, I. Because if it had been miraculous, it would have been noted to be so, as those others are, seeing miracles are not slighted over and smothered in silence. II. Because Manoah and his wife being in such a fright would have fallen upon their faces, as they afterwards did. III. Hereby they would have discovered the man to be an angel of God, whom they thought to be a prophet, till they saw him ascending in the flame.

*{the angel of the Lord ascended in the flame}* i.e. The great Angel of the covenant, who ascending up into heaven carries with him our prayers, our services and sacrifices, and putting them into the golden vials or censers full of the odors of his own merits and obedience, presents them unto God, unto whom they are acceptable being thus perfumed, Rev. 5:8 and 8:3. And this also evinces, that this was not a created angel, but our Lord Christ.

*{and fell on their faces to the ground}* Being affrighted with the sight of this miracle, and affected with the sense of their own frailty and mortality.

**V. 21. ***{Manoah knew that he was an angel of the Lord}* i.e. The eternal Word, the Angel of the covenant: which he calls *Elohim*, or God; and his wife, *Jehovah*, v. 22, 23.

**V. 22. ***{We shall surely die, because we have seen God}* See on ch. 6:22, Deut. 5:26, Exod. 33:20, Psa. 45:6, Gen. 32:30.

**V. 23. ***{If the Lord were pleased to kill us}* She uses three arguments to comfort her husband, and to strengthen his faith in this assurance, that they should not die, as he feared: I. God had accepted their sacrifice, as appears both by his commanding them to offer it (and he requires nothing but what is pleasing unto him) and also by his ascending in the flame of it, to carry it up as it were to heaven with him, that he might present it unto God. II. If he had purposed to kill them, he would not have respected them so much as to show them such a miraculous sign of his gracious favor, as was his ascending up in their sight in the flame of their sacrifice. III. He would not have promised them they should have a son who should begin to save Israel; nor have given directions how she should order herself and the child when he was born. All which must needs have been false and frivolous, if he had not intended they should live to execute what he had given them in charge.

**V. 24. ***{the Lord blessed him}* i.e. Endued him with an heroic spirit, invincible valor, miraculous strength, with all other gifts and graces befitting his calling.

**V. 25. ***{And the spirit of the Lord began to move him}* i.e. To inspire into him magnanimous thoughts, and to endue him with miraculous strength of body, thereby inciting him to attempt, and enabling him to execute wonderful and more than human acts, only fit for him that was so called to be such a deliverer of God’s people.

*{in the camp of Dan}* This place was near unto Hebron and had its name either from that expedition of the Danites, ch. 18:11, 12 (and then the things recorded in that story were done before the days of Samson, though related afterwards; see on ch. 17:1), or else because there the Danites at this present had their standing camp, to preserve their country from the inroads and vastations of the Philistines. And here Samson in his youth had occasion to show his strength and valor.

*{Zorah}* See v. 2.

*{Eshtaol}* A city in the tribe of Dan; westward towards the great sea. See Joshua 15:33 and 19:41, 1 Chron. 2:53.

## CHAPTER 14

**Verse 1. ***{And Samson went down to Timnath}* See Gen. 38:12, Josh. 15:10, 2 Chron. 28:18. This city lay westward from Zorah, toward the Mediterranean, and was at first allotted to Judah, Josh. 15:57, but afterward by lot given to Dan, Josh. 19:43. But the Danites not being able, it seems, to drive out the inhabitants, the Philistines, they lived there still (as is very probable) until and long after Samson’s time. Unto this city Samson went. For though the Israelites and the Philistines had oftentimes war one with another, yet now the Philistines having gotten the better, and brought them into subjection upon condition of paying tribute, or otherwise, they now lived in peace, and married and traded together: so that Samson might with safety go down to Timnath, though inhabited by the Philistines.

**V. 2. ***{now therefore get her for me to wife}* i.e. Because I dare not marry a wife without your consent, who are my parents, though I much like and love her, therefore I pray you let it stand with your liking, and use your best endeavors to procure her for me. Hereby it appears, that even in those days, which were times of ignorance and much profaneness, even by the dim light of nature and of the law their consciences were convinced of this truth, that children ought not to marry without consent of their parents, Gen. 21:21 and 24:3 and 26:34, 35.

**V. 3. ***{Is there never a woman among the daughters of thy brethren}* This is not to be taken literally and precisely (for it had been unlawful for him to have married his brother’s daughter) but, as it is commonly used by the Hebrews, for one of the same kindred: q.d. You have choice enough, and are no ways necessitated to marry a Philistine: for you might marry one of your kindred and allies, or if there were none of them, yet one of the same tribe, or if also there were never a Danite to be found, you should rather choose any that pleases you among all the people of Israel, than seek a wife among strangers.

*{the uncircumcised Philistines}* The ground of their refusal was because God had straitly forbidden his people to make marriages with these cursed nations, of which the Philistines were a part, Deut. 7:3, Exod. 34:16. And if it were unlawful generally for the Israelites thus to marry, then how much more unbeseeming him who was a Nazarite, and devoted to God’s service in a special manner; from which a heathen and infidel wife would withdraw him, 1 Cor. 7:32, 2 Cor. 6:14. Now they call them uncircumcised by way of scorn and reproach, because such were slighted and vilified by the people of Israel, 1 Sam. 14:6 and 17:26, 36, Gen. 34:14. And that they might the more strongly dissuade him from this match, seeing if he had any respect either to the law of God or his own honor and reputation amongst his people, he had no reason to think of it.

*{for she pleaseth me well}* Heb. *she is right in my eyes*: i.e. I so like her person that I have set my heart and affection upon her. Wherein as nature had its work, so there concurred a secret operation of God’s spirit, carrying him on towards the effecting of what he intended should come to pass.

**V. 4. ***{knew not that it was of the Lord}* See 1 Kings 12:15, 2 Chron. 10:15 and 22:7 and 25:20.

*{that he sought an occasion against the Philistines}* Some conceive these words to be spoken of the Lord, that he sought an occasion to revenge the wrongs the Philistines had offered his people, and therefore in his wise providence inclined Samson’s heart to take this occasion to fall at enmity with them, that out of their private quarrel he might proceed to public and hostile actions suitable to his calling. As of old God had made use of the malice and envy of the patriarchs for the accomplishment of Joseph’s dreams and his own decrees, Gen. 50:20, so here he uses the youthful affection that Samson bore to this Timnathite as a means or occasion of bringing to pass his own purposes of being avenged on the Philistines. But then the speech seems improper, that the Lord should seek an occasion by this means of revenge, seeing he might justly punish them at his own pleasure, either himself, or how and by whom he would. Therefore others, and that very probably, understand it to be spoken of Samson, that he sought this occasion against the Philistines, to revenge the cause of God’s people upon them. For though in respect of the public there was cause enough to do it, seeing the Philistines not only possessed a great part of that inheritance which God had given his people, and out of which they were straitly charged to dispossess them, but also had made war against them and held them under subjection, yet this was no plea for Samson in his private and personal way of taking revenge; they having, in respect of the public, concluded a peace between them, unless he had some just cause and quarrel to revenge himself, and the people of God together, upon occasion of some great wrong offered unto him in respect of his own particular interest. But against this exposition there is also a scruple to be removed, namely, if it were his end and aim to seek an occasion of revenging the cause of God’s people in a private way, how is it said that he desired to marry her out of his great love and liking of her person, v. 3, and that she pleased him well, v. 7, especially considering that he not only said so, but also evidenced the truth of his love by his carriage towards her, till she had justly incensed him by betraying his secret to her countrymen, to his great prejudice, and also afterwards by returning unto her, intending to present her with a kid, when that wrong was digested, and his wrath appeased, chap. 15:1.

*{for at that time the Philistines had dominion over Israel}* This is added as a reason why Samson sought a private occasion to be avenged of the Philistines, namely, because he would take the whole blame upon himself, and not exasperate them against his whole nation, seeing they had dominion over them, and therefore would revenge themselves upon them, if they saw they had any hand in Samson’s actions against them. See ch. 15:9–12.

**V. 5. ***{and his father and mother to Timnath}* They had now changed their minds, and yielded to their son’s desires, either being persuaded by some reasons he had given them, that what he did was of the Lord, and that he was moved thereunto by some secret revelation or special instinct of his spirit, or else being moved by their natural affection to please and give him content, though the thing he desired were contrary to their own judgment and liking.

*{and behold, a young Lion roared against him}* Being gone aside from his father and mother (as is implied v. 8), upon some occasion not expressed, this lion meeting him, as the original has it, roared, as they used to do after their prey.

**V. 6. ***{And the spirit of the Lord came mightily upon him}* i.e. Inspired him in an extraordinary and supernatural manner with courage and strength, that by this essay he might confirm him in his calling (as it befell David in the like case, 1 Sam. 17:34) and to encourage him to take up enmity against the Philistines, trusting upon God’s power assisting him, and that he might have matter supplied for his riddle, which proved the occasion and cause of their quarrel.

*{and he rent him as he would have rent a Kid}* i.e. He tore in pieces this fierce strong lion with as little difficulty as if he had been a tender weak kid not able to make any resistance.

*{and he had nothing in his hand}* i.e. Neither weapon nor staff, but did it only with his bare hands. Herein Samson was a notable type of Christ vanquishing and triumphing over Satan that roaring lion, 1 Pet. 5:8, Heb. 2:14, by the sole virtue of his own power.

*{but he told not his father or his mother what he had done}* Because he would not have them speak of his heroic act, manifesting his strength and courage, among the Philistines at Timnath, which might tend to his prejudice, by filling them with jealousies and fears.

**V. 7. ***{And he went down}* Namely, being accompanied with his father and mother.

*{and talked with the woman}* Concluding on the marriage between them, and the time when it should be solemnized.

**V. 8. ***{And after a time he returned to take her}* He came accompanied with his parents, v. 9, to solemnize the marriage which they had agreed upon, v. 7. This some say was a type of the calling of the Gentiles; Hos. 2:19.

*{and he turned aside to see the carcase}* Namely, out of the common way into the place where he had slain the lion.

*{and behold, there was a swarm of bees, and honey in the carcase}* Which came to pass by the special providence of God, acting this in a supernatural way. For it was never known among naturalists that bees should breed in the carcass of a lion, but only of an ox or bullock; or though they should, yet it cannot be imagined that in an ordinary and natural way the carcass should be consumed, the bees bred, and the honey made in so short a time as we can reasonably think was between the concluding of the marriage and the solemnizing of it.

**V. 9. ***{but he told not them}* Neither where he had the honey, nor of the killing of the lion, partly because he would not have it reported among the Philistines, and partly that keeping it secret it might be the subject of his riddle: v. 16.

**V. 10. ***{So his father went down}* To wit, as the chief of his company; but with him his mother went also and the chief of his friends.

*{and Samson made there a feast}* i.e. A wedding feast, lasting seven days, as young men or their parents for them used to do in those times when they married wives, Gen. 29:22.

**V. 11. ***{And it came to pass, when they saw him, that they brought thirty companions to be with him}* i.e. When in a special manner they observed him, his person, stature, strength, countenance, posture, and carriage, all very remarkable, they brought thirty companions, in outward show and semblance to honor him, but indeed to have an eye over him, and to provide for their own safety whatsoever should happen. This I think to be the most probable exposition and so it is approved by the most: for it is expressly said, that when they saw him, they chose thirty companions; and though they had seen him before and were not afraid, that is to be understood of his wife’s father, family, and some special friends, who saw him when he came as a suitor; and not of all these guests which were now come to the marriage. And though they had all seen him before and were not afraid of him, yet it does not follow but that now they might, looking then upon him in a slight and ordinary manner, but now observing him more seriously and advisedly. And though the text makes no mention of anything extraordinary and remarkable in Samson’s person which might cause fear in the beholders, yet it follows not that therefore no such thing was observable in him, seeing the Scriptures do not mention every particular. And though he acted his famous exploits when the Spirit of God came upon him and enabled him thereunto, yet I make little doubt but he had both natural stature and strength very observable above the most men: neither does the principal cause take away the inferior causes and means, but usually works by them. And whereas it may be said that Samson purposely kept secret his killing of the lion lest the Philistines should be afraid of him, seeing his strength and courage; this strongly argues that they had him in jealousy, and were apt to fear him, seeing he was so careful to conceal that from them. And though they knew not his strength by his killing the lion, yet they might suspect it when they observed his person and carriage, and also it is very probable they had heard some famous exploits done by him when the spirit moved him at times in the camp of Dan, ch. 13:25. Besides, it is not likely that though he should have bridemen to grace his wedding they should attend him in so great a number. And if indeed it had been for Samson’s honor to have had such a train, then they should have been chosen by himself out of his own people and familiar friends, and not put upon him by strangers and reconciled enemies.

**V. 12. ***{And Samson said unto them, I will now put forth a riddle unto you}* A riddle is a dark speech, in which something lies hidden under the literal sense, that is to be found out by the wit of the hearers. And this was a pleasant and innocent exercise of the wit, fit for feasts, to keep the guests from worse employments, Ezek. 17:2, 1 Kings 10:1. And being, as it seems, usual in those times, it was thought therefore fit by Samson to be propounded to his companions, that under the colorable show of custom, he might seek an occasion of having a just quarrel against them: and therefore surely he took them not for friends that came to honor his wedding, but for an ill-affected guard that were set to watch him. Samson’s propounding a riddle to the Philistines, some will have to be a type of the gospel, which is a dark and mysterious doctrine to flesh and blood, and was preached to the world at the time of the calling of the Gentiles: 1 Cor. 2:7.

*{within the seven days of the feast}* The usual time for the keeping of marriage feasts, Gen. 29:27.

*{Then I will give you}* As a reward of your wit and industry.

*{thirty sheets}* Or, *shirts*. That is, fine linen clothes, which in those east countries they used for sundry purposes, both by day and night. Of this we read, Matt. 27:59, Mark 14:51. So here the same is used in the Greek.

*{and thirty changes of garments}* i.e. Upper vestments, which they often changed, putting them on and off according to their occasions, as we do our cloaks or gowns. See on Gen. 45:22.

**V. 13. ***{ye shall give me}* By way of penalty for your dullness and ignorance. So the condition was on both sides equal.

*{And they said unto him, Put forth thy riddle}* q.d. Accept of my wager, therefore propound your riddle.

**V. 14. ***{Out of the eater}* i.e. Which lives by prey, and devours all, and is food for none, *came forth meat*, fit to preserve the life of others.

*{and out of the strong}*, i.e. the strongest of beasts that live by prey, *came forth sweetness*, i.e. that which is most sweet. Which several branches signify the same things, to wit, the lion and honey, but it may be were in the riddle purposely repeated, that they might distract and disturb their mind and judgment in finding out the meaning, whilst they imagined they were several things. An express figure we here have of the sweet and wholesome food of the soul delivered from the death of Christ, by the which the devil and death are both destroyed by him; Joh. 6:51, Heb. 2:14.

*{And they could not in three days expound the riddle}* i.e. They tired their wits in vain for the three first days of the feast to find out the meaning: and from thence to the seventh day they began to work with Samson’s wife by incitements; but not prevailing with her, or rather she not prevailing with her husband, on the seventh day they provoke the uttermost of her endeavors by terrors and threats, which though (being so terrible) they meant not to execute, seeing she gave them not the least cause of offense, but did her best to satisfy their desire, yet they used this spur to put her on the utmost of her power and skill, that she might prevail with her husband. Thus Satan employed Adam’s, Job’s, Salomon’s wives to tempt their husbands.

**V. 15. ***{And it came to pass on the seventh day}* i.e. When the last hour approached in which they must make known the meaning of the riddle or else lose their wager.

*{that he may declare unto us the riddle}* i.e. That making it known to you, you might declare the meaning of it unto us. Thus the mysteries of the gospel can never be comprehended by man’s natural understanding; but must be revealed by the Spirit of Christ, 1 Cor. 2:10, and the ministry of the church.

*{have ye called us to take that we have?}* Heb. *to possess us*, or *to impoverish us*. i.e. Have you, your father and friends, under color of love invited us to your feast, that you may make a prey of us to our undoing? Though the share of every particular man in this loss came but to a shirt and an upper garment, yet they account themselves utterly undone, and in the meantime (so partial they were and full of self-love) they regard not Samson’s case, who was to lose thirty for their one, if he miscarried in his wager.

**V. 16. ***{Behold, I have not told it my father nor my mother}* i.e. Whom I ought most to reverence, and of whose piety and faithfulness I have had long experience; *and shall I tell it thee*, although being my wife you are in a nearer relation, seeing I have been but a little while acquainted with you, and have small knowledge of your fidelity and secrecy?

**V. 17. ***{And she wept before him the seven days}* Or, *unto the seventh day*; or, *the rest of the seven days*, that is, from the day when his thirty companions began to employ her, which was the fourth day of the feast: for three days they exercised their own wits to find the riddle out, and when they saw they could not do it, they came unto her, craving her help, v. 14.

*{And it came to pass on the seventh day that he told her, because she lay sore upon him}* i.e. He was so vexed and overburdened with her importunity and tears that he could no longer endure and bear it. And thus he who was able by his valor and strength to rend a fierce lion with ease, and to subdue and slay thousands of his enemies, was vanquished with the tears and importunity of a woman! Which shows that the most martial men may be and often are, venereous and uxorious.

*{and she told the riddle}* i.e. The meaning of the riddle, to some of Samson’s thirty companions.

**V. 18. ***{And the men of the City}* i.e. Samson’s companions which were chosen out of the city to accompany him at the marriage feast.

*{What is sweeter than honey?}* They do not say that by the eater and the strong is understood a lion, and by meat and sweetness honey, but only in two words express the meaning.

*{If ye had not plowed with my heifer}* i.e. If my wife had not unfaithfully discovered my secret, being persuaded hereunto by your subtlety, or terrified with your threats, you had never by your own wit found out the meaning. He uses an allegory taken from husbandmen, who in plowing, by the labor of their beasts, find things hidden under the earth, when the plow turns it up; and hereby he taxes both his wife’s perfidiousness, and, as some think, her lightness and overmuch familiarity with them (in which respect he resembled her to a young, wanton and untamed heifer) and withal, their fraud and want of wit.

**V. 19. ***{And the Spirit of the Lord came upon him}* See on v. 6.

*{and he went down to Askelon}* A city belonging to Judah and Simeon, ch. 1:18, but now possessed by the Philistines, and one of their chief and metropolitan cities, 1 Sam. 6:17, Amos 1:8. But it was in such a great distance from Timnath that we can give no reason why Samson went so far to do this exploit, but only this, that the Spirit of God which inspired him to do it, did also by its secret motion carry him thither.

*{and slew thirty men of them}* This was lawful for him to do, not only because they were of those cursed nations which God long before had sentenced to death and destruction, but also because he was called now of God to be a judge, to deliver his people, and to avenge them on their enemies, and the Spirit of God came upon him and excited him to do it.

*{and took their spoil}* i.e. Stripped them of their apparel when they were dead. How could Samson do this, seeing he was a Nazarite, unto whom it was unlawful to touch a dead body? Numb. 6:6. R. He might lawfully do it, being inspired and acted by the Spirit of God, who when he pleases may dispense with his own law. And further, that law that inhibits the touching of a dead body may seem to have been peculiar to such as were Nazarites by vow only for a time, and not to have concerned such as were called by God to continue so for term of life, of which kind Samson was; because the other might easily observe it for a time, but not these for their whole life; and if the other were defiled, there were means appointed for their cleansing, Num. 6:9, 10, but not so for perpetual Nazarites. Hence it is, that in Samson’s consecration we find mention of meats, wines and strong drinks, and also that no razor should come upon his head, but nothing spoken of touching the dead.

*{and his anger was kindled}* i.e. Both against his thirty companions, and also against his wife, because she had showed far greater love to them than to him, seeing she had rather that he alone should bear the whole loss, than that every one should bear a little. And this anger he expressed by a temporary separation from her, but he intended not, as appears by the sequel, a full and final divorce.

**V. 20. ***{But Samson’s wife was given}* That is, in marriage, to his companion, i.e. one of the thirty Philistines, whom in the marriage feast he had chosen for his friend, namely, the first and chief brideman, who was nearest unto him, according to the custom of those times and countries; unto which, as some think, John Baptist alludes, Joh. 3:29.

## CHAPTER 15

**Verse 1. ***{In the time of wheat harvest}* Which in that hot country was in April and May. Hereof mention is made, because this time gave opportunity to Samson of burning their corn, which being ripe and dry, was partly standing, and partly cut down in the sheaf and shock.

*{Samson visited his wife with a kid}* i.e. Went to visit her with a desire and purpose to be reconciled unto her, having now digested his former wrong. And to this end he carried a kid with him, as a testimony of his love, that they might with their friends in her father’s family feast together.

*{and he said, I will go in to my wife}* i.e. He said so within himself, or being come to the house, professed so much to her father, that he would go into his wife’s chamber. For in those days the women had their chambers peculiar to themselves, Gen. 24:67 and 31:33.

**V. 2. ***{I verily thought}* Heb. *Saying I said*; i.e. I said in my heart and certainly concluded. So Gen. 20:11, Exod. 2:14, Psa. 94:18.

*{that thou hadst utterly hated her}* To wit, because you went away from her in wrath and displeasure, Chap. 14:19.

*{is not her younger sister fairer?}* Heb. *better*.

*{take her}* Heb. *let her be thine*. So to please Samson, he offers him an incestuous match; which he accepted not, Lev. 18:18.

**V. 3. ***{Now shall I be more blameless than the Philistines [Or, now shall I be blameless from the Philistines] though I do them a displeasure}* To wit, because by this wrong they have given me just cause to take revenge. So verse 11. See Chap. 14:4.

**V. 4. ***{And Samson went and caught three hundred foxes}* This will not seem a matter of so great difficulty as some may imagine, if we consider that the land of Canaan abounded with foxes, Cant. 2:15, Neh. 4:3, Psa. 63:10, Lam. 5:18, the which Samson might take in nets and snares, both by his own industry and the help of others, especially seeing the work he had in hand was inspired into him by God’s Spirit, who by his providence could easily supply what was wanting in outward means, being the supreme Lord and of all the creatures, and having them at his command, Gen. 6:20, Psa. 50:11, Exod. 8:5, 16 and 10:12, Numb. 11:31, could easily cause them to fall into his snares. Some refer these foxes to the disciples, Luk. 10:1, some, to the incendiaries intimated Luk. 12:49, some, to heretics, who though opposite to one another yet combine together to waste the church. Whatever mystery may lie hid in this action, certainly this revenge of Samson’s was very suitable to the Philistines’ offense: they by wiles and violence had extorted his secret, and taken his wife from him, and he by force combined wily creatures together to do them a spite.

*{and took firebrands}* Or, *torches*.

*{and turned tail to tail, and put a firebrand in the midst}* i.e. He fastened them one to another by tails with cords, having a firebrand in the middle, lest being frightened with the fire they should have run into their holes; which they would have done, had they not been thus hampered, rather than into the fields; whereas now shunning the fire at their tails, they tugged each other into the fields, and so set on fire both the standing corn, and that also which being cut down was in the sheaf and shock. In all which we are not so much to look upon these things with the eye of reason in a natural way, and so to describe the manner how it was acted, as to God’s providence, who at his pleasure ordered and disposed of all as was most fit for the effecting of his will: otherwise it were hard to conceive, though they burnt up the dry corn, how they could also burn the vineyards and olives.

**V. 6. ***{Then the Philistines said, Who hath done this?}* i.e. The Philistines of other cities, whose corn was burnt up, and not of Timnath, as appears by the answer to their question.

*{And the Philistines came up, and burnt her and her father with fire}* As having by their unjust dealing been the causes and occasions of all their losses, he by his giving Samson’s wife to another, and she by giving her consent thereunto, and by her treachery to her husband. Where it is very observable, that she brought upon herself and her father’s house that misery and mischief which she thought to prevent by her wicked discovery of her husband’s secret, chap. 14:15, according to Prov. 10:24 and 11:8, Hos. 8:7.

**V. 7. ***{Though ye have done this, yet I will be avenged of you, &c.}* i.e. Though you may seem to have done me a pleasure in revenging my just quarrel, yet seeing you have done it with a wicked mind and in an unjust and tumultuous manner, having respect herein only to your own losses, this shall not serve your turn; but for all this I will not desist till I be thoroughly avenged of you; and then I will for a time, till you have given me some other just occasion of further revenge. In which speech we must not look upon Samson as a private man revenging his own quarrel, but as judge of Israel, called and appointed of God to deliver his people from their oppressions, and to avenge them on their enemies: otherwise these his resolutions could not have been lawful, seeing the nations of the Philistines had not given unto him in particular any just cause.

**V. 8. ***{And he smote them hip and thigh with a great slaughter}* A proverbial expression signifying the mortal wounding of their bodies in all places where his blows light, or the total ruin of all their strength and abilities. Such phrases soldiers now have, as pell-mell, and *cap-à-pie*. See Deut. 28:35, *The Lord shall smite thee in the knees and in the legs with a sore botch that cannot be healed, from the sole of thy foot to the top of thy head*. So Isa. 9:14, *The Lord shall cut off from Israel head and tail, branch and rush*. Some conceive by hip and thigh to be meant, that he made this great slaughter without weapons, only kicking them with his foot, like cowards, out of scorn and disdain, or knocking and pushing them with his knee: others think this phrase signifies the same that *manibus pedibusque* does in Latin, and implies the violence and vehemence of Samson’s conflict. The Chaldee paraphrast interprets it, that *he slew both horse and foot*.

*{and he went down}* To wit, from Timnath.

*{and dwelt in the top of the rock Etam}* Etam was a city situate on the south of Judah upon a strong and high rock near the brook Etam, and belonged to Simeon, whose inheritance was mingled with Judah’s, Josh. 19:1. Hither for safety Samson retired himself, that he might not suddenly be surprised by the Philistines, who in all likelihood would seek to revenge themselves upon him, after he had thus slaughtered many of their nation. See 1 Chron. 4:32 and 2 Chron. 11:6.

**V. 9. ***{Then the Philistines went up, and pitched in Judah}* Because Samson, whom they went to surprise, was come to Etam, in Judah’s lot, and because of the land of Israel, which they came to invade, Judah was nearest unto them, a great part of the Philistines’ country being in their lot, Josh. 15:45.

*{and spread themselves}* This implies they were a great army that came in this expedition, as thinking all the Israelites were partakers with Samson in that offense which he had given them, either setting him on and counseling him to it, or abetting and countenancing him in it, or at least conniving at it, and not apprehending and punishing him for it: and so imagining they would rise in his defense, they raised a great army against them, making his private act a cause of public quarrel between both the nations.

*{in Lehi}* Signifying a jawbone. It was a place in the Philistines’ country bordering upon Judah, and is here called Lehi by anticipation; for Samson afterwards gave it this name upon occasion of that slaughter which he had made with the jawbone of an ass, verse 17.

**V. 10. ***{Why are ye come up against us?}* q.d. We have given you no cause to make war against us, seeing we live in subjection to you, pay our tribute, and observe all covenants of peace made between us.

*{And they answered, to bind Samson are we come up}* i.e. We will have nothing to do with you, but only with Samson, who has greatly wronged us, unless by protecting him you make his offense yours, and so draw upon yourselves our just revenge.

*{to do to him as he hath done to us}* i.e. Proceeding in a common course of justice and equity, to put him to death, who has slaughtered so many of us. Therefore deliver him into our hands, that hereby it may appear you are not guilty of his faults, and we will depart and no further trouble you.

**V. 11. ***{what is this that thou hast done unto us?}* i.e. In what a labyrinth of troubles and dangers have you involved us, by incensing the Philistines against us who are under their subjection?

**V. 12. ***{We are come down to bind thee}* i.e. Though we have no quarrel against you for anything done by you in respect of ourselves, more than this, that you have incensed the Philistines against us, who are able to do us mischief, yet we must bind and deliver you into their hands, to give them satisfaction, appease their wrath, and turn away their army from us.

*{Swear unto me that ye will not fall upon me yourselves}* He does not expostulate with them, as he justly might, nor upbraid them with their cowardice and faintheartedness, not daring to join with him whom God had called to be their judge and deliverer; nor with their base treachery in betraying him into the hands of their common enemies, but only desires to be secured that they themselves would do him no mischief, lest if they refused to do this, he might thereby have just cause to turn his strength against them in a way of just revenge, and stand it out in his own defense: where he shows his great love towards them, and care of their safety as well as his own, seeing he was solicitous to prevent any hurt which might befall them either from himself or Philistines, choosing rather to be bound to give them satisfaction than they should be exposed to their revenge if they refused it. Now as Samson abode to be bound by his own countrymen, that his freeing of himself and his victory over his enemies might be the more glorious; so Christ, though he could have called in twelve legions of angels to his rescue, yea with a word laid his enemies on the ground, yet was willing to be bound, that his triumph might be the greater.

**V. 13. ***{but surely we will not kill}* This asseveration it is very likely they ratified with an oath, as he required, to give the better satisfaction, and make him the more willingly submit to be bound by them.

*{And they bound him with two new cords}* And so the stronger and more hardly broken. Herein Samson was a type of Christ, who was bound by the Jews, and delivered up to death by the hands of the Gentiles, Matth. 27:2.

*{and brought him up from the rock}* i.e. To Lehi, where the Philistines were encamped; that so by laying all the blame upon Samson, and exposing him to extreme danger free themselves from them whom they feared.

**V. 14. ***{the Philistines shouted against him}* To wit, for joy, because their chief enemy was fallen unto their hands.

*{and the spirit of the Lord}* i.e. The spirit of strength, courage, and earnest desire to fight.

*{and the cords—became as flax [or, as threads made of flax] that was burnt [or, scorched] with fire}* i.e. Were as easily broken by his irresistible strength as threads of burnt or singed flax.

**V. 15. ***{And he found a new jawbone of an ass}* i.e. Which had not been long dead, and was therefore the stronger and tougher. Thus the rude unlearned tongues of the apostles prevailed over many thousand.

**V. 16. ***{With the jawbone of an ass heaps upon heaps}* Heb. *an heap, two heaps*. i.e. By this weak means and contemptible instrument, through God’s almighty power and assistance, I have slain a thousand his and of his people’s enemies, and laid their dead bodies on heaps one upon another.

**V. 17. ***{called that place Ramath-Lehi}* That is, *The lifting up of the jawbone*, or, *The casting away of the jawbone*.

**V. 18. ***{And he was sore athirst}* His great labor and pains in the fight by heating his body made him dry and thirsty: besides which natural cause there might also be a special hand of God in it, that hereby he might humble him, and keep him from being proud of so great a victory achieved by himself alone. And thus God usually deals with his servants whom he has used as instruments in great and glorious employments, knowing how prone we are to fall into this odious sin of pride, if his grace by such means as these do not restrain and humble us. This thirst of Samson’s after his victory some make a type of that of our Savior’s about the end of his passion, Joh. 19:28.

*{and called on the Lord}* i.e. Being destitute of all other help in a place where there was no hope of finding any water, he seeks unto God as all-sufficient, craving relief from him in his great extremity.

*{Thou hast given this great deliverance}* He ascribes the whole victory unto God, and gives him all the glory of it; and withal, makes it a ground of strength in his faith in this assurance, that God would hear his prayer: for he that had done the greater, in giving him such a glorious victory, would not deny him the less, but either take away his thirst, or give him some means to quench it.

*{into the hand of thy servant}* i.e. Who in all this fight have been but your instrument to perform that service which you would have done, the which I have faithfully performed, though to the extreme peril of my life; and therefore contracting such a calamity upon me in such an employment, and by my faithful walking in my calling, I cannot but hope that you will relieve and refresh me.

*{and now shall I die for thirst, and fall into the hand of the uncircumcised}* q.d. Certainly I shall not, or surely I can by no means believe it, seeing this would blast and black all the glory of the former action, and spoil God of the honor due unto him, seeing the enemies would be ready to say it was not his power that gave the victory, for then he would now have been as able to keep Samson from perishing by thirst, or from falling through faintness into the power of his enemies. See Heb. 11:32, 2 Sam. 17:26, 36, 2 Sam. 1:20.

**V. 19. ***{But God clave a hollow place}* Hebr. *a mortar*. i.e. An hollow place in figure like a mortar. See Prov. 27:22.

*{that was in the jaw}* Or, *Lehi*. Not in the jawbone itself, but in the place called Lehi or Jawbone on this occasion, did this spring arise.

*{and when he had drunk, his spirit}* i.e. His vital spirits, now well-nigh wasted and spent with labor, heat and thirst.

*{came again}* Which was ready to depart, and so he to die, by reason of this thirst.

*{and he revived}* Being thus refreshed, he became lively, strong and vigorous.

*{wherefore he called the name thereof}* i.e. Not of the jawbone, but of the spring in Lehi.

*{En-hakkore}* That is, *The well of him that called*, or, *cried*. This name he gave it in memorial of God’s great mercy, and to testify his thankfulness both for his giving him so great a victory over his enemies, and also for his miraculous raising up of his spring to refresh his fainting spirits, when he was ready to die of thirst, and for a perpetual monument of the efficacy of prayer.

*{which is in Lehi unto this day}* i.e. The time when this story was written, which was many years after these things were acted. And therefore the spring of water was not in the jawbone, which every passenger that saw it might carry away, and would surely have done it, especially in those far countries, in which they had so much need of a fountain of water, but the place called Lehi upon this occasion.

**V. 20. ***{And he judged Israel}* i.e. He revenged their wrongs upon the Philistines, and so in some degree gave them deliverance, though he did not fully free them from their tyranny. See on ch. 13:5.

*{in the days of the Philistines}* i.e. When they held the Israelites in subjection, and tyrannized over them.

*{twenty years}* Ch. 16:31.

## CHAPTER 16

**Verse 1. ***{Then went Samson to Gaza}* Gaza lay in that part of the country towards the Mediterranean which was allotted to Simeon and Judah, but was one of the greatest and most famous cities of the Philistines, therefore Samson could not go thither without great danger, having lately slaughtered such a multitude of them. But it seems he had some weighty occasion drawing him, which is not here expressed; he resolved to go thither secretly, relying either on his secrecy, or on his courage and strength, if he happened to be discovered; or else he did it out of contempt of his enemies, who he had found by experience were not able to stand in his hands. Of this city see on chap. 1:18, Amos 1:6, Zeph. 2:4.

*{and saw there an harlot}* He went not to Gaza purposely to see this harlot, as some have conceited, but being come thither, and accidentally setting his eye on her, he was inveigled with her beauty; and loving, or rather lusting after her, he committed uncleanness and folly with her, Gen. 16:2 and 38:16, 18. A weakness no less to be wondered at than his great strength, that he who so gloriously overcame whole multitudes of men should so basely suffer women to overcome him, that he who would not drink wine should be drowned in lust.

**V. 2. ***{they compassed him in}* i.e. As some think, they environed the house where he lodged, in some distance, that they might not be discovered; or, as others, hearing that he was secretly come to the city, and not knowing where he harbored, they set a strong watch at the city gate through which he was to return, that they might suddenly kill him before he was aware at his going out, which lay quiet and silent all night, lest he hearing any noise or tumult might grow suspicious that he was discovered and betrayed.

**V. 3. ***{And Samson lay till midnight, and arose}* i.e. Having satisfied his lust, he thinks of providing for his safety, and suspecting that the next day’s light might discover him, he rises at midnight, and prepares to be gone.

*{and took the doors of the gate of the City}* i.e. Finding the gate locked, barred and bolted to prevent his escape, he pulled up the two posts upon which it hanged, and carried all away, posts and gate and bar, upon his shoulders, the watchmen (as is most probable) being all run away affrighted with his sudden approach in that dead season of the night. Wherein Samson was a type of Christ, who rising again after he had vanquished sin and Satan, death and hell, maugre all opposition and means used to prevent it, did strike the soldiers appointed for a watch with fear and astonishment, and did go away in triumph, carrying with him the trophies of his victory, Isai. 63:1, 2, 3, Act. 2:24.

*{and carried them up to the top of an hill that is before Hebron}* i.e. Not to the hill adjoining to Hebron; for that was above twenty miles from Gaza, and it is not probable he would carry such a burden so far, no reason appearing that should induce him to it; but to a hill that lies eastwards from Gaza, from whence one might see Hebron seated upon another hill right over against it.

**V. 4. ***{And it came to pass afterward that he loved a woman}* i.e. Having once defiled and drenched his soul in the deep pit of sin and uncleanness, Prov. 22:14 and 23:27, contrary to the profession of a true Israelite, much more of a pure and holy Nazarite, instead of rising out of it by unfeigned repentance, he sinks deeper and deeper into this whirlpool, and relapses again into the same sin.

*{in the valley of Sorek}* Which is called the grape-valley, from the abundance of the best grapes growing there. Some read *by the brook Sorek*, which runs along by the valley of Eshcol, where those grapes grew, Deut. 1:24. And some think that these are both one, Num. 13:23, 24.

*{whose name was Delilah}* This also, like that at Gaza, was a Philistine harlot, as plainly appears by her acquaintance and compliance with the lords of the Philistines, and her hiding of the Philistines in her house that by those lords were appointed to apprehend Samson.

**V. 5. ***{and said unto her, Entice him}* i.e. Use all the art and skill which they commonly have that are of your profession, and allure him to discover unto you this great secret, in what part of his body or other qualification his strength chiefly lies.

*{that we may bind him to afflict him}* Or, *humble him*. They do not say, to kill him, lest it may seem harsh and cruel to a woman that had entertained him for her paramour, and professed so much love unto him; but, to afflict and correct him for all the wrongs he had done unto their nation, and so to tame and humble the haughtiness of his spirit. And in truth their intention was not to have him killed, because his bare and sole death could not satisfy their rage and malice, unless they did first triumph over him in his miseries, and torment him worse than death with their insolencies and indignities.

*{and we will give thee every one of us eleven hundred pieces of silver}* Which if every one of the five lords of the Philistines agreed to give, it amounted to five thousand and five hundred pieces, which came to six hundred eighty-seven pounds and ten shillings, if we take these pieces for shekels, and value each at two shillings sixpence. Which great sum offered to a woman of that base condition showed how much they thought the matter did concern them, and how earnestly they desired to have it achieved.

**V. 6. ***{Tell me, I pray thee, wherein thy great strength lieth}* It is not to be doubted but she used all her art and skill to make and prepare a way for her suit, and that she did not bluntly propound it, but allured him with all signs of her love, that she might free him from all suspicion of treachery, magnified his heroic acts, and wondered at his more than human strength whereby he achieved them, and then desired for her own satisfaction to know wherein his great strength did lie, and added many promises, confirmed it may be with oaths (which are most common with those of her profession) that she would keep it to herself as a great secret.

**V. 7. ***{If they bind me with seven green withs}* Heb. *moist*: or, *with new cords*. This he says, hoping this would satisfy her without making any trial of it, or at least for the present to free himself from being vexed and pressed with her importunity to discover that secret which he was purposed to conceal.

**V. 8. ***{which had not been dried}* Dryness would have made them brittle; but these were green, strong and tough, and so hard to be broken.

**V. 9. ***{Now there were men lying in wait}* i.e. Ready to apprehend him, if the experiment proved true: otherwise they dared not appear for fear of Samson.

*{The Philistines be upon thee}* Are at hand ready to surprise you, if you do not prevent it by standing in your own defense. All this was done unto him in a way of sport and wanton dalliance, or when she had caused him to sleep on her lap, as v. 19, the former is much more probable, seeing it is not likely she could bind him fast with hard withes, and not awake him.

*{So his strength was not known}* i.e. Neither to her, nor to the Philistines, either whence it came, or how it was retained and preserved.

**V. 10. ***{And Delilah said unto Samson}* To wit, at some other time afterwards, when she found him in a good mood, being bewitched with her flatteries and allurements. For it would not stand with her policy to attempt it presently after her former failing and defeat, lest she should work in his head some suspicion of treachery or intention of evil.

**V. 11. ***{And he said unto her, If they bind me fast with new ropes}* Herein Samson discovers great weakness, in that having had experience that Delilah would put what he said upon trial whether it were true or no, he would again feign a false cause, because he had not the patience to endure the vexation of her importunity, though she gave him just cause to suspect her evil intention towards him.

*{that never were occupied}* Heb. *wherewith work had not been done*. Namely, to wear and weaken them.

**V. 13. ***{If thou weavest the seven locks of my head with the web}* That is, if you do part the hair of my head into seven locks; or, if you do take them as they are already so divided, verse 19, and weaving them with the web, do wind them both about the beam of the loom. Here Samson begins to faint indeed, now he suffers the false strumpet to finger his consecrated locks. See on verse 19.

**V. 14. ***{And she fastened it with the pin}* i.e. Having done all according to Samson’s appointment, she for more surety fastened the beam with a pin, that when Samson rose it might not turn or move.

*{went away with the pin, web, and beam}* Upon which his hair was woven and wound, and carried all away with him.

**V. 15. ***{when thine heart is not with me}* i.e. When you bear no true affection towards me, but only make an outward show of love to serve your own turn.

**V. 16. ***{so that his soul was vexed unto death}* i.e. Grievously distracted, on the one side being extremely loath to discover a secret which did so nearly concern him, on the other being no less loath to deny her anything upon whom he so impotently doted, especially being so pressed with her daily importunity, and his life so embittered, that having no rest nor comfort he chose rather to die than to live.

**V. 17. ***{that he told her all his heart}* Behold here man’s frailty and weakness when left to himself and his own corrupt heart, especially to the base lusts of uncleanness and carnal concupiscence. Samson, one of God’s great worthies, commended for his faith, and ennobled by his glorious victories, who with his hands rent a roaring and ravening lion as if he had been a kid, and slaughtered and routed a great army of his enemies, is now become so impotent, being vanquished by his prevailing lusts, that he is vexed even to the death by the importunity of a filthy harlot, and not able to keep from her a secret, the concealing whereof concerned him no less than his life. Yea, though he had had thrice experience that she intended to betray him unto death, yet his doting affection so blinded him that he could not beware, but discovered unto her all that was in his heart concerning this great secret, though it tended to his utter ruin. And thus did God justly give him up to fall into the following miseries, that thereby he might severely, yet deservedly, correct him for his former uncleanness, and his relapsing now again into the same sin.

*{If I be shaven, then my strength will go from me}* Not because his strength lay in his hair; for it was God’s free gift of grace, conferred upon him particularly, and not upon another Nazarite, that he should be of such an invincible strength that none of God’s enemies could stand before him; but because being a Nazarite, he was by God’s express command enjoined not to cut his hair, ch. 13:5, Numb. 6:5, which he transgressing, and breaking his vow, God withdrew from him his Spirit of strength and courage, and left him to his own human frailty. And this is noted to be the true, real and moral cause of his weakness, that the Lord was departed from him, verse 20, of which the preserving of his hair was but a shadow and ceremony. Some conceive that from this story of Samson the fable of Nisus had its original, whose power and life both are feigned to have depended on his purple hair, Ovid. *Metamorph.* lib. 8.

**V. 18. ***{And when Delilah saw that he had told her all his heart}* Namely, by his countenance, gesture, speech, and serious carriage of himself in all his relation.

*{Come up this once}* i.e. Though divers times before you have lost your labor and come in vain, because being myself deluded by Samson I have deluded you, yet if you please to come but this once again, you shall not fail of your desire.

*{and brought money in their hand}* i.e. Which they had promised to give her as the reward of her treachery, verse 5, this they now brought, lest she suspecting their payment should be more cold and slack in executing their design.

**V. 19. ***{and she caused him to shave off}* She might have suddenly dispatched him by cutting his throat: but this she had not in her commission; or if she had, yet perhaps she would have been loath to do it, in respect of that love and dalliance which had been between them, he having not given her any cause of distaste. And besides, the Philistine lords desired not his present death, but by putting out his eyes, to make him sure enough for doing them any further hurt, and to keep him in prison to do their drudgery, and, when they pleased, to make them sport; they in the meantime deriding, insulting and triumphing over him. But chiefly this preserving of his life came of God, who inclined their hearts unto it, that he living till his hair were grown again, and in the meantime by repentance renewing his covenant, and so recovering his strength, might at his death destroy more of them than he had done in his whole life.

*{the seven locks of his head}* Into which all his hair was divided and plaited, that he might the more easily cast it behind him upon his shoulders, and keep it from fluttering over his face, and so hindering his sight. The gifts of the Holy Ghost (amongst which Samson’s extraordinary strength and courage was one, ch. 13:25 and 14:6, 19 and 15:14, Isa. 11:2) are often in Scripture represented by the number of seven, to note their perfection; so Exod. 23:37, Zech. 3:9 and 4:2, Rev. 1:4 and 5:6.

*{and she began to afflict him}* i.e. To vex and trouble him, by her sudden rousing him out of his sleep, jogging, thrusting, and calling upon him, as before. Yea, and it is like, before she did this, she bound him, as she had formerly done: which bonds, when he awaked, he was not able to break: for otherwise she could not have known that his strength was gone from him, nor dared have called in the Philistines that waited to apprehend him.

**V. 20. ***{and said}* i.e. Thought within himself.

*{I will go out and shake myself}* i.e. Rouse up myself and spirits, and put off drowsiness, that I may go about my employments.

*{And he wist not that the Lord was departed from him}* Because upon his sudden awaking he observed not either his binding or shaving, or that the Lord had deprived him of that supernatural strength whereof his hair was a ceremonial sign and figure, which he had continued unto him, so long as he continued in obedience and kept his vow. Some make Samson even in this also a type of Christ, whose divinity withdrew as it were for a while, that his humanity might suffer.

**V. 21. ***{But the Philistines took him}* When God leaves us our enemies take us, and have power over us to use us at their pleasure; we not being able to make any resistance in our own strength.

*{and put out his eyes, and brought him down to Gaza}* Herein the Philistines had their ends, and God his, unto which he made theirs subservient.
