The Philistines in a revengeful way put out Samson’s eyes, to make him miserable, and his life uncomfortable, and to secure themselves from all fear of danger by him in future times; and they carried him along through the country, that the people all the way might flock about him, gaze upon him, and insult over him; and they brought to Gaza rather than any other place, not only because being one of their chief cities, there was in it the greatest concourse of people to mock and abuse him, but also because they might there triumph over him in his misery where he had formerly dishonored them by carrying away the gates and posts of their city. But God’s end in all this was, to bring blind Samson to a sight of his sin, that he might unfeignedly repent of it; leading him as it were by the hand to take notice of it, by the resemblance that was between it and the punishment. For his eyes were first put out, which were the instruments and organs of inflaming his lust, by glancing and gazing on a filthy harlot; and Gaza must be the place where he must suffer punishment, imprisonment, and even death itself, where he first acted his sin of uncleanness.

*{and he did grind in the prison house}* Being a great worthy he is made a drudge, and not suffered to eat his bread before he earned it by his slavish service. See in Isa. 47:2.

**V. 22. ***{Howbeit the hair of his head began to grow}* And with it his strength, whereof it was a sign; Samson in the meantime reconciling himself unto God by unfeigned repentance; renewing his vow which he had broken, and so recovering his former state of a Nazarite, from which he had fallen, and above that, the love and favor of God, whom by his sins he had displeased.

*{after he was shaven}* Or, *as when he was shaven*. i.e. It began to grow towards that length it was of when he was shaven. Which shows that there continued some good time before they called him out to make them sport at Dagon’s feast. And why they deferred so long, after they had surprised Samson, to praise their idol for delivering him into their hands, we cannot guess, unless perhaps this feast was anniversary in remembrance of so great a benefit; and this the second year wherein it was celebrated.

**V. 23. ***{Dagon their god}* The Philistines lived on the seacoast, and therefore had a sea-idol which they worshiped. For Dagon is derived from the Hebrew word *Dag*, signifying a fish, because it was portrayed like a fish from the middle downward, having the upper part like a man, 1 Sam. 5:4. Such idols other heathens worshiped, as gods of the sea, to wit, Neptune, Leucothea, Triton, etc. See on 1 Chron. 10:10.

**V. 24. ***{slew many of us}* Heb. *multiplied our slain*.

**V. 25. ***{when their hearts were merry}* Heb. *when their heart was good*. i.e. Frolic and of good cheer. So chap. 18:20 and 19:6, Ruth 3:7, 2 Sam. 13:28.

*{that he may make us sport}* To wit, passively, that he may be the subject of our mirth. Herein also Samson was a type of our Savior, whom his enemies made the subject of their scorn and malice, Matth. 26:67, 68 and 27:29.

*{made them sport}* Heb. *made sport before them*. i.e. He became the subject of their sport, and gave them occasion of laughter, either by unwillingly committing some errors and mistakes being blind, or willingly to blind them by these shows, that they might not discover his main intention.

*{they set him between the pillars}* As in a place where he might be best seen by the princes and people met together.

**V. 26. ***{whereupon the house standeth}* i.e. The temple of Dagon, wherein these idolaters kept their feast, as the manner was. See chap. 9:27.

*{that I may lean upon them}* Pretending weariness, either by his grinding in the mill, or his long standing there to make them sport, or his sudden and speedy coming to that place.

**V. 27. ***{there were upon the roof about three thousand men}* As being flat after the manner of those countries.

**V. 28. ***{remember me, I pray thee, and strengthen me}* Whereby it is evident that he relied not upon his hair now newly grown, nor on his own strength, but only on that strength which he expected to receive from God for the aiding and executing of his intended work.

*{that I may be at once avenged}* He does not seek, as a private person, to take vengeance out of God’s hand, to whom alone it belongs, Rom. 12:19, but being a public person and a judge, he desires that he might, as God’s substitute and vicegerent, be the instrument under God of executing vengeance upon God’s and the church’s enemies.

*{for my two eyes}* Not so much for doing me a private wrong, in respect of my person, as because they have thereby disabled me to fight in God’s quarrel for the deliverance of his people. This his prayer was made by the extraordinary instinct of God’s Spirit, according to his will, as appears, in that God heard this prayer of faith, Hebr. 11:32, and accordingly gave him supernatural strength to act this great and miraculous work according to his desire; which otherwise he would not have done, if he had asked amiss, to satisfy his lust in a way of private revenge, Jam. 4:3.

**V. 30. ***{Let me die with the Philistines}* Here is no warrant or ground to encourage or countenance self-murderers; seeing what Samson did, was done by an extraordinary instinct of God’s Spirit. Neither was this the principal end at which he aimed, to kill himself, but the destruction of God’s and his church’s enemies, unto which he was called and consecrated as a public person and a judge, though his own death did follow as a necessary consequent; the which he contemned in regard he should pull so many thousand Philistines into the pit with him, and out of the fervency of his zeal devoted himself to glorify God and give deliverance to his people by doing of this service. Nor was this a despairing speech of one impatient in respect of his miseries to live any longer, but of a faithful man hazarding himself even to the death, that he might do God service, and that not in an ordinary way, but by an extraordinary motion of the Holy Ghost inspiring him and suggesting unto him this prayer. Wherein Samson was a type of our Lord Christ, who humbled himself to the death, that he might save his people out of the hands of all their spiritual enemies, Phil. 2:8.

*{So the dead which he slew at his death were more}* Herein also he was a figure of Christ, who by his death overcame death, and him that had the power of death, the devil, with all the power of hell, Heb. 2:14.

**V. 31. ***{Then his brethren—came down and took him}* The Philistines condescending thereunto, either being overruled by God, who has the hearts of all men in his hand, Prov. 16:1, or being affected for the present by this common calamity, or because they were so weakened by the death and loss of their princes, principal commanders, the flower of their chivalry, and chiefest of their people, that they thought it not fit to provoke the Israelites by denying their request, grounded on humanity and common honesty and civility, and turning them off with an harsh answer. And here again we have some typical resemblance of Christ’s body begged of Pilate, and buried by Joseph, Matth. 27:57.

*{between Zorah and Eshtaol}* See ch. 13:2.

*{And he judged Israel twenty years}* See ch. 13:20.

## CHAPTER 17

**Verse 1. ***{And there was a man}* It is not certainly known when the things in this and that following chapters were done. But most of the learned expositors upon evident grounds do agree, that they happened not in Joshua’s time, nor in the days of the elders that survived him: for all that time Israel served the Lord, Josh. 24:31, and did not commit such gross idolatry as is related in this chapter. Neither did these things happen after the death of Samson, but it is probable some hundreds of years before, soon after the death of Joshua and the elders. For I. It appears, ch. 2:8–13, that they being all dead, the generation that succeeded fell from God and his true worship, corrupted his pure religion, and set up gross idolatry. II. Some hold that Jonathan the Levite, the Danites’ idol-priest, was the son of Gershom, who was born unto Moses before he went into Egypt out of Midian, Exod. 2:22, three hundred and fifty years before Samson died. See on chap. 18:30. III. The story of the Levite’s concubine, chap. 19, and of the war with Benjamin, ch. 20 (which happened after this of Micah’s idolatry, as some gather from ch. 20:1), was acted when Phinehas the grandson of Aaron was high priest, and therefore not after the days of Samson, unless we will say that Phinehas lived (which is not likely) above three hundred years. See on ch. 20:18. IV. If the desolation of Benjamin, chap. 20:35, 48, happened after Samson’s time, the increase of that tribe, 1 Chron. 12:29, is very wonderful. These reasons (whereunto others might be added. See on ch. 18:1, 12 and 19:10) make it more than probable that the things spoken of in these five last chapters were acted rather before Othniel was judge than after Samson’s death. Yet that the history of the judges, which is the main matter of this book, might be finished in the first place without interruption, these stories of things happening in the time of anarchy, v. 6, ch. 18:1 and 19:1 and 21:25, are here set by themselves in the end of all: the scope whereof is to show how corrupt the people were both in religion and manners when there was no king in Israel, and how just it was for God to deliver such heinous sinners into the hands of cruel oppressors.

**V. 2. ***{The eleven hundred shekels of silver}* See 23:15.

*{that were taken from thee}* i.e. Stolen.

*{about which thou cursedst}* Imprecating mischief and misfortune, death and destruction against them who had stolen them away, if they repented not of what they had done, and so restored them again unto her.

*{and spakest of also in my ears}* i.e. In my hearing; wherewith being affrighted, as thinking it a dreadful thing to lie under a mother’s curse, I now come to acknowledge my fault, that receiving from you pardon and your blessings, I may be freed from the curse and the fear of it.

*{Blessed be thou of the Lord, my son}* In her passion she overshoots herself in two extremes, cursing before at random she knew not whom, and now, knowing it was her son, pronouncing a blessing, not so much as touching upon his sin against God, nor in the least manner reproving him for it: q.d. I do not only free you from the curse, but turn it now into a blessing.

**V. 3. ***{I had wholly dedicated the silver unto the Lord}* i.e. Unto Jehovah the only true God. So they cloaked their gross idolatry under show of God’s service, though he had so often forbidden and condemned it in his law. Whereby it appears there were two kinds of idolatry; one among the heathens, whereby they worshiped strange and false gods; the other among the Israelites, whereby they worshiped the true God in an idolatrous and false manner, contrary to the prescript rule of his word, under the name of heathenish idols and images, or some other way according to their own invention and wills, as the golden calf, Exod. 32:5, and Baal, Hos. 2:16, and such was the idolatry of Jeroboam, and of Micah’s mother in this place. And generally it is observed, that when an image or idol is mentioned, and not appropriated by name to some heathen god, there is to be understood that the true God was represented by it.

*{from my hand}* i.e. To be bestowed by me upon my son and his son for religious uses, v. 5.

*{to make a graven image and a molten image}* Many expositors think she did not intend to make two images, one molten, and another graven, but only one, which was first cast in a mold, and then graven with tools and beautified by the art of the workman; and that because of the small quantity of silver designed for this use, which was not enough to make two several images: but it is apparent they were two; for it is said ch. 18:18 that the Danites fetched out of Micah’s house the carved image, the Ephod, and the Teraphim, and the molten image.

*{now therefore I will restore it unto thee}* Namely, the right and interest in it; for it was still in his possession: q.d. Seeing it was devoted for your use, there is no need you should bring it unto me, but keep it still yourself.

**V. 4. ***{Yet he restored the money unto his mother}* Though she pressed him to keep it, yet he would needs restore it, to quiet his conscience, as thinking thereby to be more free from the curse.

*{and his mother took two hundred shekels of silver}* It is not likely that having devoted eleven hundred shekels unto the Lord in her superstitious way, she would alienate nine hundred shekels to common uses, but rather that she laid out the whole sum, in building and fitting an house for idol-gods, filling it with them when it was prepared, and making a rich ephod and teraphim and all other furniture necessary for this idolatrous service.

**V. 5. ***{made an Ephod}* Not a linen ephod, such as the Levites used, but such a rich ephod as Gideon had made in resemblance in that which the high priest used in the tabernacle, ch. 8:27, and with it all other garments fitting the priest in his religious service.

*{and Teraphim}* Images, made like unto men; as appears, in that Michol made use of one of them, by putting it in the bed in David’s room, that by resembling him it might deceive those whom Saul sent to fetch him, 1 Sam. 19:13. And such were those idols which Rachel stole from her father, Gen. 31:19, and those which Josiah with other abominations put away, 2 Kings 23:24. By these idols they divined, and made use of them as oracles to resolve their doubts, Ezek. 21:21, Hos. 3:4, Zech. 10:2.

*{consecrated one of his sons}* Heb. *filled the hand*. A form of speech taken from the consecration of Aaron and his sons by Moses, to the priest’s office, with putting into their hands the fat and shoulder of the ram, a loaf of bread and an oiled cake, for an offering before the Lord, Exod. 29:22, 23, 24. So Jeroboam, 1 Kings 13:33.

*{who became his Priest}* Though neither of Aaron’s line nor tribe. All this he did, as a gross idolater, expressly contrary to the law of God.

**V. 6. ***{In those days there was no King in Israel}* So ch. 18:1 and 19:1 and 21:25, i.e. no supreme magistrate. And so king is taken, Gen. 36:31, Deut. 33:5. Neither can the word be here taken in its proper sense, seeing the Israelites had no king until the time of Saul. But the meaning only is, that they had in these times of all confusion no judge to keep the people in awe and order, and to restrain them from doing what they listed. Which anarchy was a most desperate state and condition, when no man had any other guide to lead him but his own blind and corrupt nature, which is prone to all sin and wickedness. And this implies that Micah’s course in all he did was naught and wicked; seeing if there had been a good judge or magistrate, he would have restrained him from this superstitious idolatry; in which he now went on without control.

*{that which was right in his own eyes}* Not that which was right in God’s eyes, but what himself fancied to be right.

**V. 7. ***{Beth-lehem-judah}* So called, to distinguish it from another Bethlehem in the tribe of Zebulun, Josh. 19:15.

*{of the family of Judah}* There could no Levite be properly said to be of the family of Judah, seeing the tribes were distinct one from another. But this is spoken, as some think, because his mother was of Judah, though his father were a Levite; and so he might be said to be of that family in respect of her, especially if she were an inheritor, like Zelophehad’s daughters, his father for her sake sojourning there. But because the Scripture uses not to style men after their mother’s family, therefore others think he is said to be of the family of Judah, because he was born, bred and sojourned in Beth-lehem-judah. For though it were none of the Levites’ cities, yet he had his habitation there where he might find best entertainment, the Levites in those times being for want of means and maintenance put to shift for themselves, as this Levite did in this place, and afterwards in Laish, chapter 18, v. 27, 30. For this is he who is there called Jonathan.

**V. 8. ***{And the man departed out of the City from Beth-lehem-judah}* Being necessitated, it seems, by want of maintenance. For idolatry being set up in the church, and anarchy accompanied with all confusion and disorder in the commonwealth, religion was corrupted, the service of God, sacrifices and oblations neglected, the priests and Levites despised, and all means and maintenance being withheld from them, they were enforced to leave the tabernacle and their own cities where formerly they had lived, and to wander into other parts of the land where they might find a livelihood. And this was the case of the priests and Levites in Nehemiah’s time, Neh. 13:10, 11. And no better condition are we likely to be in, in these days, if for want of government God’s pure worship be neglected, and sects, errors and heresies be tolerated, and his ministers despised, and their means and maintenance withheld which are allotted and allowed unto them by the law of God and the land, and they left without protection to the will and pleasure of the common people, who for the most part aim only at their private profit, without any respect to God’s service or the eternal salvation of their own souls.

*{to the house of Micah, as he journeyed}* Heb. *in making his way*. Not with a purpose at first to reside there, but to take up his lodging for a night.

**V. 10. ***{Dwell with me, and be unto me a father and a Priest}* Or, *a father, in being a priest*. For though he were younger than Micah, yet for honor’s sake he says he should be a father unto him, in respect of his calling and office. See on Gen. 45:8.

*{and I will give thee ten shekels of silver by the year, and a suit of apparel [or, a double suit. Heb. an order of garments] and thy victuals}* He offers to entertain him at a poor rate and for base allowance, not out of the scantness of his estate, but the penuriousness of his mind, and the vile esteem he had of his calling and condition. For he that had eleven hundred shekels to bestow upon his idols and idol-service, could hardly afford ten shekels to the Levite for his maintenance. Only he makes up this defect by good-cheap titles and compliments, calling him father, and acknowledging him for his priest: herein just like many people in these days, who abounding in superfluous expenses, are only strait-handed and hearted towards their faithful ministers, esteeming that only lost which is drawn from them towards their maintenance: only they feed and fatten them with titles, caps and compliments, but allow them no means of comfortable subsistence for their encouragement in the duties of their calling; herein using them as they do their parrots, which they keep hungry that they may make them talk.

*{So the Levite went in}* i.e. Though the conditions upon which he offered to entertain him were base, yet being necessitated and pinched with want he accepted of them, and goes into Micah’s house to dwell with him.

**V. 11. ***{and the young man was unto him as one of his sons}* i.e. He tendered, cherished, and made much of him, as if he had been his own child; and so in some sort supplied what was wanting in wages by his kind usage and loving entertainment: and so now he that was a father in respect of office, v. 10, is used as a son in respect of his youth.

**V. 12. ***{And Micah consecrated the Levite}* As he had done his son, v. 5, either causing his son to lay down his calling, and give his place of priest unto this newcomer, seeing it would more countenance his idolatrous service, when it was officiated by a Levite, than by his son who was an Ephraimite, or else associating and joining them both together in the priesthood. And this latter act was not much less unlawful than the other, seeing it was not permitted by God’s law to a Levite to execute the priest’s office, which only belonged to the sons of Aaron, Numb. 3:3, 10 and 19:40.

**V. 13. ***{Then said Micah, Now know I that the Lord will do me good}* Thus he blesses himself in his blind devotion and superstition, which wholly tended to gross idolatry, though indeed it exposed him to God’s wrath and vengeance, as afterwards appears.

## CHAPTER 18

**Verse 1. ***{In those days}* i.e. Soon after the days of Joshua before God raised up judges extraordinarily to deliver and govern his people.

*{there was no King in Israel}* See ch. 17:6 and 19:1 and 21:25. This is prefixed as a reason why the Danites were put to provide an inheritance for themselves, whereas whilst the whole people were under any government, the tribes assisted one another in driving out the Canaanites, and getting possession of that inheritance which by lot was fallen unto them, Numb. 32:21, Josh. 22:2, Judg. 1:3.

*{For unto that day all their inheritance had not fallen unto them}* i.e. Though they had their full inheritance fallen unto them by lot, and so had a right unto it from God’s donation, yet because of their apostasy, idolatry, and diffidence in God, who had promised his assistance in driving out these nations before them, they had not yet obtained the possession of a great part of it. This was the case of many tribes, but of the Danites above the rest; they had not yet gotten an inheritance large enough for their numerous tribe, Josh. 19:47, and the Amorites also had driven them out of part of that which they had gotten, ch. 1:34. By this it appears, that the things here related were not done after the death of Samson, seeing it is not probable that so potent a tribe as the Danites were, were to seek for their inheritance allotted unto them, and for a place of habitation to dwell in, above three hundred years after they had been in the land of Canaan; yet now they were not utterly destitute, having some place in the mountains, Zorah, and Eshtaol, to dwell in, but yet far too scant and strait for their great multitude; which makes them now use means to enlarge themselves.

**V. 2. ***{sent of their family}* i.e. Of their tribe.

*{men of valour}* Heb. *sons of valor*.

*{from Zorah and from Eshtaol}* See ch. 13:2, 25.

*{Go search the Land}* i.e. Of their enemies that belonged to their lot, and was not yet subdued, to find out a place most convenient for their habitation, and which might with least pains and danger be won and conquered.

*{they lodged there}* i.e. In that place where Micah had an house, and not far from it.

**V. 3. ***{they knew the voice of the young man the Levite}* i.e. Either they knew it particularly and personally, by reason of former acquaintance which some of the spies might have with him whilst he sojourned in Beth-lehem-judah, their tribes lying near one another: or by his pronunciation they perceived he was an Ephraimite, but of Judah; as it is usual for men of the same country dwelling in several parts of it, remote one from another, to have different dialects and manner of speaking, whereby they may be distinguished one from another, as the Ephraimites were from the Gileadites, ch. 12:6, and the Galileans from those that dwelt at Jerusalem, Mark 14:70.

*{who brought thee hither?}* To wit, from Beth-lehem-judah, you being a Levite, and not an Ephraimite.

*{what makest thou in this place?}* Seeing you have no Levitical administrations or employments here, being so far remote from the tabernacle.

*{and what hast thou here?}* i.e. What means of subsistence or maintenance is here by Micah allowed unto you?

**V. 4. ***{Thus and thus dealeth Micah with me}* i.e. Upon these and these conditions has he entertained me, and thus and thus kindly he has used me.

*{and I am his Priest}* i.e. Having set up an house for the exercise of his religion, and filled it with gods and images of divers kinds, he has hired me to officiate in it as a priest.

**V. 5. ***{And they said unto him, Ask counsel, we pray thee, of God}* Hearing that he was a priest, and that he had in his chapel divers images representing God, with an ephod, and all priestly requisites, and above all the rest, a teraphim by which they divined, and which as an oracle did foretell things to come, they desired him to inquire concerning the success of their enterprise and expedition.

**V. 6. ***{And the Priest said unto them}* To wit, after he had inquired of his idol, by whom it is like the devil gave this answer; or at least he made them believe that he inquired, and then himself feigned it to humor them in their course, and so to draw from them the better reward; or God might give answer to this idolatrous priest, as he did to Balaam the soothsayer. A prophet or a dreamer may work a wonder, or utter an oracle, and yet himself say, *Let us go after other Gods, and serve them*, Deut. 13:1, 2.

*{Go in peace}* i.e. You shall dispatch your business with safety, and prosper in it without running into much difficulty and danger.

*{before the Lord is your way wherein ye go}* i.e. The eyes of the Lord watch over you for good, and to favor, direct and prosper you in your journey. And thus he abuses God’s holy name to countenance his idolatrous courses, Psa. 33:18 and 34:15, Deut. 11:12.

**V. 7. ***{Then the five men departed, and came to Laish}* i.e. Being filled with confidence and courage by his prediction, they set forward on their journey, and came to Laish, which was also called Leshem, Josh. 19:47, and afterwards Dan, verse 29, and belonged to Dan’s lot, Josh. 19:48.

*{and saw the people that were therein, how they dwelt careless}* i.e. That they were quiet and secure, in their opinion, from all danger of surprise, v. 27.

*{after the manner of the Zidonians}* i.e. Using their customs and laws, having it may be some dependence upon them, either by way of subjection, or else in respect of some league contracted between them: and so being confident of their aid and assistance, and fearing no danger, they imitated them in their security. For as the Zidonians lived in all pleasure, plenty and prosperity, not fearing any enemy that might disturb their peace, in respect of the situation of their country and the strength of their city both by sea and land, so was it with these men of Laish.

*{and there was no Magistrate}* Heb. *possessor*, or *heir of restraint*. i.e. Either none at all, or none hereditary and perpetual, to restrain them from any wickedness, or to punish them for committing it, but such as were chosen by themselves from year to year, who did let them live as they list, for fear of displeasing them who had power in their hands by most voices to turn them out of their places at their pleasure. And so all things became lawful, and nothing that pleased them was accounted dishonest.

*{put them to shame}* By inflicting upon offenders deserved punishments, yea, they were so far from punishing them according to their demerits, that they would not so much as disgrace and countenance their vices and faults.

*{and they were far from the Zidonians}* The Zidonians lay far west from them, and therefore could not on a sudden send them any aids to keep them from being surprised, if any enemy attempted it, though they were the only people upon whom they depended.

*{and had no business with any man}* i.e. They were so rich, and had such plenty of all manner of provisions within themselves, that they had no commerce with any others; and so secure in their own strength and their Zidonian confederates, that they cared not to be in league with any of their neighbors, that they might have their aid upon any occasion, but lived of themselves pleasantly and securely.

**V. 8. ***{And they came to their brethren}* i.e. Having as spies seen and observed the country and the manners of the people, they returned to give an account of all that they had done to the Danites that sent them.

*{What say ye?}* i.e. Now you are returned, give us an account of your journey, that is, whether you have found any convenient place for us to inhabit in, and what hope there is of obtaining it.

**V. 9. ***{And they said, Arise, that we may go up against them}* q.d. We have found out such a place, and such people which do inhabit it, that there needs not much discourse about it, but rather that we fall upon action: for the land is very good, pleasant and fruitful, and to be conquered, and gotten with little danger.

*{and are ye still?}* Do not spend time in multiplying words, and in replying to our relation, but take the present opportunity, and out of hand set upon the conquest.

*{Be not slothful to go, and to enter to possess the land}* i.e. By your sloth and negligence do not let slip this fair opportunity, for there is no more to do but to go and possess the land, seeing you are likely to find but small and weak opposition, and little danger or pains in subduing of it. But yet for your better encouragement, and to remove all cause of fear, know this that follows:

**V. 10. ***{When ye go, ye shall come unto a people secure}* Who therefore may easily be taken by a sudden surprise, when apprehending no danger they are altogether unprovided of all means to make resistance, and keep no watch.

*{to a large land}* Therefore fit to be a place of habitation for your numerous tribe.

*{for God hath given it into your hands}* As appears not only by his oracle which we received of him in our journey from one of his priests, but also as we see it in part already verified in the event, God directing us to a people that are secure and careless, having no means to make resistance.

*{a place where there is no want}* i.e. Which abounds with all pleasures and profits, and with such plenty and prosperity that nothing is wanting that can be desired.

**V. 11. ***{six hundred men}* Chosen out of the whole tribe, as fittest and ablest, in respect of their valor and fortitude, for such an expedition.

**V. 12. ***{And they went up, and pitched in Kirjath-jearim}* i.e. Not in the city itself, which probably would not have admitted such an army of strangers, but without the walls, near unto it. So Josh. 10:10. This city, otherwise called Kirjath-baal, Josh. 15:60, was situate upon a hill on the borders of Judah and Dan, northward, and called the city of woods, and was one of those four cities of the Hivites with which Joshua and the princes contracted for their lives, Josh. 9:17, and afterwards fell to the lot of Judah, Josh. 15:9. See on Josh. 18:14.

*{wherefore they called that place Mahaneh-Dan, unto this day}* i.e. The camp or leaguer of Dan. Here afterwards Manoah the father of Samson dwelt, ch. 13:2, 25, whereby it appears that the things related in this chapter were long before the birth of Samson.

*{behold, it is behind Kirjath-jearim}* As the east with the Hebrews is called the forepart, so the west the hinder part. So Josh. 1:4 and 13:5. Therefore they pitched not in the city itself, but on the west side of it. See on Job 23:8.

**V. 13. ***{came unto the house of Micah}* i.e. To that village where Micah dwelt.

**V. 14. ***{Then answered the five men}* i.e. They began to speak and propound that which follows to the rest of their company. For so this phrase is usually taken: 1 Sam. 14:28, Ezr. 10:2, Matth. 11:25, Act. 5:8.

*{Do ye know}* i.e. We would have you to know, that in these houses, i.e. one of these houses, there is an Ephod and Teraphim, of which in our journey we made special use, by inquiring what success we should have, and had an answer which we found true: which makes us think that it may be very useful for us for the time to come, if we take along with us all these things, and with them the priest also.

*{Now therefore consider what you have to do}* i.e. Whether you will carry them with you, or only inquire of them, as we did, verse 5, and then leave them behind you.

**V. 15. ***{And they turned thitherward}* i.e. Having resolved to make a purchase of these things, they declined out of the direct way of their journey towards the house of the Levite, which was some part of Micah’s house near unto the chapel, allotted unto the Levite for his more convenient lodging.

*{and saluted him}* Heb. *asked him of peace*. i.e. Of his welfare and prosperity, and how it fared with him in that his place and employment.

**V. 16. ***{stood by the entering of the gate}* Waiting for the coming forth of the five men that were gone into the house, to know what success they had in their enterprise, and to second them, if finding opposition they needed their assistance.

**V. 17. ***{and came in thither}* i.e. Entered into the idol-chapel where the ephod and images were, and took them all away.

*{and the Priest stood in the entering of the gate}* i.e. Being, it is likely, drawn thither by the five spies, to salute their brethren that stood without, whilst they in the meantime went in to do their exploit.

**V. 18. ***{And these went into Micah’s house}* i.e. The five spies went into that part of Micah’s house which was set apart for idolatrous service, whilst the Levite was without the gate saluting and talking with the Danites.

*{and fetched the carved image}* Wherein they greatly sinned, in that being private men they stole and took away that which was another man’s, and that with an evil intention, not doing it out of hatred of his idolatry, but rather out of love to it, purposing to set up these idols and idolatrous service in their own tribe.

*{Then said the Priest unto them, What do ye?}* i.e. What do you mean to take away these things that are Micah’s, and belong not unto you?

**V. 19. ***{lay thy hand upon thy mouth}* i.e. Refrain from speaking anything against our proceedings. The like expression we have Job 21:5 and 29:9 and 40:4, Mic. 7:16.

*{and be to us a father and a Priest}* i.e. We will highly honor you, as if you were our natural father that begat us. In such great esteem were men of that function in former times, how basely soever they are slighted in our days.

*{Is it better for thee, &c.?}* q.d. We persuade you to nothing but what is most for your own good and advancement, both in respect of honor and gain.

**V. 20. ***{And the Priest’s heart was glad}* As respecting only his own preferment, and in the meantime ungratefully forgetting all Micah’s former kindness.

*{and went in the midst of the people}* Namely, for his greater safety, if either any enemies should meet them and set upon them in their march; or if Micah pursuing them to recover his losses should assault them in the rear.

**V. 21. ***{and put the little ones, and the cattle, and the carriage before them}* Out of their confidence of an easy conquest, they do not march forward as men preparing for a dangerous war, but as removing to settle themselves in some new plantation, taking their wives and children, their cattle and carriages along with them: and these they placed in the fore-rank, as more suspecting Micah’s pursuit than any incursion of enemies before them.

**V. 22. ***{in the houses}* i.e. In the village where Micah dwelt.

**V. 23. ***{What aileth thee, that thou comest with such a company?}* Heb. *that thou art gathered together*. Armed might insults over feeble right, and thieves complain of true men for demanding their own goods. These Danites having spoiled Micah, complain of him and his company, as troublesome people, who with their clamors and outcries disquieted and hindered them in their march.

**V. 24. ***{and what have I more?}* i.e. What have you left me worth the having, in comparison of my images and priest, which you have unjustly taken away?

**V. 25. ***{Let not thy voice be heard among us}* Your clamors and reproachful language.

*{lest angry fellows run upon thee}* Heb. *bitter of soul*. i.e. Embittered and incensed with fury by the clamors and provoking speeches.

*{and thou lose thy life, with the lives of thy household}* Which are more precious than your idols and priest, though you value them more than the rest of your goods.

**V. 27. ***{a people that were at quiet and secure}* See verse 10.

*{and burnt the City with fire}* They went to seek out a city and place fit for habitation, of which they stood in great need; and yet coming to such an one, they burnt it with fire. This was done either out of the heat and fury of war, which, for the time it lasted, made them forget or neglect their own convenience; or else because they could do no otherwise take it, without (at least) the great slaughter of their own men, seeing the people being put to the worst in the streets, would run into the houses, and there fight it out in their own defense.

**V. 28. ***{And there was no deliverer, because it was far from Zidon}* Laish was seated hard by mount Lebanon, and so far off from Zidon as they could have no help from thence in this sudden surprise, though there it seems was their chiefest hope. See on verse 7.

*{and it was in the valley that lieth by Beth-rehob}* A region or part of Syria-Trachonitis, called Syria-Beth-rehob, and Syria-Rehob, 2 Sam. 10:6, 8. In which was a city of that name, bordering upon the utmost north part of Canaan, near unto Hamath, Numb. 13:21, which fell to Asher’s lot, Josh. 19:28, and was designed to be one of the Levites’ cities, Josh. 21:31, but never conquered by the Asherites, chap. 1:31, for the Syrians did possess it in David’s time, 2 Sam. 10:6, 8.

**V. 29. ***{And they called the name of the City Dan}* i.e. In honorable remembrance of the patriarch Dan, their great-grandfather, or the father of their tribe, Gen. 30:6, and also that it might be a memorial to posterity, that though they lived in the utmost borders of the country, and in a place so far remote from the rest of the Israelites, yet they were a part of one of their tribes. This city was afterwards called Caesarea Panadis, from Paneas, the name of the country thereabouts, and of one of the fountains of Jordan, Plin. l. 5, cap. 15 and 18. It was called also Caesarea Philippi, because built, or much enlarged and beautified, by Philip the son of Herod the Great, in honor of Tiberius Caesar, Joseph. Antiq. l. 18, c. 3. See on Amos 8:14 and Matth. 16:13. Agrippa the younger called it afterwards Neronias, in honor of Nero, Joseph. Antiq. l. 20, c. 8.

**V. 30. ***{And the children of Dan set up the graven image}* Having an high esteem of it, with the rest of the idols, and also of the priest, because of the prediction of their good and prosperous success in their expedition against Laish, which came to pass according to their own desire.

*{and Jonathan the son of Gershom, the son of Manasseh}* The Jewish expositors and some others are of opinion, that this Jonathan, who lately was Micah’s but now the Danites’ priest, was grandchild to no meaner a person than Moses; and that by inserting the letter *nun* Moses is here transformed into Manasseh, to save the credit of that great prophet. But this is but an uncertain conjecture: for how often does the Scripture mention the wicked children of godly parents! See on ch. 17:1 and 7, and on Numb. 27:18.

*{until the day of the captivity}* What captivity is here meant is very doubtful and much controverted, whether that, when the Israelites had that great defeat by the Philistines, when the ark was carried away as it were captive out of the land, and with it no doubt a great many of the Israelites taken prisoners in that battle, or that great captivity, when the ten tribes were carried away by Shalmaneser king of Assyria. For either of these opinions there are divers strong reasons, and no less strong objections against them. Therefore some expositors will have neither of those captivities to be here understood, but a third, namely the particular captivity of the tribe of Dan, that happened between the two other, though not mentioned in the Scripture, as many other things are not which yet were done. But I see no necessity that may drive us to this shift, seeing this captivity may very probably be meant of the taking away of the ark, and many of the Israelites with it, 1 Sam. 4. For whereas it is objected against it, that there is no mention in the Scriptures of any more captivities than that of the ten tribes by Shalmaneser, and the other of Judah by Nebuchadnezzar, it may be answered, that no more is this captivity of the tribe of Dan mentioned there, but only imagined by them that make this objection. And again it may be said, that though those were the two great captivities, that hinders not but there might be a third lesser than they: and that this taking of the ark was a captivity, appears, Psa. 78:60, etc., where it is so called: *So he forsook the Tabernacle of Shiloh, the tent which he placed among them, and delivered his strength into captivity, and his glory into the enemies hand*. *He gave his people also unto the sword*, &c. *He smote his enemies in their hinder parts*, to wit, with emerods, after they had carried away the ark, and kept it in their country. In all which the psalmist alludes to that great defeat which the Philistines gave the Israelites, 1 Sam. 4:10, 11. Again, verse 31, the continuance of this image and idolatrous worship set up by the Danites is limited to that time only in which the house of God was in Shiloh; and that was not till the captivity by Shalmaneser, but only till the captivity of the ark by the Philistines. For soon after this it was brought to Kirjath-jearim, and never after returned to Shiloh. Furthermore, there is no probability, that these idols and idolatrous service and priesthood should continue after that time of reformation by Samuel, when as the people returned unto God, and put away all their idols, 1 Sam. 7:3, 4. Or though it should be imagined that Samuel’s power extended not to this remote place, which was in the utmost borders of the land, yet what show of reason is there, that they should not be taken away by David, a man according to God’s own heart, who was so zealous of God’s pure worship, and abhorred all manner of idolatry, especially reigning so long a time, and neither wanting will nor power to effect such a pious work? For whereas it is answered, that though a public reformation were brought in in the days of Samuel and David, yet this idol-service might be still retained and secretly acted by many of the Danites even to the days of Jeroboam, and so continued to the captivity by Shalmaneser, this is little to the purpose: for the question is not what might be done by some of that tribe secretly (for close idolatry might lurk amongst them even to the second captivity, as well as the first, if at least any of the Danites remained so long in the land) but how long this chapel of idols and idolatrous priesthood and service was publicly upheld and maintained by that tribe. Now this was only whilst the house of God remained in Shiloh, verse 31, and that was no longer but till the ark was carried away captive by the Philistines. Moreover, it is most probable that this book of Judges was written by Samuel, or at least by some other that lived in his or David’s days, and not by Ezra, as some have dreamed, having no other ground for their opinion but the dotage of the apocryphal Esdras: and therefore this captivity cannot be that of Shalmaneser, which happened so long time after the days of him that wrote this story, unless we will say he wrote it by a prophetical spirit, which is never to be observed in any penman of historical Scripture without some intimation or notice given of it. And besides, if he had written this by way of prophecy, he would have done it in a prophetical not an historical manner, and would not have said that Jonathan his sons were, but that they should be, the priests to the time of the captivity by Shalmaneser.

**V. 31. ***{all the time that the house of God was in Shiloh}* This is to be limited to the time after the image was set up, and not to be understood of the whole time of the being of the tabernacle in Shiloh, seeing it was placed there in Joshua’s time, long before this graven image was erected, Josh. 18:1.

## CHAPTER 19

**Verse 1. ***{And it came to pass in those days}* i.e. Soon after the death of Joshua, and before Othniel was judge, as appears v. 11, compared with ch. 1:7.

*{when there was no King in Israel}* i.e. No supreme magistrate to restrain the people from any vice, or to punish sin. Which is here prefixed before the following history, as a reason why the Levite’s wife committed whoredom, and the men of Gibeah such abominable wickedness, because there was no judge to restrain or punish them. See ch. 17:6 and 18:1 and 21:25.

*{who took to him a concubine}* Heb. *A woman a concubine*; or, *a wife a concubine*. For there were two sorts of concubines: one, who were not wives, nor contracted in marriage, whose sons had by law no right to inherit with the brethren of the lawful wives, as we see ch. 11:2. The other were contracted to their husbands, but not in so solemn manner as the first and chief wives, who were mothers and mistresses of the family. Such were Jacob’s two maids, who were taken to his wives, and whose children shared in their father’s inheritance. Now these in Scripture are called sometimes wives, sometimes concubines. And so this woman here is called *a wife a concubine*, to distinguish her both from a harlot, and also from a lawful wife, who was mother of the family and the only true wife, both by God’s first institution, Gen. 2:18, 24, Mal. 2:15, Matth. 12:4, 5, and also by his law, Lev. 18:18, 1 Cor. 7:2. See on Gen. 25:1.

*{out of Beth-lehem-judah}* See on ch. 17:7. It was lawful for him to take a wife from thence, though he were a Levite, because only maids that were heirs were restrained from marrying out of their own tribe, that their inheritance might not go from it, but all other were left to the liberty of their own choice, Numb. 36:8.

**V. 2. ***{And his concubine played the whore against him}* Which she could not have done, if she had been only a harlot-concubine, and not his wife. But being so, she sinned against him; that is, against her faith and promise plighted and made unto him, she committed whoredom with others. For though such concubines as she were no wives equal to the lawful wives, yet were they bound to keep their faith with their husbands, unto whom they were contracted. See Gen. 12:24, 1 Kings 11:1.

*{and went away from him}* Either out of dislike, or fear of receiving punishment when her husband should discover her whoredom, or at least because there were continual jars and contentions between them about it.

*{unto her father’s house}* Who as a fond father received her, and so countenanced her in her sin.

*{and was there four whole months}* Or, *a year and four months*. Heb. *days four months*.

**V. 3. ***{And her husband arose, and went after her}* i.e. When she neglected to use means whereby she might be reconciled, his anger being appeased, and his affection inclining towards her, he goes to reconcile himself unto her, though she were the peccant party.

*{to speak friendly unto her}* Heb. *to her heart*. i.e. Words of love, peace and comfort, to allure and draw on her affection towards him. So Hos. 2:14. Thus Shechem spoke to Dinah, Gen. 34:3, and Joseph to his brethren, Gen. 50:11.

*{a couple of asses}* One to carry provisions for the journey, and the other to ease himself and his wife, if she would return with him.

*{he rejoiced to meet him}* Hoping he was come to be reconciled to his wife.

**V. 4. ***{and he abode with him three days}* i.e. Of his own voluntary accord, to rejoice together in their reconcilement. And two days more he abode unwillingly, being overborne with the importunity of his father-in-law.

*{So they did eat and drink}* i.e. They feasted together, to testify that they were reconciled, and that all differences were accorded between them, Exod. 32:6.

**V. 5. ***{when they arose early}* i.e. They of the family.

*{that he rose up}* i.e. The Levite also.

*{Comfort thy heart with a morsel of bread}* i.e. Refresh and strengthen yourself for your journey with a little food; or, as we say, break your fast before you set out. So Gen. 18:5, 1 Kings 13:7, 8. And with this kind entertainment the father detains him, that by their longer stay their renewed love might be the more increased and confirmed, like things glued together, which are strengthened by time and continuance.

**V. 8. ***{until afternoon}* Heb. *till the day declined*.

**V. 9. ***{the day draweth}* Heb. *is weak*.

*{the day groweth to an end}* Heb. *it is the pitching time of the day*. i.e. Toward evening, and therefore it is a time more fit to take up one’s lodging than to begin a journey.

*{home}* Heb. *to thy tent*.

**V. 10. ***{came over against Jebus, (which is Jerusalem)}* So called afterwards, but now Jebus, being a city of the Jebusites, Josh. 15:63 and 18:28. So that this story is of things done before the taking of Jerusalem, mentioned ch. 1:8, and the expelling of the Jebusites: if at least the people of Judah were not for their sins beaten out by the Jebusites after they first took it; which is not probable, there being not the least touch of any such thing in the Scriptures. Some conceive, though that part of Jerusalem which belonged to Judah were taken and possessed by them, ch. 1:8, yet the other part, which belonged to Benjamin, was still possessed by the Jebusites, seeing it is said ch. 1:21, the children of Benjamin did not drive out the Jebusites, but they dwelt together. But neither is this likely, that it was so at this time, seeing the Levite might have then gone to that part of Jerusalem which was in the possession of Judah, or to that part which belonged to the Benjamites, if it were nearer to him, and have lodged with some of them, they dwelling there together with the Jebusites, as well as have gone further, when it was so late, to lodge at Gibeah. Neither would he have called it Jebus, or the City of a stranger, if the better part of it were possessed by the children of Judah, and the rest in a great part by the children of Benjamin.

**V. 11. ***{the Jebusites}* Who were of the posterity of Canaan, Gen. 10:16.

**V. 12. ***{the City of a stranger, that is not of the children of Israel}* i.e. A cursed nation, and estranged from God and his people. This he would not have said, if the greatest part of it at this time had been in the possession of Judah and Benjamin.

**V. 13. ***{in Gibeah, or in Ramah}* These two cities were next Jebus northward, situate on hills in the way to mount Ephraim, whither the Levite traveled; Ramah was four miles from Jebus; Gibeah, somewhat nearer. Some think there were two cities called Gibeah in the tribe of Benjamin, one situate on a mountain, the other in a plain. See ch. 20:31. There was also another Gibeah in Judah, Josh. 15:57.

**V. 14. ***{Gibeah, which belongeth to Benjamin}* This is added to distinguish it from that of Judah. It is also called Gibeah of Saul, because Saul was born there, and was therefore often resident in that city.

**V. 15. ***{And they turned aside thither, to go in and lodge in Gibeah}* The Levite’s counsel and intention is to be commended for pious and honest, as desiring to lodge rather with God’s people than with strangers and heathens; and his servant, to be condemned, or at least suspected faulty, as savoring of sloth and desire of ease; and yet in the event the Levite’s counsel and course proved pernicious to himself, his wife, and many thousands of his people; God so disposing of it by an overruling providence. Which may teach us not to trust in our own wisdom, but in all our enterprises to crave God’s direction, Prov. 16:9 and 19:21 and 20:24, Jer. 10:23.

*{For there was no man that took them into his house}* i.e. Either because they would not, being wicked and inhumane; or because they dared not, seeing they knew the inhabitants were so abominably wicked that they would assault their houses to find out strangers whom they might abuse in exercising upon them their filthy lusts.

**V. 16. ***{there came an old man from his work out of the field}* Here are given two reasons why this old man entertained them rather than any other of the city; one, because he was an honest man, who lived by his painful labor, though he were old, whereas the rest of the city were idle and luxurious: the other, because he was also of mount Ephraim, and only sojourned in Gibeah; and therefore being a stranger too, and their countryman, he had more compassion of them in their distressed condition than the Benjamites, who were of another tribe.

**V. 18. ***{I am now going to the house of the Lord}* Which at this time was at Shiloh, ch. 18:31. Thither he intended to go first, that he might do service unto God, as being a Levite, and then afterwards to pass on to his own house.

*{and there is no man that receiveth me}* i.e. Who will be entreated to give me lodging upon any conditions.

**V. 19. ***{Yet there is both straw and provender—there is no want of any thing}* q.d. They have no reason to refuse us, seeing we all have things with us of our own needful for our journey, and would put them to no other charge or trouble but only to lodge us.

**V. 20. ***{Let all thy wants lie upon me}* i.e. Take no further care either for your lodging or provision, seeing I will take all the care upon myself to provide all necessaries both for you and all that belong unto you.

*{only lodge not in the street}* q.d. This only I desire, that you will not so much wrong yourself and your wife as to lie in the open street. And thus the good old man, out of his humanity and love to hospitality, makes it his suit to the Levite that he might entertain him, who before sued unto others to be entertained, and was rejected.

**V. 21. ***{they washed their feet}* Which was one of the rites of hospitality in those hot countries. See Gen. 18:4 and 19:2, 2 Sam. 11:8.

**V. 22. ***{certain sons of Belial}* i.e. Most flagitious men, and notoriously and desperately wicked, stigmatized villains, and even incarnate devils, men without yoke, as the word signifies, who will not submit to be governed by religion or reason, by the principles of nature, or by the laws of God or man; like unto the devil himself, who is also called by this name, 2 Cor. 6:15, in which respect they may be fitly called his children, yea, his eldest sons, Deut. 13:13. See 1 Sam. 1:16.

*{beset the house round about}* Like those, Gen. 19:4, who it may be were their precedents, even as these children of iniquity were exemplary to after ages, Hos. 9:9 and 10:9.

*{Bring forth the man—that we may know him}* A modest expression of their filthy lust, according to the Scripture phrase, Gen. 19:5 and 4:1.

**V. 23. ***{Nay, my brethren}* He seeks to take them off first by this meek and loving compellation, and then by telling them that their intended action was both wicked and foolish. So 2 Sam. 13:12, 13, Gen. 34:7, Prov. 7:7, 1 Sam. 20:12.

*{seeing that this man is come into my house}* To wit, that he may be safe under my protection according to the laws of hospitality. So Gen. 19:8.

**V. 24. ***{Behold, here is my daughter a maiden, and his concubine}* This good old man is upon the sudden so transported with his care to observe the laws of hospitality, that he sinfully offers to prostitute his own innocent daughter and the Levite’s wife to their wicked lust, to be abused by them. For though it be lawful in evils of punishment to choose the less that we may avoid the greater (for so respectively the lesser evil is good) yet in the evils of sin we must choose neither, no not the least to avoid the greatest, seeing the lightest and most venial sin is greater (if we be actors in it, and make it our own by choice) than the most abominable wickedness in which we are but mere patients and the bare subjects of other men’s villainies.

*{and humble ye them}* i.e. Abuse them at your pleasure, but abuse not my guest by making him the subject of so base a villainy and unnatural filthiness. It was very commendable in him, that he thus highly prized the laws of hospitality; but the means he used are to be condemned as sinful, seeing we may not do evil that good may come thereof, Rom. 3:8, nor act the least evil ourselves that we may prevent the greatest in others. Neither had this man power over his daughter and the concubine, to prostitute them to be abused by these wicked, graceless and shameless villains.

**V. 25. ***{So the man}* That is, the Levite, not daring himself to come out unto them for fear of being abused by them, required the old man to do it, so reverencing the laws of hospitality, that he thought it unreasonable his host’s daughter should be abused for his sake.

*{and they knew her}* The old man offered his daughter to be abused, as well as this concubine; but this object of their filthy and brutish lust being presented unto them, they suddenly catch at it, and so forget the other. This came to pass by a just and overruling providence of God, causing them to forget the innocent virgin, that she might be preserved, and suffering the concubine to be abused, for a deserved punishment of her former unchastity and unfaithfulness.

**V. 26. ***{and fell down}* i.e. Either dead or ready to die.

*{till it was light}* i.e. She came and fell down at the door in the dawning of the day; and there lay dead till it was clear light or fair day.

**V. 27. ***{And her lord rose up}* i.e. Her husband: so Sarah called Abraham for honor’s sake, 1 Pet. 3:6. But perhaps this woman had been his servant, bought with his money, if so, he was her lord in a double respect. See Exod. 21:8.

*{and opened the doors of the house}* To see, it is likely, what was become of his wife, that they might go on their journey, and hasten away from that wicked city.

*{and behold, the woman his concubine was fallen down}* Namely, stark dead at the door, the Lord having called her to account for her former wickedness, and by a just law of requital punished her in the selfsame kind as she had sinned, though the matter were taken up between her and her husband, and they reconciled one to the other, Heb. 13:4.

*{and her hands were upon the threshold}* In her fall catching hold at it, in the pangs of death.

**V. 28. ***{And he said unto her, Up, and let us be going}* i.e. Supposing she was come too late in the night to be let in, and there fallen asleep, he seeks to awaken her.

*{Then the man took her up}* i.e. The Levite discerning by her speechlessness that she was stark dead, as also by making other experiments upon her, being assured that it was so, he took her up to carry her away with him upon another design that came into his mind.

**V. 29. ***{and divided her, together with her bones, into twelve pieces, and sent her into all the coasts of Israel}* Because there was no other way to bring these wicked varlets to deserved punishment for their abominable wickedness, he appeals and addresses himself to the whole nation, and sends his wife about piecemeal, flesh and bones, unto every of the twelve tribes apart; the Levites, his own tribe, being dispersed among the rest, that by such an horrid spectacle he might incense their wrath, and raise up their spirits to take the sharpest revenge upon the actors of so foul a fact, the heart being usually more affected by visible spectacles presented to the eye, than by bare reports related unto the ear. This action of the Levite thus mangling the dead body of his wife was in itself barbarous, bloody and inhumane, seeing the bodies of the dead ought to have their due honor and respect, Deut. 21:22, yet it is excusable in him, seeing he acted it for an higher design; which did not so much dishonor the dead as benefit the living, by bringing those children of Belial to condign punishment, and not suffering those pests to poison the air with their breath, and by their indemnity to pull down God’s fearful judgments upon the whole land. And thus it is lawful for physicians and surgeons to anatomize a dead body, that by searching into the secrets of nature they may get more skill to prevent or cure the diseases of the living.

**V. 30. ***{And it was so, that all that saw it}* i.e. The elders and magistrates of every tribe, and with them other of the people, seeing these pieces of the dead body, and hearing the whole matter related by the messengers that brought them, and all the several passages of this lamentable tragedy, generally concluded there was never such an horrid villainy done or seen, either in their own times, or in the days of their ancestors, since their coming out of Egypt.

*{consider of it, take advice, and speak your minds}* i.e. What course is to be taken, and what means are fittest to be used to bring these desperate offenders to deserved punishment, lest we be guilty of their sin by not executing justice, and make the whole land liable to God’s wrath.

## CHAPTER 20

**Verse 1. ***{Then all the children of Israel}* i.e. A great part of the whole body of the people, out of every tribe which had heard of this abominable fact by the relation of the Levite’s messengers, the tribe of Benjamin excepted, went out of their houses and places of habitation, being summoned by their elders and officers. So Gen. 41:57, Mar. 1:5. Nor can this be understood of the whole nation, which far exceeded in number four hundred thousand men, as appears by their first numbering in the wilderness, Numb. 1:46, 47, and that in David’s time, 2 Sam. 24:9. Neither is it probable that going out they left their country and cities naked and destitute of inhabitants to defend them against bordering enemies, and to manage all their domestical affairs, seeing these that went out were not to return till this war was ended, v. 8. And much less probable it is, that this should be understood only of the elders and officers, seeing they could not possibly amount to so great a number as four hundred thousand men. Therefore it is meant of all their chief men of war, together with their elders, officers, captains and commanders, fit to be called out for such a service.

*{gathered together as one man}* i.e. With unanimous consent they went out upon this expedition, with such speed and readiness as if one man alone had undertaken the business. So v. 8, 11, 1 Sam. 11:7, 2 Sam. 19:14, Ezra 3:1.

*{from Dan even to Beer-sheba with the land of Gilead}* i.e. From all the utmost borders of the land. For it was bounded with Dan on the north, 1 Kings 4:25, Beer-sheba in the tribe of Judah towards the Philistines, Gen. 21:33, 34, on the south, Gilead beyond Jordan on the east, and the Mediterranean sea on the west.

*{unto the Lord in Mizpeh}* Not because the tabernacle was there, but because they came thither to act this weighty business as in God’s presence, and withal to worship, pray and ask of the Lord at Shiloh, which was near Mizpeh on the east. This Mizpeh was a place in the borders of Benjamin, Josh. 18:26, and therefore fit for this present occasion. And it was often used for general assemblies, as lying in the heart of the land, and so commodious for such meetings, 1 Sam. 7:5 and 10:17, 2 Kings 25:23. Some think here was some synagogue or place of public worship, 1 Macc. 3:46, though sacrifices and the ceremonial worship were limited to the tabernacle, which was now at Shiloh, ch. 18:31. See on ch. 10:17 and 11:19.

**V. 2. ***{And the chief of all the people}* Or, *the corners of all the people*. i.e. The chief princes gathered out of all the corners of the land, or the corners themselves, as the Hebrew has it, which called the chief princes corners, as some think, because, as cornerstones or pillars, they are the chief strength that bears up the fabric of the commonwealth. So 1 Sam. 14:38, Isai. 19:13, Zeph. 3:6.

*{four hundred thousand footmen}* For in the armies of the Israelites none fought on horseback, or in chariots, but on foot only.

*{that drew sword}* See ch. 8:10.

**V. 3. ***{Then said the children of Israel}* To wit, unto the Levite.

*{How was this wickedness?}* i.e. Tell us the manner how this horrible wickedness was acted, and all the circumstances of it.

**V. 4. ***{And the Levite}* Heb. *the man the Levite*.

*{the husband of the woman}* This shows the woman was not his harlot, but contracted unto him as his wife. See on ch. 19:1, 2.

**V. 5. ***{thought to have slain me}* i.e. Either by abusing me, as they did my wife (which for modesty’s sake, he does not express in plain terms), or they indeed resolved to kill me, if I would not yield to prostitute my body to their abominable lusts, which to do I should have thought much worse than death itself. And this his accusation and complaint it is likely he confirmed by producing his old host and servant as his witnesses, Deut. 17:6.

**V. 6. ***{they have committed lewdness and folly}* i.e. Such an horrible and worse than brutish wickedness as ought not to go unpunished. See ch. 19:23 and on Josh. 7:15.

**V. 7. ***{Behold, ye are all children of Israel}* i.e. Whom this horrible fact concerns, as well as me, seeing you are the people of God, who are straitly bound by his command to punish such outrageous wickedness, and so to put away evil from Israel; which if you do not, the guilt thereof will lie upon the land, v. 12.

**V. 8. ***{We will not any of us go to his tent}* So resolute they were in the fervency of their zeal to see this matchless villainy severely punished, that they resolve not to make any delay in seeing it done, if at least the Benjamites themselves would not see justice executed upon the offenders; and if they refused so to do, they would also make war against them as abettors of the villainy. And here it is probable that they vowed, and ratified their vow with an oath, that if any of the Benjamites should escape the sword and remain alive, they would not (after they had destroyed all their women and maids, together with their children) give any of their daughters in marriage unto them; and withal, that they would put to the sword the men of any town or city which had not assisted them in this war: both which are mentioned ch. 21:1, 5.

**V. 9. ***{We will go up by lot against it}* i.e. To prevent all controversies, differences and emulations, which might otherwise happen, the lot shall decide the matter, who shall make provision for the camp, and who shall go out to fight.

**V. 10. ***{And we will take ten men of an hundred}* That is, in the whole, forty thousand out of four hundred thousand.

*{to fetch victual for the people}* i.e. To victual the camp.

**V. 11. ***{knit together as one man}* Heb. *fellows*. i.e. Joining both heart and hand to assist one another in this war.

**V. 12. ***{And the tribes of Israel sent men through all the tribe of Benjamin}* i.e. So careful they were not to raise a civil war, and to shed blood, that they used all lawful means to prevent it. Therefore before they would further engage themselves, they send these messengers, as ambassadors of peace, if they would punish the offenders; or as heralds to proclaim war, if they refused. For that war is not just that is begun before it be proclaimed, and propositions of peace tendered to prevent it, if they be accepted. So Deut. 20:10, 11.

*{What wickedness is this that is done among you?}* They do not inquire what the sin was, for that they knew already, but expostulate with them concerning the heinousness of it, the which they aggravate by this interrogation, q.d. How horrible and abominable is this wickedness which has been committed among you?

**V. 13. ***{Now therefore deliver us the men}* i.e. Keep them not under your protection, but give them up to justice; that as they have been examples unto others to encourage them in such a matchless villainy, so others may be deterred from committing the like sin by their exemplary punishment.

*{the children of Belial}* See ch. 19:22.

*{and put away evil from Israel}* i.e. Both the evil of sin, cutting off the offenders by the sword of justice, and preserving others from being infected by their wicked example; and also the evil of punishment, seeing the guilt of their sin will lie upon the land, and draw down upon it God’s heavy judgments, if justice be not executed.

*{But the children of Benjamin would not hearken to the voice of their brethren}* i.e. Though as brethren they persuaded them out of love to do that which tended as well to their own good as the public welfare of the whole state, yet they would give no ear to their advice either out of pride, because they scorned that the other tribes should meddle in their government, or out of self-confidence presuming on their own strength and skillful prowess in martial affairs; or God gave them up to the hardness of their own hearts, because he was purposed to destroy them, 1 Sam. 2:25. See on Gen. 49:27.

**V. 14. ***{But the children of Benjamin gathered themselves together}* i.e. Being resolved to stand it out, they make a general muster of their whole tribe at Gibeah, that they might be ready to protect them, if any attempt should be made against them by the other tribes; showing herein their heat and eagerness to fight in their defense, though they knew them to be notoriously wicked. And so they made themselves accessories, abettors, and guilty of their horrible sin.

**V. 15. ***{that drew sword}* i.e. Fit and able men to do service in the war. See ch. 8:10.

**V. 16. ***{left-handed}* See ch. 3:15. Some wittily observe here, that as these many left-handed persons descended from Benjamin, whose name signifies son of the right hand, so it is too often seen that virtuous and religious parents have an ungracious offspring.

*{every one could sling stones at an hair’s breadth, and not miss}* An hyperbolical expression, whereby is signified that they were so exceedingly skillful in slinging stones that they would commonly hit a small mark.

**V. 18. ***{went up to the house of God, and asked counsel of God}* Before they would undertake such a weighty business they consulted with God, and do not rashly rush upon it, but first desire to be directed by him. See chap. 1:1.

*{Which of us shall go up first to the battle?}* They do not inquire whether they should fight or no; but being full of confidence in respect of the justness of their cause, and also their great number and strength, they strive for precedency and the honor of the day, presuming already of victory before the fight, and so seek to God only for the determining of this difference between them, that there might be no contentions and emulations about it.

**V. 21. ***{And destroyed down to the ground of the Israelites that day twenty and two thousand men}* Though they fought in a just cause and quarrel against wicked and graceless men, who stood up in defense of those who had deserved utter ruin and destruction for their abominable wickedness, yet God executed this heavy judgment upon them, I. To punish and correct the pride and self-confidence of his people, which appeared in that they sought unto God only to know who should have the precedency in the war, v. 18, but did not by humble and hearty prayer crave his assistance and blessing upon their endeavors. II. Because they were generally guilty of many great and heinous sins, especially of idolatry and backsliding from God, of which they had not repented, nor made their peace with God by serious humiliation and turning from their wicked ways. III. Because if they had at first prevailed and obtained victory, they would have gloried and triumphed in their own policy and strength, and not ascribed the glory to God. IV. To teach them and all others not to judge of the justice of the cause by the event, seeing outward things happen to all alike, Eccl. 9:1, 2, the wicked oftentimes thriving and prospering in their sin, and the godly and righteous being oppressed by them, though their cause be much the better; as we see in the example of God’s people and the Assyrian and Babylonish kings, and at this day of the Turks and Christians. In all which God is just: for though his children suffer as innocents in respect of their enemies, yet not in respect of himself, who always inflicts upon them deserved punishments for their sins, or at least fatherly chastisements to bring them to repentance, or to prevent sin when they are ready to fall into it: and on the other side he suffers the wicked to prosper and flourish in their sin for a time, that he may bring them to the greater destruction in the end, as the Benjamites in this place.

**V. 22. ***{the men of Israel encouraged themselves}* i.e. Attributing their defeat to some outward accidents, which now by experience they had learned to prevent, they take new heart unto them, hoping for good success in respect of their great strength and number that still remained.

*{in the place where they put themselves in array the first day}* This is noted (as it seems) to imply, that it was a place of disadvantage, in respect of the multitude of the Israelitish army, and the small number of the Benjamites.

**V. 23. ***{and the children of Israel went up and wept before the Lord}* Here is to be observed, that their great defeat wrought in them some reformation of their former errors and omissions, but not such an one as was sufficient to fit them for a victory. For they wept before the Lord, but not so much (as it seems) for their sin as for their smart and loss, nor humbled themselves by fasting and prayer. And again, they asked counsel of the Lord whether they should the second time go to battle against the children of Benjamin their brother, but inquired not what should be the success of their enterprise, nor acknowledged that they did rely upon him alone for victory, having no conceit of their own strength, nor craved by hearty prayer his assistance, without which there was no hope of prevailing against their enemies.

*{against Benjamin my brother}* Doubting it is likely of their success, because they raised war against their brother, that is, a tribe of their own people, and not looking to their sin, the chief cause of their defeat.

*{And the Lord said, Go up against him}* i.e. He answered them according to their inquiry, but gave them no encouragement by promising victory.

**V. 25. ***{All these drew the sword}* i.e. Were valiant men and skillful, and able to manage their weapons. So v. 15.

**V. 26. ***{and came unto the house of God, and wept}* Not as they did before, but in a solemn and serious manner, not so much bewailing their loss, as their sins, which were the cause of it.

*{and sat there before the Lord}* i.e. According to the posture of mourners, Lam. 1:1, Psa. 137:1. Or, as others take it, they abode there humbling themselves before God with true contrition and compunction of heart, from morning to night, in the true sight and sense of their sins, 2 Sam. 7:18.

*{and fasted}* i.e. Did not perform the duty of humiliation and repentance slightly, overly and formally, as before, but in a thorough and serious manner.

*{and offered burnt offerings}* To wit, for the expiation of their sins. In these the whole sacrifice was consumed and offered unto God.

*{and peace offerings before the Lord}* In peace offerings part was offered to the Lord, part belonged to the priests, and part to him that offered the sacrifice. And this they did to make their peace and atonement, and to testify their faith and assurance of victory, having sought God in a right manner.

**V. 27. ***{enquired of the Lord}* To wit, by the high priest, after the judgment of Urim and Thummim, Numb. 27:21.

*{for the ark of the covenant of God was there in those days}* i.e. It was in the tabernacle at Shiloh, which was near unto Gibeah.

**V. 28. ***{And Phinehas the son of Eleazar—stood before it in those days}* i.e. Ministered before the Lord in the priest’s office, the high priest. So Deut. 10:8 and 18:5. Hence it is evident that these things here related were not done after the death of Samson, but soon after Joshua’s time. For if Phinehas had continued till after Samson’s death, supposing that he were thirty years old when he killed Zimri and Cozbi, he should have been now about three hundred and fifty years of age. See on ch. 17:1.

*{Go up, for to morrow I will deliver them into thy hand}* Before God bade them go up, but gave them no promise of good success, but now they have his word for it, and are thereby assured of victory.

**V. 29. ***{And Israel set liers in wait round about Gibeah}* So Josh. 8:4. They set a secret ambush near the city, to take it when the people were drawn out to battle and having surprised it, and set some part of it on fire, then to issue out and fall upon the rear of the Benjamites’ army, so that it appears by this relation (though it be intricate and much perplexed) that the Israelites divided their army into three parts: one was this here laid in ambush: the second was a party drawn out of the army to present themselves unto the Benjamites, and for a while to entertain a fight with them, and by and by to counterfeit a flight, till they saw the city taken by the ascending of the flame and smoke, and then to face about and make a stand: the third was to be at Baal-tamar, as a strong reserve, consisting of the main battle or body of the army who were appointed to second them who were to feign a flight, when they made a stand, and had drawn the enemy far enough out of the city, and brought them within the reach of this reserve. In the two former battles, when they relied upon their own strength and number, and had from God no promise of victory, they were so bold and confident that they neglected stratagems of war, as thinking there was no need of them. But now that they had a promise of victory which could not fail, they think no providence or policy too much. Which plainly shows what a wide difference there is between vain presumption grounded on our own strength and natural abilities, and true faith relying upon God’s word and promises. Presumption is bold and confident upon weak and false grounds, and thinks surely to attain its ends, though all means be neglected that may advance and further them; but true faith, when it has God’s word to build upon, is no less careful to use all lawful means than if there were no promise, because it knows that ordinarily God appoints that the means and end should go together, and therefore it is a tempting of God, when we have a promise, to neglect the means subservient to his providence. See Acts 27:31 and on Isa. 38:21.

**V. 30. ***{And the children of Israel went up}* To wit, that part of the army which were appointed to fight with the Benjamites, and to counterfeit a flight.

**V. 31. ***{went out against the people}* i.e. Against that party which was drawn out to fight against them.

*{in the highways}* Or, *in the crossways*, leading to divers places.

*{the house of God}* Or, *Bethel*, i.e. either Shiloh, where the tabernacle was placed; or the city Bethel, of which see on Hos. 10:15.

*{and the other to Gibeah in the field}* So called, as it seems, to distinguish it from the other Gibeah on the hill, against which the Israelites now fought, v. 23, 30. See ch. 19:13, Josh. 18:24, 28. Both these cities lay near together in the tribe of Benjamin.

**V. 32. ***{unto the highways}* i.e. Where their great reserve, consisting of the main body of the army, encamped.

**V. 33. ***{And all the men of Israel}* i.e. The main body of their army, which lay encamped near the highways, rose up and put themselves in array, to second those who had feigned a flight, and now made a stand.

*{at Baal-Tamar}* Lying on the west of Gibeah, where they had placed themselves to receive the Benjamites when they pursued the Israelites in their flight.
