*{and the liers in wait}* i.e. Those who lay in ambush in the meadows near the city to surprise it, when the Benjamites were drawn out to battle.

**V. 34. ***{And there came against Gibeah ten thousand chosen men}* i.e. The liers in wait that lay in ambush to surprise the city. Which when they had done, they put themselves between the city and the Benjamites’ army, to hinder their retreat.

*{and the battle was sore}* Namely, to the Benjamites, who were hemmed in on all sides, before and behind, though for the present they apprehended not their danger till they saw the flame ascend out of the city.

**V. 35. ***{destroyed of the Benjamites that day twenty and five thousand and an hundred men}* The particulars are afterwards set down thus: eighteen thousand slain in the battle, v. 44; five thousand gleaned in the highways after the army was slaughtered, routed, and put to flight, and two thousand pursued to Gidom, and slain there, v. 45. And over and above these there were some odd numbers slain in the field and in the pursuit which make up the odd hundred in this verse. But besides these five and twenty thousand and one hundred mentioned here, and the six hundred that fled to the rock Rimmon, v. 47, there lacks one thousand to complete the whole number of the Benjamites’ army, v. 15. Now what became of these is not expressed, but it is probably conceived they fell in the two former battles.

**V. 36. ***{for the men of Israel}* Having in general related the Benjamites’ defeat and the Israelites’ victory, he does now describe the manner and circumstances of it.

*{gave place}* i.e. Feignedly fled.

**V. 37. ***{drew themselves along}* Or, *made a long sound with the trumpets*. To wit, out of the places where they had lain in ambush.

**V. 38. ***{there was an appointed sign}* Namely, between the liers in wait and that part of the army which were in the fight, that they should make a stand and turn upon the enemy when they saw the flame ascending out of the city.

**V. 40. ***{the Benjamites looked behind them}* Being amazed to see the fleeing Israelites upon a sudden make a stand and renew their fight, they stood at a gaze, and so looking back they perceived that their city was taken and set on fire, and so that evil was come upon them, being on all sides environed by their enemies, and no place left for a safe retreat, and therefore that they were all utterly lost without any hope of escaping.

**V. 42. ***{The way of the wilderness}* i.e. That leads to the wilderness.

*{the battle overtook them}* i.e. The main battle, or the chief strength of the army which for this purpose had placed themselves at Baal-tamar, v. 33.

*{and them which came out of the Cities}* This place is diversely interpreted. Some will have the meaning to be, that the party which had fired Gibeah came now out and charged the rear of the Benjamites; others, that beside the army of the Israelites, the people of the cities all about came forth and slaughtered the Benjamites, as they fled; others, that besides the slaughter of the Benjamites’ army, the Israelites destroyed even those also which had not taken up arms, but abode quiet in their cities.

*{they destroyed in the midst of them}* i.e. Having enclosed them round about on every side, they slew them.

**V. 43. ***{with ease}* Or, *from Menuchah*.

**V. 44. ***{And there fell}* To wit, in the battle.

**V. 45. ***{unto the rock Rimmon}* i.e. A city lying upon a rock between Bethel and Gibeah, on the southwest of Benjamin.

*{and they gleaned}* i.e. They slew here and there, as they could meet with them, cutting them clean off, as ears of corn are gleaned after the harvest, or grapes after the vintage.

*{and pursued hard after them}* As it were close at the heels, not giving them any scope, rest or respite.

**V. 46. ***{So that all that fell that day of Benjamin were twenty and five thousand men}* He expresses only the grand and round number, leaving out the odd hundred mentioned, v. 35. See on ch. 11:26, 2 Sam. 5:5.

**V. 48. ***{and the men of Israel turned again}* i.e. Not satisfied with the slaughter of the men of Gibeah and the other Benjamites that came to fight in their defense, they fell upon all other cities of that tribe, because they likewise had sent aids to this war, and slew men, woman and child, and the very beasts. The which they did, as some think, because for their protecting those that had committed this abominable crime, they had anathematized and devoted them to utter destruction; which was the case of Jericho, and Hazor, and of such as should set up idolatry in the land, Deut. 13:15, 16. But this cannot probably be thought to be the cause, seeing if all had been devoted to a total ruin, they neither would nor could upon second thoughts have spared the six hundred at Rimmon, nor afterwards have repented of the desolation they had made. And therefore it is rather to be thought, that what they did was out of the heat and fury of war, unto which they were incensed and enraged not only by the Benjamites’ horrible wickedness in patronizing and protecting such a matchless villainy, but because they had suffered in so just a cause a great slaughter of forty thousand of their men.

## CHAPTER 21

**Verse 1. ***{Now the men of Israel had sworn in Mizpeh}* When they entered into a solemn covenant there, that they would make war against the Benjamites, if they would not let justice proceed against the men of Gibeah, ch. 20:1, 8.

*{There shall not any of us give his daughter unto Benjamin to wife}* Which oath first implies a supposal, that some of the Benjamites might outlive the war, escaping by flight or otherwise; and withal, that it might so come to pass, that there would not so many of the female sex escape the sword as might be a number sufficient to be wives unto the men that should remain. For if they had women enough of their own tribe surviving, they should not need to have wives from other tribes. Secondly, it expresses a full determination, that if those Benjamites that survived were brought to extremity, as to want wives, and no women left of their own tribe to marry withal, that none of the other tribes should give their daughters unto them, nor hold communion with them, but account them as Gentiles and heathens. It is very likely that when out of sudden passion and displeasure they made this oath, they thought not of the mischiefs and inconveniences that would necessarily ensue upon their observing of it; as, that if the Benjamites had neither wives of their own, nor might fetch any from the other tribes, then either they must live single, and not marry at all, and so, contrary to God’s revealed will and Jacob’s prophetical blessing, one of the twelve tribes be quite extinct at their death; or else, contrary to God’s express command, they must of necessity match themselves with heathens and strangers, and so pollute the holy seed and beget a mongrel generation. But as the oath itself was rash and inconsiderate, so the performance and execution hereof was most barbarous and bloody, seeing in their heat and fury they sought to destroy the whole tribe, not only the men that came out in battle against them, but those also that afterwards they found in their cities, among which it may well be presumed there were many that were not accessory to the foul fact of the men of Gibeah; and not them alone, but even the women, virgins, and little children, who were altogether innocent. Neither did any escape their bloody rage, except the six hundred men which fled unto the rock Rimmon, and by God’s special providence were there preserved against their wills. All this outrage was disguised under the color of a religious oath, which they had taken out of blind zeal and superstitious ignorance.

**V. 2. ***{and the people came to the house of God}* Being now in cold blood and having learned in what straits they were, and into what a labyrinth they had brought themselves, being necessitated either to violate their solemn oath or dismember their body politic by cutting off one of their tribes, they assemble themselves at Shiloh, where the tabernacle now was, and in it the ark the visible sign of God’s presence, there to advise one with another, but principally to ask counsel of God, how they might wind themselves out of those difficulties in which they were involved by their own rashness and rage, whereof they now seriously repented, and how they might find out some way thereby to preserve the Benjamites from utter extirpation, and themselves from perjury. Now all this difficulty arose from their gross ignorance in those times. For they had no other cause to perplex themselves about their oath, but to stop the outcry of a superstitious conscience, seeing their oath being wicked, they were not bound at all to keep it, but having unlawfully made it, they might, when they knew it, lawfully break it, or else they should make themselves guilty of a double sin, and the latter greater than the former; the one, rashly to take such an oath; the other, advisedly to keep it, when it appeared to be sinful. But when men swerve from the rule of God’s word, they run out of one sin into another, and using shifts and shuffles to extricate themselves, they sink into the whirlpool of wickedness deeper and deeper. Yet in these dark and superstitious times they had so much light remaining, as to see that it was unlawful for children to marry without the consent of their parents; seeing otherwise this scruple of theirs might easily have been removed: for though the fathers could not give their consent by reason of their oath, yet their daughters, if they so pleased, might have married without it; and it is probable that many of them would have done it, because hereby they might have made themselves mistresses of very large estates, the whole inheritance of Benjamin being to be divided amongst that small remnant of six hundred persons.

**V. 3. ***{why is this come to pass in Israel}* This is not propounded as a question to be resolved, but as an expostulation or complaint of their present condition, which was to be pitied, like that Isa. 63:17, *O Lord, why hast thou made us to err from thy ways, and hardened our heart from thy fear?* q.d. How comes it to pass, O Lord, that you have thus given us up to be so transported by rage and passion, as to do things which tend so much to your dishonor and our own discomfort, namely the cutting off of one of our tribes, and the causeless shedding of so much innocent blood?

**V. 4. ***{and built there an altar}* Some conceive this altar was erected for a memorial to testify their thankfulness for their victory, because it was unlawful to have any more altars than one only for sacrifices. But to erect an altar for a monument of their victory was not suitable to the present occasion, they now coming as mourners to lament the evil fruits that came of it, namely the destruction of one of their tribes. And besides, this conceit is plainly confuted in the very next words, which express the use for which this altar was erected, namely, to offer upon it burnt offerings and peace offerings. And therefore the better to clear this point, we are to know, that God appointed but one altar to be ordinarily used for sacrifices, as there was also to be but one tabernacle, and one temple, as types of our only Savior Jesus Christ, Heb. 13:10, 11. Therefore the ten tribes were highly offended at the two tribes and half when they thought they had built another altar for ordinary use, Josh. 22:11. But upon extraordinary and necessary occasions God allowed his people to build more than one altar in number, though they were all one in kind, both in respect of matter and form, especially when it was done by holy men, and of prophetical spirits, and without danger of idolatry; yet with this difference, that the one was to be fixed and permanent, the other to stand only so long as the necessary occasion continued. And thus we read of divers altars erected; as by Joshua, upon mount Ebal, Josh. 8:30, according to God’s special command, Deut. 27. By Gideon, ch. 6:24, 26, by Samuel, 1 Sam. 7:9, 17 and 11:14 and 16:2, 5, by David, 2 Sam. 24:25, and that by God’s appointment, v. 18, by Solomon, 1 Kings 8:64, by Elijah, 1 Kings 18:32. Now the occasion of setting up this altar, when they were so near the other in the tabernacle, was the same with that of Solomon’s, 1 Kings 8:64, namely, because there were so many sacrifices to be offered by that great assembly that one altar was not sufficient for them. See on ch. 13:16.

**V. 5. ***{For they had made a great oath}* Both in respect of the great multitude by whom it was taken, and the great solemnity observed in the taking of it. It may be also because there was some such great and bitter execration added to ratify and confirm it, as was added to the other oath of not giving their daughters to the Benjamites, v. 18.

*{concerning him that came not up}* This cannot be understood of every particular person; for if all had come the whole land should have been left empty and open to all incursions of bordering enemies; but of every city and town which did not send some to assist in this expedition.

*{to the Lord to Mizpeh}* See ch. 20:1.

*{He shall surely be put to death}* This oath was in itself lawful, and the punishment just which was threatened. For it was a lawful war proclaimed for inflicting deserved punishment upon grievous offenders. And seeing at this time there was no supreme magistrate to punish sin, the government was in the body of the people; and they might justly, having the power in their hands, make ordinances, and punish transgressors; and particularly all those, who in this common cause concerning the public good had not given their assistance when it was required, and so, as much as in them lay, did betray the commonwealth, and by their neutrality showed that they favored the cause of the offenders, seeing they would not help to bring them to condign punishment. Besides, they by their example taught others to contemn public edicts, and so did weaken the authority of the commonwealth, and introduce a pernicious anarchy.

**V. 6. ***{For Benjamin their brother}* All the Israelites were brethren, being all in covenant with God their heavenly Father, all descended from Israel their natural father, and all called after his name.

*{There is one tribe cut off}* i.e. In danger to be cut off, if some present course be not taken to preserve it. Others read, *Should we this day cut off a tribe of Israel?*

**V. 7. ***{wives for them that remain}* That is, the six hundred, ch. 20:47, that so by them the tribe may be preserved from perishing.

**V. 8. ***{from Jabesh-gilead}* A city beyond Jordan, in the tribe of Manasseh, near unto the lake Gennesareth. It was afterwards besieged by the Ammonites, and rescued by Saul, 1 Sam. 11:1, 2.

**V. 10. ***{with the women and children}* They had a just cause, but spoiled it in the handling, doing execution in a most cruel and unjust manner upon the women and innocent children. For they were not summoned to this service, nor was it so much as expected from them that they should come to the assembly, having no abilities to assist in the cause; and yet these innocents must fare no better than the offenders. Neither was this outrage committed in the heat of war, but when they found no resistance, and after they had lately offended in the same kind against the Benjamites, yea, and that in the very time when they saw their sin, and mourned for it. Neither will this excuse their bloody fact, that the Midianitish women were slain by Moses’ command as well as the men, seeing they were no less guilty than these of that sin, which was the cause of the war, Num. 31:16. Neither were they, as execrable, devoted to destruction, like Jericho and the inhabitants of it; for then they could not lawfully have reserved the four hundred virgins to be wives for the Benjamites.

**V. 11. ***{Ye shall utterly destroy every male}* Lest, as some conceive, when they came to age, they should revenge the death of their parents; for which methinks there was but little cause of fear, seeing it was not likely that the children of one city should take revenge of the whole nation, whose common cause this was. Therefore, for aught I know, this may go in among the other acts of their injustice and cruelty: Ezek. 18:20. Neither will it excuse their fact, that Moses commanded the like execution to be done upon the male children of the Midianites, seeing they were professed enemies to God’s people, and therefore he thought it just and fit to abate and weaken their strength by cutting off their posterity, Numb. 31:17.

*{and every woman that hath lien by man}* Heb. *knoweth the lying by man*. Reserving only virgins for the Benjamites.

**V. 12. ***{four hundred young virgins}* Heb. *young women virgins*. Which were marriageable.

*{brought them unto the camp}* i.e. Having killed all the other women that had lain by man, and (it is likely) with them many other virgins, which were in that city besides these four hundred.

*{to Shiloh, which is in the land of Canaan}* For Jabesh-gilead, lying beyond Jordan, properly was not in the land of Canaan, which did lie within it.

**V. 14. ***{And they gave them wives—of the women of Jabesh-gilead}* Supposing that therein they violated not their oath, because they gave them not their own daughters.

*{and yet so they sufficed them not}* Heb. *found they nothing for them*. i.e. They were not enough to fit the number of the Benjamites, and so to satisfy their desire. The like phrase is used Numb. 11:23, Josh. 17:16.

**V. 15. ***{And the people repented them for Benjamin}* Both because they had in the heat of war slain their brethren with so great a slaughter; and also more especially, because they had killed the women, and knew not how to furnish the Benjamites that remained with wives, in regard of the oath wherewith they had bound themselves, v. 1, 18.

*{because that the Lord had made a breach}* i.e. Because he had so ordered it by his providence, that for the just punishment of their sin such a fearful destruction should be brought upon them by the hands of the other tribes, as the executioners of his righteous judgments, so that the body of their state had received a dangerous maim by having one of its members very near cut off from it.

**V. 16. ***{for wives for them that remain}* i.e. The two hundred Benjamites which yet have not wives provided for them.

**V. 17. ***{There must be an inheritance for them that be escaped of Benjamin}* i.e. Benjamin must be preserved to have the twelfth portion in the inheritance of Jacob, in the time of Joshua allotted unto them, seeing it is unlawful to alienate any part thereof to another tribe. And therefore it being so large, and they so few in number, there is the more need that all of them have wives to multiply their tribe, that they may people their inheritance the sooner.

**V. 18. ***{Howbeit we may not give them wives of our daughters: for the children of Israel have sworn}* It was a rash and unlawful oath, and could not bind to that which was evil; only it was a bond to a blind and superstitious conscience. For they must either give them wives, or they must marry strangers, which was expressly contrary to God’s law, unless they became proselytes, or else the tribe must necessarily be extirpated for want of posterity.

*{saying, Cursed be he}* i.e. They backed their oath with a bitter imprecation or execration, wishing some great mischief to befall him that did break it.

*{that giveth a wife to Benjamin}* i.e. At this time, or to these men. For they intended not their oath should be perpetual and oblige posterity, but themselves only.

**V. 19. ***{Behold, there is a feast of the Lord in Shiloh}* What this feast was expositors diversely conjecture. Some think it was a civil or town feast kept yearly at Shiloh, and the rather, because they used, as a part of the solemnity of it, dancing, piping, or whistling, as the word may indifferently signify, 1 Kings 1:40, Psa. 87:7. Others very probably think that it was a religious feast, because it is called a feast of the Lord, and was also kept at Shiloh, where the tabernacle now  was placed; and that it was most likely to be the feast of tabernacles. And they conceive, it could not be the feast of Passover, because that was in the beginning of the spring, before their vines bore leaves, or yielded any convenience for these liers in wait to hide themselves behind them in the vineyards. But why might not vines be leaved at that time, as well as corn be ripe? See on Josh. 5:11, Lev. 23:10, 15, Deut. 16:9, Ruth 2:22, 1 Sam. 6:13. Whatsoever feast of the Lord it was, they suppose that it was much abused and profaned, seeing they spent the time of solemnizing it in dancing and reveling, which should rather have been employed in hearing the word, prayer, offering of sacrifices, and such like religious exercises. But seeing charity binds us to take things which are doubtful in the best sense, and seeing dancing and music were in the times under the law used as a part of their festival solemnities, to express their joy in praising of God for his special favors, as we see in the examples of Miriam, Deborah, Barak, and David, I see no reason why we should not judge this dancing and music here mentioned to have been of this nature, a religious act, and religiously performed in due time and place, and so not hindering God’s public service in the sanctuary. And to this I rather incline, because it is noted that this dancing was sober and modest, acted only by the virgins among themselves, and not, like the mixed dancing of our days, between men and women, which in all ages of the church has been censured and condemned as wanton and lascivious. See ch. 11:34, Exod. 15:20, 1 Sam. 18:26 and 2 Sam. 6:14.

**V. 21. ***{and catch you every man his wife}* They were not allowed to take any more than every man his wife. Which shows that though polygamy was practiced by some, yet it was not publicly approved in those times: and yet there was then more than ordinary cause for the allowing of it to these Benjamites, to increase and multiply their tribe now reduced to such a small number, to build up their cities now ruined, and to replenish their inheritances now almost lying waste and destitute of inhabitants. Now whereas the elders in a common council do not allow this rape at the Benjamites’ suit, but plot and contrive it for them, this shows, if at least they had not some special dispensation from God, in respect of the present necessity, to give this counsel and direction, the great ignorance and superstition of those times, in that they dispensed rather with the violent rape of wives without consent either of parents or parties, than they would give them their daughters, and thereby violate a rash and unlawful oath: though indeed they did not hereby make themselves less guilty and faulty, but added to their perjury the sin of rape, fraud and deceit, seeing they might much better have given their daughters, acknowledging their sin in making a rash oath, and repenting of it, than under the privilege of their power and authority have appointed them to take them themselves. But all this they did to silence the cry of a superstitious conscience, which usually is sooner satisfied by idle conceits of men’s own brains than by the evidence of truth and right reason.

**V. 22. ***{Be favourable unto them}* Or, *gratify us in them*.

*{for our sakes}* i.e. Out of the love that you bear unto us your brethren, that you may free us from that faultiness and guilt which otherwise will lie upon us, seeing we have brought this necessity upon them, by destroying their women, wives, and virgins, and not reserving a sufficient number for them in the war against Jabesh-gilead.

*{For ye did not give unto them at this time}* i.e. You did not voluntarily give your daughters unto them, but they by force have taken them away without your knowledge or consent, yea against your wills; and therefore you are free from the breach of your oath: and so indeed they were. But they were not innocent who used this plea, seeing they had contrived and appointed this rape. But this they conceal, which would but have made them odious to the parents of the virgins, and so less able to satisfy and appease them.

**V. 24. ***{And the children of Israel departed thence at that time, every man to his tribe, &c.}* i.e. They went in troops to the tribe of which they were; and being come thither they parted company and went severally to their families from which they were descended, and then particularly every man to his own house.

**V. 25. ***{In those days there was no King in Israel}* These words have already been often inserted in these histories, ch. 17:6 and 18:1 and 19:1, and are here placed for the close of all, to show how great a blessing kings and governors are unto a nation, seeing under an anarchy nothing springs and thrives but idolatry and superstition and all outrageous lusts and villainies.
